Ever since this time you hear that mountain wherein lived the gods with their grandmother called Thunder Mountain; and often, indeed, to this day, the lightning flashes and the thunder plays over its brows and the rain falls there most frequently.

===Chinese===

咱们这可闯了大乱子！祖母！祖母！”他们喊道。“开门让我们进去呀！”可老祖母已经唱完了她最后一口气，除了流水声，再无一点声息。他们在屋顶上坐下，等水退低些。然后他们爬下去，砸开房门，水哗地涌出来；随水一同冲出来的，还有他们可怜的老祖母——她双眼圆睁，头发湿成一绺一绺、沾满泥污，手指和腿僵得像雪松棍子。
“哦，神啊！神啊！”两个小伙子喊道；“我们把自己的祖母害死了——可怜的老祖母，她骂我们骂得那样狠，爱我们爱得那样深！等水流走了，咱们就把她埋在这门前面吧。”
于是等地一干，他们就把她埋在了那里。不到四天，那地方长出一株奇异的植物，小小的枝条上、亮绿的叶子间，垂着长长的尖角果实，红得像红雀胸前的火色。
“这就好了，”一天，两个小伙子站在那株植物跟前说。“咱们把种子撒出去，让人们拾来种上吧。看来我们把老祖母害死于我们的玩闹，并非全无缘由：从她的心里长出了一株植物，那果子里流淌的，仿佛正是她那条骂人舌头的火气和颜色；我们虽然失去了祖母——我们很爱她，她更爱我们——世人却得了一样新吃食，它虽辣人，却叫人欢喜，胜过她的话语之火曾叫我们欢喜。可怜的老祖母！世人吃辣椒的时候，做梦也想不到，辣椒的种子最初是从阿海尤塔与马赛莱玛的祖母那颗火辣辣的心里生出来的。”
于是两人摘下果实，在掌间捻碎，那股冲鼻的气味让他们欢喜得叫出声来。他们把种子四处撒开，种子这里那里都生了根；长出来的植株被人们发现，视作好东西，种了起来——直到今天，祖尼（Zuni）的辣椒园里还种着呢。
从那时起，你就听说那座住着两位神明和他们祖母的山叫作雷霆山；而且的确，直到今天，闪电还常常在那山的眉脊上闪掣，雷还常常在那上面轰鸣，雨也数那里落得最勤。

===Original===

It is said by some that the two boys, when asked how they stole the lightning-shaft and the thunder-stone, told on their poor old grandfather, the Centipede. The beloved Gods of the Rain gave him the lightning-shaft to handle in another way, and it so burned and shrivelled him that he became small, as you can see by looking at any of his numerous descendants, who are not only small but appear like a well-toasted bit of buckskin, fringed at the edges.
So shortens my story.

===Chinese===

据说，有人问起那两个小子怎样偷到闪电矢和雷石时，他们把可怜的老祖父蜈蚣给招了出来。受爱戴的雨神们便让闪电矢换了一种方式落到他身上，把他烧得又缩又焦，从此变得小小的——你只消看看他众多的子孙后裔就知道：不但个头小，模样还像一块烤得恰到好处的鹿皮，四边毛茸茸地翘着。
我的故事就此收短。

## How the Corn-Pests Were Ensnared / 玉米害兽是怎样被诱陷的
===Original===

You may know the country that lies south of the valley in which our town stands. You travel along the trail which winds round the hill our ancients called *Ishana-takʻyapon*﻿—which means the Hill of Grease, for the rocks sometimes shine in the light of the sun at evening, and it is said that strange things occurred there in the days of the ancients, which makes them thus to shine, while rocks of the kind in other places do not﻿—you travel on up this trail, crossing over the arroyos and foothills of the great mesa called Middle Mountain, until you come to the foot of the cliffs. Then you climb up back and forth, winding round and round, until you reach the top of the mountain, which is as flat as the floor of a house, merely being here and there traversed by small valleys covered with piñon and cedar, and threaded by trails made not only by the feet of our people but by deer and other animals. And so you go on and on, until, hardly knowing it, you have descended from the top of Middle Mountain, and found yourself in a wide plain covered with grass, and here and there clumps of trees. Beyond this valley is an elevated sandy plain, rather sunken in the middle, so that when it rains the water filters down into the soil of the depressed portion (which is wide enough to be a country in itself) and nourishes the grasses there; so that most of the year they grow green and sweet.
Now, a long, long time ago, in this valley or basin there lived a village of Prairie-dogs, on fairly peaceable terms with Rattlesnakes, Adders, Chameleons, Horned-toads, and Burrowing-owls. With the Owls they were especially friendly, looking at them as creatures of great gravity and sanctity. For this reason these Prairie-dogs and their companions never disturbed the councils or ceremonies of the Burrowing-owls, but treated them most respectfully, keeping at a distance from them when their dances were going on.
It chanced one day that the Burrowing-owls were having a great dance all to themselves, rather early in the morning. The dance they were engaged in was one peculiarly prized by them, requiring no little dexterity in its execution.

===Chinese===

你们也许知道我们镇所在的这条山谷以南的地方。你沿着小径前行，小径绕着我们先民称作伊沙纳·塔基亚庞（Ishana-takʻyapon）的山丘蜿蜒——这名字意思是“油脂之丘”，因为傍晚阳光之下，那里的岩石有时会闪闪发亮；据说先民时代有怪异之事发生在那儿，才使它们如此发光，而别处同类岩石却不然——你沿这条小径继续上走，跨过一道道旱沟和山麓丘陵，全在名叫“中山”的大台地边缘上，直到来到崖脚。然后你来回攀爬，盘旋复盘旋，直到登上山顶——那山顶平得像屋里的地面，只是纵横错落着一条条小谷，谷里长满矮松和雪松，小径穿行其间，那路不但是我们人的脚踩出来的，也是鹿和其他走兽踩出来的。你就这样走啊走，不知不觉间已从中山顶上下来，走进一片宽阔的草原，绿草满地，东一丛西一丛点缀着树木。越过这道山谷，是一片隆起的沙质平原，中央却相当凹陷，因此每逢下雨，雨水便渗进洼下部分的泥土里（那洼地宽阔得自成一个乡国），滋养着那里的青草；所以一年中大部分时候，草长得又绿又甜。
很久很久以前，在这道山谷或盆地里，住着一座草原犬鼠的村镇，他们与响尾蛇、蟰蛇、变色蜥、角蟾（Horned-toads）和穴鸮（Burrowing-owls）相处得还算太平。他们与穴鸮尤其交好，把穴鸮看作极庄重、极神圣的生灵。因此，这些草原犬鼠和他们的同伴从不搅扰穴鸮的议事或祭祀，对它们礼敬有加，穴鸮跳舞的时候，他们总离得远远的。
碰巧有一天，穴鸮们一大清早正独自举行一场盛大的舞会。他们跳的那种舞是他们格外珍视的，跳起来着实需要不小的巧劲。

===Original===

Each dancer, young man or maiden, carried upon his or her head a bowl of foam, and though their legs were crooked and their motions disjointed, they danced to the whistling of some and the clapping beaks of others, in perfect unison, and with such dexterity that they never spilled a speck of the foam on their sleek mantles of dun-black feather-work.
It chanced this morning of the Foam-dance that a Coyote was nosing about for Grasshoppers and Prairie-dogs. So quite naturally he was prowling around the by-streets in the borders of the Prairie-dog town. His house where he lived with his old grandmother stood back to the westward, just over the elevations that bounded Sunken Country, among the rocks. He heard the click-clack of the musicians and their shrill, funny little song:

> “I yami hota utchu tchapikya,

Tokos! tokos! tokos! tokos!”

===Chinese===

每个舞者，无论小伙还是姑娘，头上都顶着一只盛满泡沫的碗；尽管他们的腿弯弯曲曲、动作磕磕绊绊，他们却随着一些同伴的哨声和另一些同伴的击喙声跳得整整齐齐，而且那么稳当，一丝泡沫也没有洒在他们茶褐色羽毛织成的光亮披风上。
碰上这泡沫舞的早晨，一只郊狼（Coyote）正在四处嗅探蚱蜢和草原犬鼠。于是再自然不过，他在草原犬鼠村镇边缘的小巷间逡巡起来。他和老祖母同住的家在西边不远，就在界定“洼乡”的岗岩那边，乱石之间。他听见了乐手们的咔嗒声和他们尖细滑溜的小曲：

> “I yami hota utchu tchapikya,

Tokos! tokos! tokos! tokos!”

===Original===

So he pricked up his ears, and lifting his tail, trotted forward toward the level place between the hillocks and doorways of the village, where the Owls were dancing in a row. He looked at them with great curiosity, squatting on his haunches, the more composedly to observe them. Indeed, he became so much interested and amused by their shambling motions and clever evolutions, that he could no longer contain his curiosity. So he stepped forward, with a smirk and a nod toward the old master of ceremonies, and said: “My father, how are you and your children these many days?”
“Contented and happy,” replied the old Owl, turning his attention to the dancing again.
“Yes, but I observe you are dancing,” said the Coyote. “A very fine dance, upon my word! Charming! Charming! And why should you be dancing if you were not contented and happy, to be sure?”
“We are dancing,” responded the Owl, “both for our pleasure and for the good of the town.”
“True, true,” replied the Coyote; “but what’s that which looks like foam these dancers are carrying on their heads, and why do they dance in so limping a fashion?”
“You see, my friend,” said the Owl, turning toward the Coyote, “we hold this to be a very sacred performance﻿—very sacred indeed. Being such, these my children are initiated and so trained in the mysteries of the sacred society of which this is a custom that they can do very strange things in the observance of our ceremonies. You ask what it is that looks like foam they are balancing on their heads. Look more closely, friend. Do you not observe that it is their own grandmothers’ heads they have on, the feathers turned white with age?”
“By my eyes!” exclaimed the Coyote, blinking and twitching his whiskers; “it seems so.”
“And you ask also why they limp as they dance,” said the Owl. “Now, this limp is essential to the proper performance of our dance﻿—so essential, in fact, that in order to attain to it these my children go through the pain of having their legs broken. Instead of losing by this, they gain in a great many ways. Good luck always follows them. They are quite as spry as they were before, and enjoy, moreover, the distinction of performing a dance which no other people or creatures in the world are capable of!”
“Dust and devils!” ejaculated the Coyote. “This is passing strange. A most admirable dance, upon my word!

===Chinese===

于是他竖起耳朵，翘起尾巴，一路小跑，朝村中小丘与门户之间那片平地奔去——穴鸮们正排成一行跳舞。他蹲坐在后腿上，带着极大的好奇打量他们，好沉住气细细观瞧。说真的，他们那些蹒跚的步态和灵巧的队形变换让他觉得那么有趣，他再也按捺不住好奇心了。于是他上前一步，朝主持祭祀的老穴鸮又假笑又点头，说道：“我的老丈，这些天您和您的孩子们都好啊？”
“又满足又快活。”老穴鸮答道，说罢又把心思放回舞蹈上去了。
“是嘛，可我看你们在跳舞呢，”郊狼说。“好一场妙舞，我说话算话！迷人！迷人！要是你们不满足不快活，干吗跳舞呢，可不是这个理？”
“我们跳舞，”穴鸮答道，“一为自家快活，二为全镇的福气。”
“对，对，”郊狼应道；“只是这些舞者头上顶着的、看着像泡沫的东西是什么？他们干吗一瘸一拐地跳？”
“你瞧，朋友，”穴鸮说着转向郊狼，“我们认为这是一场非常神圣的表演——实在非常神圣。正因如此，这些我的孩子们都受了礼，又在神圣会社的奥秘中受过训练——这舞正是那会社的一项规矩——所以他们能在我们的祭祀中做出非常奇异的事情来。你问他们头上顶着的、看着像泡沫的东西是什么。看仔细些，朋友。你没瞧出来吗，他们顶着的正是自己祖母们的头颅？那羽毛都因年迈而变白了。”
“我的眼睛作证！”郊狼喊道，直眨眼睛，胡须直抽抽；“倒真是这么回事。”
“你还问他们跳舞为什么一瘸一拐，”穴鸮说。“听着，这一瘸一拐正是我们这场舞跳得地道的要害——实在太要紧了，为了练成它，这些我的孩子们都经受了断腿之痛。他们非但无损，反倒处处得福，好运总跟着他们。他们跟从前一样轻快，更何况还享有一份殊荣：跳一场世上任何别人、别的生灵都跳不了的舞！”
“土和魔鬼！”郊狼脱口喊道。“这可太稀奇了。好一场令人叹服的舞，我说话算话！

===Original===

Why, every bristle on my body keeps time to the music and their steps! Look here, my friend, don’t you think that I could learn that dance?”
“Well,” replied the old Owl; “it is rather hard to learn, and you haven’t been initiated, you know; but, still, if you are determined that you would like to join the dance﻿—by the way, have you a grandmother?”
“Yes, and a fine old woman she is,” said he, twitching his mouth in the direction of his house. “She lives there with me. I dare say she is looking after my breakfast now.”
“Very well,” continued the old Owl, “if you care to join in our dance, fulfill the conditions, and I think we can receive you into our order.” And he added, aside: “The silly fool; the sneaking, impertinent wretch! I will teach him to be sticking that sharp nose of his into other people’s affairs!”
“All right! All right!” cried the Coyote, excitedly. “Will it last long?”
“Until the sun is so bright that it hurts our eyes,” said the Owl; “a long time yet.”
“All right! All right! I’ll be back in a little while,” said the Coyote; and, switching his tail into the air, away he ran toward his home. When he came to the house, he saw his old grandmother on the roof, which was a rock beside his hole, gathering fur from some skins which he had brought home, to make up a bed for the Coyote’s family.
“Ha, my blessed grandmother!” said the Coyote, “by means of your aid, what a fine thing I shall be able to do!”
The old woman was singing to herself when the Coyote dashed up to the roof where she was sitting, and, catching up a convenient leg-bone, whacked her over the pate and sawed her head off with the teeth of a deer. All bloody and soft as it was, he clapped it on his own head and raised himself on his hindlegs, bracing his tail against the ground, and letting his paws drop with the toes outspread, to imitate as nearly as possible the drooping wings of the dancing Owls. He found that it worked very well; so, descending with the head in one paw and a stone in the other, he found a convenient sharp-edged rock, and, laying his legs across it, hit them a tremendous crack with the stone, which broke them, to be sure, into splinters.
“Beloved Powers! Oh!” howled the Coyote. “*Oh‑o‑o‑o‑o!* the dance may be a fine thing, but the initiation is anything else!”
However, with his faith unabated, he shook himself together and got up to walk.

===Chinese===

哎呀，我身上的每一根毛都在跟着乐声和他们的舞步打拍子！听着，朋友，你不觉得我能学会那舞吗？”
“唔，”老穴鸮答道；“这舞挺难学的，再说你又没受礼，你知道；不过，你要是拿定主意想入伙跳——对了，你有祖母吗？”
“有，还是个挺好的老太太，”他说着朝自家的方向努了努嘴。“她跟我一块儿住在那儿。这会儿多半在照管我的早饭呢。”
“很好，”老穴鸮接着说，“你要是想入我们的舞，就把条件办齐，我想我们就能把你收进我们的会。”他又侧过身加了一句：这蠢货；这鬼鬼祟祟、不知轻重的贱货！我倒要教教训他，看他再拿那只尖鼻子去捅别人的事！
“好嘞！好嘞！”郊狼兴奋地喊。“要跳多久？”
“跳到太阳亮得刺眼为止，”穴鸮说；“还早着呢。”
“好嘞！好嘞！我一会儿就回来，”郊狼说；他把尾巴一甩甩向空中，一路跑回家去。到了家，他看见老祖母正在屋顶上——那屋顶就是他洞旁的一块岩石——从几张他叼回家的皮子上薅毛，好给郊狼一家铺床。
“哈，我亲爱的老祖母！”郊狼说，“多亏了您帮忙，我要做成一件多体面的事啊！”
老太太正自唱自听的当儿，郊狼冲上她坐着的屋顶，抓起一根顺手的腿骨，照她天灵盖就是一下，又用鹿的牙齿锯下了她的头。那头血淋淋、软塌塌的，他往自己头上一扣，后腿立起来，尾巴拄在地上，两只前爪垂下、脚趾张开，尽量学那跳舞的穴鸮耷拉着的翅膀的样子。他发觉这还真管用；于是头衔在一只前爪里、石头攥在另一只里下了屋顶，找了一块有利刃的石棱，把双腿架上去，抡起石头狠狠一砸——腿自然是断成了几截。
“亲爱的神力们！哎哟！”郊狼嚎道。“哎——哟——哟——哟！这舞兴许是件美事，可这受礼另当别论！”
话虽如此，他的信念丝毫未减，抖擞精神，挣扎着站起来要走。

===Original===

But he could walk only with his paws; his hindlegs dragged helplessly behind him. Nevertheless, with great pain, and getting weaker and weaker every step of the way, he made what haste he could back to the Prairie-dog town, his poor old grandmother’s head slung over his shoulders.
When he approached the dancers﻿—for they were still dancing﻿—they pretended to be greatly delighted with their proselyte, and greeted him, notwithstanding his rueful countenance, with many congratulatory epithets, mingled with very proper and warm expressions of welcome. The Coyote looked sick and groaned occasionally and kept looking around at his feet, as though he would like to lick them. But the old Owl extended his wing and cautioned him not to interfere with the working power of faith in this essential observance, and invited him (with a *hem* that very much resembled a suppressed giggle), to join in their dance. The Coyote smirked and bowed and tried to stand up gracefully on his stumps, but fell over, his grandmother’s head rolling around in the dirt. He picked up the grisly head, clapped it on his crown again and raised himself, and with many a howl, which he tried in vain to check, began to prance around; but ere long tumbled over again. The Burrowing-owls were filled with such merriment at his discomfiture that they laughed until they spilled the foam all down their backs and bosoms; and, with a parting fling at the Coyote which gave him to understand that he had made a fine fool of himself, and would know better than to pry into other people’s business next time, skipped away to a safe distance from him.
Then, seeing how he had been tricked, the Coyote fell to howling and clapping his thighs; and, catching sight of his poor grandmother’s head, all bloody and begrimed with dirt, he cried out in grief and anger: “Alas! alas! that it should have come to this! You little devils! I’ll be even with you! I’ll smoke you out of your holes.”
“What will you smoke us out with?” tauntingly asked the Burrowing-owls.
“Ha! you’ll find out. With yucca!”
“O! O! ha! ha!” laughed the Owls. “That is our succotash!”
“Ah, well! I’ll smoke you out!” yelled the Coyote, stung by their taunts.
“What with?” cried the Owls.
“Grease-weed.”
“He, ha! ho, ho! We make our mush-stew of that!”
“Ha! but I’ll smoke you out, nevertheless, you little beasts!”
“What with? What with?” shouted the Owls.

===Chinese===

可他只能靠两只前爪走道儿了；两条后腿在身后无可奈何地拖着。尽管如此，他忍着剧痛，一步弱似一步，拼命赶回草原犬鼠的村镇，可怜的老祖母的头颅还搭在他肩上。
他走近舞者——他们还在跳——他们便装出对这位新入会者大为欢喜的样子，不顾他哭丧的脸，用一堆道贺的名号招呼他，夹着十分得体而热络的欢迎话。郊狼面色恹恹，不时哼哼，老朝自己的脚看，仿佛想舔一舔。可老穴鸮伸出一边翅膀，告诫他不得搅扰这场要紧祭祀中信力之运行，并请他入伙同舞（那一声轻咳，很像是憋着的嗤笑）。郊狼陪着笑、打着躬，想在两条断腿桩上优雅地站起来，却摔倒了，祖母的头在土里滚了几滚。他拾起那颗狰狞的头，重新扣上头顶，撑起身子，伴着一声声怎么也压不住的嚎叫，开始蹦跳绕圈；可不一会儿又栽倒了。穴鸮们见他出丑，乐不可支，笑得泡沫顺着脊背和胸脯流了一身；他们朝郊狼甩下临别的一击——让他明白他可把自己出尽洋相了，下回可别再窥探别人的事——便蹦跳着躲到离他远远的安全地方去了。
郊狼这才明白自己着了道儿，坐在地上又是嚎又是拍大腿；一眼瞥见可怜的祖母那颗血污满面、沾满尘土的头，他悲愤交加地喊道：“唉！唉！怎么落到这步田地！你们这些小魔鬼！我要跟你们算账！我要用烟把你们从洞里熏出来！”
“你拿什么熏我们呀？”穴鸮们讥笑着问。
“哈！你们等着瞧。拿丝兰（yucca）！”
“噢！噢！哈！哈！”穴鸮们笑道。“那是我们的豆煮玉米！”
“啊，好啊！我也要把你们熏出来！”郊狼被他们的讥笑刺痛，吼道。
“拿什么熏？”穴鸮们喊。
“油脂草。”
“嘿，哈！呵呵！我们拿那个做糊糊炖菜！”
“哈！可我照样要熏你们，你们这些小畜生！”
“拿什么？拿什么？”穴鸮们喊道。

===Original===

“Yellow-top weeds,” said he.
“*Ha, ha!* All right; smoke away! We make our sweet gruel with that, you fool!”
“I’ll fix you! I’ll smoke you out! I’ll suffocate the very last one of you!”
“What with? What with?” shouted the Owls, skipping around on their crooked feet.
“Pitch-pine,” snarled the Coyote.
This frightened the Owls, for pitch-pine, even to this day, is sickening to them. Away they plunged into their holes, pell-mell.
Then the Coyote looked at his poor old grandmother’s begrimed and bloody head, and cried out﻿—just as Coyotes do now at sunset, I suppose﻿—“Oh, my poor, poor grandmother! So this is what they have caused me to do to you!” And, tormented both by his grief and his pain, he took up the head of his grandmother and crawled back as best he could to his house.
When he arrived there he managed to climb up to the roof, where her body lay stiff. He chafed her legs and sides, and washed the blood and dirt from her head, and got a bit of sinew, and sewed her head to her body as carefully as he could and as hastily. Then he opened her mouth, and, putting his muzzle to it, blew into her throat, in the hope of resuscitating her; but the wind only leaked out from the holes in her neck, and she gave no signs of animation. Then the Coyote mixed some pap of fine toasted meal and water and poured it down her throat, addressing her with vehement expressions of regret at what he had done, and apology and solicitation that she should not mind, as he didn’t mean it, and imploring her to revive. But the pap only trickled out between the stitches in her neck, and she grew colder and stiffer all the while; so that at last the Coyote gave it up, and, moaning, he betook himself to a near clump of piñon trees, intent upon vengeance and designing to gather pitch with which to smoke the Owls to death. But, weakened by his injuries, and filled with grief and shame and mortification, when he got there he could only lie down.
He was so engrossed in howling and thinking of his woes and pains that a Horned-toad, who saw him, and who hated him because of the insults he had frequently suffered from him and his kind, crawled into the throat of the beast without his noticing it. Presently the little creature struck up a song:

> “Tsakina muuu-ki

Iyami Kushina tsoiyakya

Aisiwaiki muki, muki,

Muuu ka!”

===Chinese===

“黄顶草，”他说。
“哈，哈！行啊；尽管熏吧！我们拿那个熬甜糊，你这傻瓜！”
“我要收拾你们！我要把你们熏出来！我要把你们一个不剩全憋死！”
“拿什么？拿什么？”穴鸮们踮着弯腿蹦来蹦去地喊。
“脂松木，”郊狼龇着牙说。
这一下可把穴鸮们吓坏了，因为直到今天，脂松的烟他们闻了还犯恶心。他们争先恐后，扑通扑通一头扎进了各自的洞里。
郊狼随即望了望可怜的老祖母那颗血污满面、尘土满头的头，喊了起来——我想，正像如今的郊狼日落时分叫的那样——“哦，我可怜、可怜的祖母！他们害得我对你做出这样的事来！”他悲恸加伤痛，双倍煎熬，只好抱起祖母的头，尽其所能地爬回家去。
到了家，他勉强爬上屋顶，她的尸身僵在那里。他揉搓她的腿和两肋，洗净她头上的血污，寻来一小段筋腱，把她的头尽可能又仔细又麻利地缝回她的身子。然后他掰开她的嘴，把自己的口鼻凑上去，往她喉咙里吹气，指望她还阳；可气只从她脖子的针眼里漏出来，她没有一丝活气。郊狼又调了些烤细面加水的糊，灌进她的喉咙，一边声声痛悔自己干下的事，又是赔罪又是央告，求她千万别放在心上，说他不是有意的，恳求她活转来。可那糊只会从她脖子上的缝针间滴滴答答漏出来，她却越来越凉、越来越僵；郊狼终于死了心，呜咽着挪到近旁一丛矮松树里，一心要报仇，打算采些松脂，把穴鸮们统统熏死。可他伤重力衰，又满怀悲恸、羞惭与懊丧，到了那儿，就只剩躺下的力气了。
他只顾得哀嚎、想自己的苦楚伤痛，一条角蟾望见了他——这条角蟾恨他，因为没少受他和他的同类的侮辱——便趁他不备，爬进了这畜生的喉咙。不多时，小东西竟唱起歌来：

> “Tsakina muuu-ki

Iyami Kushina tsoiyakya

Aisiwaiki muki, muki,

Muuu ka!”

===Original===

“*Ah‑a‑a‑a‑a‑a*,” the Coyote was groaning. But when he heard this song, apparently far off, and yet so near, he felt very strangely inside, so he thought and no doubt wondered if it were the song of some musician. At any rate, he lifted his head and looked all around, but hearing nothing, lay down again and bemoaned his fate.
Then the Horned-toad sang again. This time the Coyote called out immediately, and the Horned-toad answered: “Here I am.” But look as he would, the Coyote could not find the Toad. So he listened for the song again, and heard it, and asked who it was that was singing. The Horned-toad replied that it was he. But still the Coyote could not find him. A fourth time the Horned-toad sang, and the Coyote began to suspect that it was under him. So he lifted himself to see; and one of the spines on the Horned-toad’s neck pricked him, and at the same time the little fellow called out: “Here I am, you idiot, inside of you! I came upon you here, and being a medicine-man of some prominence, I thought I would explore your vitals and see what was the matter.”
“By the souls of my ancestors!” exclaimed the Coyote, “be careful what you do in there!”
The Horned-toad replied by laying his hand on the Coyote’s liver, and exclaiming: “What is this I feel?”
“Where?” said the Coyote.
“Down here.”
“Merciful daylight! it is my liver, without which no one can have solidity of any kind, or a proper vitality. Be very careful not to injure that; if you do, I shall die at once, and what will become of my poor wife and children?”
Then the Horned-toad climbed up to the stomach of the Coyote. “What is this, my friend?” said he, feeling the sides of the Coyote’s food-bag.
“What is it like?” asked the Coyote.
“Wrinkled,” said the Horned-toad, “and filled with a fearful mess of stuff!”
“Oh! mercy! mercy! good daylight! My precious friend, be very careful! That is the very source of my being﻿—my stomach itself!”
“Very well,” said the Horned-toad. Then he moved on somewhat farther and touched the heart of the Coyote, which startled him fearfully. “What is this?” cried the Horned-toad.
“Mercy, mercy! what are you doing?” exclaimed the Coyote.
“Nothing﻿—feeling of your vitals,” was the reply. “What is it?”
“Oh, what is it like?” said the Coyote.
“Shaped like a pine-nut,” said the Horned-toad, “as nearly as I can make out; it keeps leaping so.”
“Leaping, is it?”

===Chinese===

“啊——啊——啊——啊——”郊狼正这样哀嚎。可他一听见这支歌，仿佛远在天边，却又近在耳边，心里顿觉说不出的异样；他想了又想，无疑纳闷这是不是哪位乐师的歌。总之，他抬起头四下张望，可什么也没听见，便又躺下哀叹自己的命。
于是角蟾又唱了一遍。这回郊狼立刻喊出声来，角蟾答道：“我在这儿。”可郊狼左看右看，就是找不着他。他便又静等歌声，听到了，就问是谁在唱。角蟾说是他。郊狼还是找不着他。角蟾唱到第四遍，郊狼开始疑心那声音就在自己身子底下。他撑起身子查看；角蟾脖子上的一根尖刺扎了他一下，同时那小家伙喊道：“我在这儿呢，你这蠢货，在你肚子里！我在这儿撞上了你，我嘛，是个小有名气的巫医，就想进去探探你的五脏，看看你到底哪儿出了毛病。”
“凭我祖先的魂灵！”郊狼喊道，“你在里面可千万小心！”
角蟾的回答是把一只手按在郊狼的肝上，喊道：“我摸着的这是什么？”
“在哪儿？”郊狼说。
“在这下头。”
“仁慈的白昼！那是我的肝啊，没了它，谁也成不了个结实身子，也没了活气儿。千万别碰坏了它；碰坏了，我立刻就得死，我那可怜的老婆孩子可怎么办？”
角蟾于是爬到郊狼的胃那儿。“这是什么，我的朋友？”他说着，摸了摸郊狼食袋的边。
“它什么样儿？”郊狼问。
“皱皱巴巴，”角蟾说，“还塞满了一摊吓人的乱七八糟的东西！”
“哦！行行好！行行好！好白昼！我的宝贝朋友，千万小心！那正是我这条命的根本——我的胃呀！”
“很好，”角蟾说。他又往上挪了挪，碰了碰郊狼的心，吓得他魂飞魄散。“这是什么？”角蟾喊道。
“行行好，行行好！你要干什么？”郊狼喊道。
“没干什么——摸摸你的五脏，”对方答道。“它是什么？”
“哦，它什么样儿？”郊狼说。
“像颗松子，”角蟾说，“就我摸出来的看；它一个劲儿地跳呢。”
“跳，是吗？”

===Original===

howled the Coyote. “Mercy! my friend, get away from there! That is the very heart of my being, the thread that ties my existence, the home of my emotions, and my knowledge of daylight. Go away from there, do, I pray you! If you should scratch it ever so little, it would be the death of me, and what would my wife and children do?”
“Hey!” said the Horned-toad, “you wouldn’t be apt to insult me and my people any more if I touched you up there a little, would you?” And he hooked one of his horns into the Coyote’s heart. The Coyote gave one gasp, straightened out his limbs, and expired.
“*Ha, ha!* you villain! Thus would you have done to me, had you found the chance; thus unto you”﻿—saying which he found his way out and sought the nearest water-pocket he could find.
---
So you see from this, which took place in the days of the ancients, it may be inferred that the instinct of meddling with everything that did not concern him, and making a universal nuisance of himself, and desiring to imitate everything that he sees, ready to jump into any trap that is laid for him, is a confirmed instinct with the Coyote, for those are precisely his characteristics today.
Furthermore, Coyotes never insult Horned-toads nowadays, and they keep clear of Burrowing-owls. And ever since then the Burrowing-owls have been speckled with gray and white all over their backs and bosoms, because their ancestors spilled foam over themselves in laughing at the silliness of the Coyote.
Thus shortens my story.

===Chinese===

郊狼嚎道。“行行好！我的朋友，离那儿远点！那正是我这条命的心，拴着我这条命的线，我一切情感的居所，我对白昼的全部知识。求求你，离开那儿吧！你哪怕挠它一下下，我就没命了，我的老婆孩子可怎么活？”
“嘿！”角蟾说，“要是我在这儿轻轻碰你一下，你就不大敢再侮辱我和我的族人了吧？”说着，他把一只角钩进了郊狼的心。郊狼倒抽一口气，四肢一挺，断了气。
“哈，哈！你这恶棍！要是让你逮着机会，你也会这样对我；这就是给你的报应”——说罢，他寻路钻了出去，找最近的水洼子去了。
---
由此可知，这发生在先民时代的事让我们可以推断：好管与自己毫不相干的闲事，把自己活成个人人嫌，见什么就想学什么，人家设下什么圈套他都肯一头钻进去——这些秉性在郊狼身上是积习已深了，因为那正是他今天的德行。
再者，如今的郊狼再也不敢侮辱角蟾，也对穴鸮敬而远之。而打那以后，穴鸮的脊背和胸脯便满布灰白斑点，因为他们的祖先嘲笑郊狼的蠢样时，把泡沫泼了一身。
我的故事，就此收梢。

## How the Coyote Danced with the Blackbirds / 郊狼怎样同黑鸟共舞
===Original===

One late autumn day in the times of the ancients, a large council of Blackbirds were gathered, fluttering and chattering, on the smooth, rocky slopes of Gorge Mountain, northwest of Zuni. Like ourselves, these birds, as you are well aware, congregate together in autumn time, when the harvests are ripe, to indulge in their festivities before going into winter quarters; only we do not move away, while they, on strong wings and swift, retreat for a time to the Land of Everlasting Summer.
Well, on this particular morning they were making a great noise and having a grand dance, and this was the way of it: They would gather in one vast flock, somewhat orderly in its disposition, on the sloping face of Gorge Mountain﻿—the older birds in front, the younger ones behind﻿—and down the slope, chirping and fluttering, they would hop, hop, hop, singing:

> “Ketchu, Ketchu, oñtilã, oñtilã,

Ketchu, Ketchu, oñtilã, oñtilã!

Âshokta a yá-à-laa Ke‑e‑tchu,

Oñtilã,

Oñtilã!”﻿—

> Blackbirds, Blackbirds, dance away, O, dance away, O!

Blackbirds, Blackbirds, dance away, O, dance away, O!

Down the Mountain of the Gorges, Blackbirds,

Dance away, O!

Dance away, O!﻿—

===Chinese===

上古时候，一个深秋的日子，一大群黑鸟聚集在祖尼（Zuni）西北峡谷山（Gorge Mountain）平滑多岩的山坡上，扑翅聒噪，开它们的议事大会。你是知道的，这些鸟同我们一样，一到秋收成熟的时节便聚到一起，在进入越冬地之前纵情欢庆；只不过我们不走，它们却振着强劲迅疾的翅膀，暂且退往常夏之国（Land of Everlasting Summer）去。
就在这个特别的早晨，它们正闹得震天响，举行一场盛大的舞蹈，跳法是这样的：它们在峡谷山倾斜的山面上聚成浩浩荡荡的一大群，队列还算齐整——年长的鸟在前，年轻的在后——顺着山坡，边啁啾边扑翅，一跳一跳一跳地蹦下去，唱着：

> “Ketchu, Ketchu, oñtilã, oñtilã,

Ketchu, Ketchu, oñtilã, oñtilã!

Âshokta a yá-à-laa Ke‑e‑tchu,

Oñtilã,

Oñtilã!”—

> 黑鸟啊黑鸟，飞舞吧，喔，飞舞吧，喔！

黑鸟啊黑鸟，飞舞吧，喔，飞舞吧，喔！

从这峡谷之山上，黑鸟啊，

飞舞吧，喔！

飞舞吧，喔！—

===Original===

and, spreading their wings, with many a flutter, flurry, and scurry, *keh keh﻿—keh keh﻿—keh keh﻿—keh keh*﻿—they would fly away into the air, swirling off in a dense, black flock, circling far upward and onward; then, wheeling about and darting down, they would dip themselves in the broad spring which flows out at the foot of the mountain, and return to their dancing place on the rocky slopes.
A Coyote was out hunting (as if he could catch anything, the beast!) and saw them, and was enraptured.
“You beautiful creatures!” he exclaimed. “You graceful dancers! Delight of my senses! How do you do that, anyway? Couldn’t I join in your dance﻿—the first part of it, at least?”
“Why, certainly; yes,” said the Blackbirds. “We are quite willing,” the masters of the ceremony said.
“Well,” said the Coyote, “I can get on the slope of the rocks and I can sing the song with you; but I suppose that when you leap off into the air I shall have to sit there patting the rock with my paw and my tail and singing while you have the fun of it.”
“It may be,” said an old Blackbird, “that we can fit you out so that you can fly with us.”
“Is it possible!” cried the Coyote, “Then by all means do so. By the Blessed Immortals! Now, if I am only able to circle off into the air like you fellows, I’ll be the biggest Coyote in the world!”
“I think it will be easy,” resumed the old Blackbird. “My children,” said he, “you are many, and many are your wing-feathers. Contribute each one of you a feather to our friend.” Thereupon the Blackbirds, each one of them, plucked a feather from his wing. Unfortunately they all plucked feathers from the wings on the same side.
“Are you sure, my friend,” continued the old Blackbird, “that you are willing to go through the operation of having these feathers planted in your skin? If so, I think we can fit you out.”
“Willing?﻿—why, of course I am willing.” And the Coyote held up one of his arms, and, sitting down, steadied himself with his tail. Then the Blackbirds thrust in the feathers all along the rear of his forelegs and down the sides of his back, where wings ought to be. It hurt, and the Coyote twitched his mustache considerably; but he said nothing. When it was done, he asked: “Am I ready now?”
“Yes,” said the Blackbirds; “we think you’ll do.”

===Chinese===

唱罢，它们张开翅膀，扑扑棱棱、忙忙乱乱地——keh keh—keh keh—keh keh—keh keh——飞上空中，卷作一团浓黑的鸟群，盘旋着越飞越高越飞越远；然后兜转过来，箭一般俯冲下去，在山脚下涌出的那泓宽宽的泉水里蘸一蘸身子，再回到岩坡上的舞场。
一只郊狼外出打猎（就仿佛它逮得着什么似的，这畜生！），看见了它们，欢喜得魂儿都飞了。
“美丽的生灵啊！”它赞叹道。“优雅的舞者们！悦我耳目！这你们究竟是怎么做到的？我就不能加入你们的舞——至少加入前半段？”
“怎么不能；可以呀，”黑鸟们说。“我们十分愿意，”司仪们说。
“那么，”郊狼说，“我能上到岩坡上，也能跟你们一起唱那歌；不过我想，等你们跃上天空的时候，我就只好坐在那儿，用爪子和尾巴拍着岩石，一边唱，看你们痛快了。”
“也许，”一只老黑鸟说，“我们能把你装备起来，让你跟我们一起飞。”
“当真可能吗！”郊狼喊道，“那可千万请你们这么做。凭受福的不朽者们起誓！只要我能像你们这些家伙一样盘旋着飞上天，我就是天下最大的郊狼了！”
“我看不难，”老黑鸟接着说。“孩子们，”它说，“你们数量众多，翎羽也多。你们每只都贡献一根翅羽给我们的朋友吧。”于是黑鸟们每只都从翅膀上拔下一根羽毛。不巧的是，它们全都是从同一侧的翅膀上拔的羽。
“你拿得准吗，朋友，”老黑鸟又说，“你愿意受这一番插羽入肤的手术？若愿意，我想我们就能把你装备起来。”
“愿意？——嗐，当然愿意。”郊狼举起一只前腿，坐下来，用尾巴撑稳身子。于是黑鸟们把羽毛沿着它两条前腿的后缘一路插下去，又插满它背部两侧本该长翅膀的地方。很疼，郊狼的胡子直抽抽；可它一声不吭。插完了，它问：“我这就算好了？”
“好了，”黑鸟们说；“我们看你能行了。”

===Original===

So they formed themselves again on the upper part of the slope, sang their songs, and hopped along down with many a flutter, flurry, and scurry﻿—*Keh keh, keh keh, keh keh*﻿—and away they flew off into the air.
The Coyote, somewhat startled, got out of time, but followed bravely, making heavy flops; but, as I have said before, the wings he was supplied with were composed of feathers all plucked from one side, and therefore he flew slanting and spirally and brought up with a whack, which nearly knocked the breath out of him, against the side of the mountain. He picked himself up, and shook himself, and cried out: “Hold! Hold! Hold on, hold on, there!” to the fast-disappearing Blackbirds. “You’ve left me behind!”
When the birds returned they explained: “Your wings are not quite thick enough, friend; and, besides, even a young Blackbird, when he is first learning to fly, does just this sort of thing that you have been doing﻿—makes bad work of it.”
“Sit down again,” said the old Blackbird. And he called out to the rest: “Get feathers from your other sides also, and be careful to select a few strong feathers from the tips of the wings, for by means of these we cleave the air, guide our movements, and sustain our flight.”
So the Blackbirds all did as they were bidden, and after the new feathers were planted, each one plucked out a tail-feather, and the most skilful of the Blackbirds inserted these feathers into the tip of the Coyote’s tail. It made him wince and “*yip*” occasionally; but he stood it bravely and reared his head proudly, thinking all the while: “What a splendid Coyote I shall be! Did ever anyone hear of a Coyote flying?”
The procession formed again. Down the slope they went, hopity-hop, hopity-hop, singing their song, and away they flew into the air, the Coyote in their midst. Far off and high they circled and circled, the Coyote cutting more eager pranks than any of the rest. Finally they returned, dipped themselves again into the spring, and settled on the slopes of the rocks.
“There, now,” cried out the Coyote, with a flutter of his feathery tail, “I can fly as well as the rest of you.”
“Indeed, you do well!” exclaimed the Blackbirds. “Shall we try it again?”
“Oh, yes! Oh, yes! I’m a little winded,” cried the Coyote, “but this is the best fun I ever had.”
The Blackbirds, however, were not satisfied with their companion.

===Chinese===

于是它们又在坡顶列好队，唱起歌，扑扑棱棱、忙忙乱乱地一跳一跳往下蹦——Keh keh, keh keh, keh keh——然后呼啦一声飞上了空中。
郊狼有点猝不及防，节拍乱了，却还是勇敢地跟着，扑腾得沉重；可是，如我先前所说，给它装的翅膀全是用同一侧拔下的羽毛编成的，于是它飞得歪斜打旋，砰的一声撞在山腰上，差点把气儿撞没了。它爬起来，抖了抖身子，冲着迅速远去的黑鸟们大喊：“且慢！且慢！等等，等等！你们把我撂下啦！”
鸟儿们回来了，解释道：“你的翅膀还不够厚，朋友；再说，就连年幼的黑鸟，刚开始学飞的时候，也净干你方才那种事——弄得一团糟。”
“再坐下吧，”老黑鸟说。它又向众鸟喊道：“从你们另一侧也拔些羽毛来，还要细心挑几根翅膀尖上的硬翎，我们靠它们劈开空气、掌控方向、托住身子。”
黑鸟们便都照吩咐做了。新羽毛插好之后，每只鸟又拔下一根尾羽，由黑鸟中最巧手的几只把这些羽毛插进郊狼的尾巴尖。疼得它直皱缩，不时“yip”地一声；可它勇敢地忍住了，还骄傲地昂着头，心里一直想：“我将是多么了不起的一只郊狼！谁听说过会飞的郊狼？”
队伍又列好了。它们顺着山坡一蹦一跳、一蹦一跳地下去，唱着歌，然后呼啦飞上空中，郊狼混在队伍当中。它们绕着大圈越飞越远、越飞越高，郊狼撒欢撒得比谁都欢。最后它们折回来，又到泉水里蘸了蘸身子，落在岩坡上。
“瞧，怎么样，”郊狼抖着它那羽毛尾巴喊道，“我飞得跟你们大家一样好了。”
“可不，你飞得不赖！”黑鸟们赞叹。“再来一回？”
“哦，好！哦，好！我有点喘，”郊狼喊道，“可这是我这辈子玩得最痛快的一回。”
然而黑鸟们对这位同伴并不满意。

===Original===

They found him less sedate than a dancer ought to be, and, moreover, his irregular cuttings-up in the air were not to their taste. So the old ones whispered to one another: “This fellow is a fool, and we must pluck him when he gets into the air. We’ll fly so far this time that he will get a little tired out and cry to us for assistance.”
The procession formed, and hopity-hop, hopity-hop, down the mountain slope they went, and with many a flutter and flurry flew off into the air. The Coyote, unable to restrain himself, even took the lead. On and on and on they flew, the Blackbirds and the Coyote, and up and up and up, and they circled round and round, until the Coyote found himself missing a wing stroke occasionally and falling out of line; and he cried out: “Help! help, friends, help!”
“All right!” cried the Blackbirds. “Catch hold of his wings; hold him up!” cried the old ones. And the Blackbirds flew at him; and every time they caught hold of him (the old fool all the time thinking they were helping) they plucked out a feather, until at last the feathers had become so thin that he began to fall, and he fell and fell and fell﻿—flop, flop, flop, he went through the air﻿—the few feathers left in his forelegs and sides and the tip of his tail just saving him from being utterly crushed as he fell with a thud to the ground. He lost his senses completely, and lay there as if dead for a long time. When he awoke, he shook his head sadly, and, with a crestfallen countenance and tail dragging between his legs, betook himself to his home over the mountains.
The agony of that fall had been so great and the heat of his exertions so excessive, that the feathers left in his forelegs and tail-tip were all shrivelled up into little ugly black fringes of hair. His descendants were many.
Therefore you will often meet coyotes to this day who have little black fringes along the rear of their forelegs, and the tips of their tails are often black. Thus it was in the days of the ancients.
Thus shortens my story.

===Chinese===

它们发现它不像一个舞者应有的那样沉稳，况且它在空中的那些胡闹腾法也不合它们的口味。于是年长的鸟儿们交头接耳：“这家伙是个蠢货，等它上了天，我们得拔它的毛。这一回我们飞得远远的，让它乏得受不住，向我们讨救。”
队伍列好，一蹦一跳、一蹦一跳地下了山坡，扑扑棱棱飞上天空。郊狼按捺不住，甚至还抢了头。飞呀飞呀飞呀，黑鸟们和郊狼，越飞越高、越飞越高，一圈又一圈地盘旋，直到郊狼发觉自己时不时漏拍一下翅膀、掉出队列；它喊起来：“救命！救命，朋友们，救命！”
“好嘞！”黑鸟们喊。“抓住它的翅膀；托住它！”年长的鸟喊。于是黑鸟们朝它扑去；它们每回抓住它一下（这老傻瓜一直当它们是在帮忙），就拔走一根羽毛，直到羽毛稀得它开始往下掉；它掉啊掉啊掉——扑、扑、扑地穿过空气——前腿上、体侧和尾巴尖剩下的那几根羽毛，总算让它砸在地上时没被摔成齑粉。它彻底失了知觉，像死了一样在那儿躺了许久。醒来后，它悲伤地摇摇头，耷拉着脸，尾巴夹在两腿之间，翻山回家去了。
那一跤摔得太惨，用力又太发热，留在它前腿和尾巴尖上的羽毛全都枯蜷成了又小又丑的黑色毛边。它的后代繁衍众多。
所以直到今天，你还常常会见到前腿后缘长着黑色毛边、尾巴尖发黑的郊狼。上古之时便是这样。
我的故事就此收短。

## How the Coyotes Tried to Steal the Children of the Sacred Dance / 郊狼们怎样企图偷走圣舞之子
===Original===

In the times of the ancients, when our people lived in various places about the valley of Zuni where ruins now stand, it is said that an old Coyote lived in Cedar Canyon with his family, which included a fine litter of pups. It is also said that at this time there lived on the crest of Thunder Mountain, back of the broad rock column or pinnacle which guards its western portion, one of the gods of the Sacred Drama Dance (*Kâkâ*),13 named Kʻyámakwe, with his children, many in number and altogether like himself.
One day the old Coyote of Cedar Canyon went out hunting, and as he was prowling around among the sage-bushes below Thunder Mountain, he heard the clang and rattle and the shrill cries of the Kʻyámakwe. He pricked up his ears, stuck his nose into the air, sniffed about and looked all around, and presently discovered the Kʻyámakwe children running rapidly back and forth on the very edge of the mountain.
“Delight of my senses, what pretty creatures they are! Good for me!” he piped, in a jovial voice. “I am the finder of children. I must capture the little fellows tomorrow, and bring them up as Coyotes ought to be brought up. Aren’t they handsome, though?”
All this he said to himself, in a fit of conceit, with his nose in the air (presumptuous cur!), planning to steal the children of a god! He hunted no more that day, but ran home as fast as he could, and, arriving there, he said: “Wife! Wife! O wife! I have discovered a number of the prettiest waifs one ever saw. They are children of the *Kâkâ*, but what matters that? They are there, running back and forth and clanging their rattles along the very edge of Thunder Mountain. I mean to steal them tomorrow, every one of them, and bring them here!”
“Mercy on us!” exclaimed the old Coyote’s wife. “There are children enough and to spare already. What in the world can we do with all of them, you fool?”
“But they are pretty,” said the Coyote. “Immensely fine! Every Coyote in the country would envy us the possession of them!”
“But you say they are many,” continued the wife.
“Well, yes, a good many,” said the Coyote.
“Well, why not divide them among our associated clans?” suggested the old woman. “You never can capture them alone; it is rare enough that you capture *anything* alone, leave out the children of the Kʻyámakwe. Get your relatives to help you, and divide the children amongst them.”

===Chinese===

上古之时，我们族人散居在祖尼（Zuni）谷地各处——如今那些地方只剩废墟——据说有一只老郊狼带着一家子住在雪松峡谷（Cedar Canyon），家里有一窝顶好的崽子。又据说那时候，雷霆山（Thunder Mountain）顶上、在那根守卫山西部的粗大石柱或石峰背后，住着一位圣戏之舞（Kâkâ）13的神明，名叫 Kʻyámakwe，同他的孩子们在一起——孩子多得数不清，个个都和他一个模样。
一天，雪松峡谷的老郊狼外出打猎，正在雷霆山下方的鼠尾草丛里转悠，忽然听见 Kʻyámakwe 们的铿锵声、响板声和尖利喊叫。它竖起耳朵，鼻子朝天上翘，四下嗅着、望着，不多时就发现 Kʻyámakwe 的孩子们正贴着山的最边缘跑来跑去。
“悦我耳目的，多俊的生灵啊！便宜了我！”它乐呵呵地尖声说。“我可是个寻孩子的行家。明天我就去把那些小家伙捉来，照郊狼该有的样子把他们抚养大。你说他们俊不俊？”
这些话它全是自言自语，一股自大风里说出来的，鼻子还翘着天（好个不知天高地厚的癞狗！），居然打起了偷神明孩子的主意！那天它也不打猎了，撒腿往家跑，一到家就说：“老婆子！老婆子！哎，老婆子！我发现了一批小家伙，一个赛一个俊，谁也没见过那么俊的。他们是 Kâkâ 的孩子，可那又有什么要紧？他们就在雷霆山的最边上跑来跑去，把响板敲得铿铿响。我明天就去把他们偷来，一个不落，全带到这儿来！”
“老天爷可怜见！”老郊狼的婆娘喊道。“家里孩子已经多得没处搁了。这一大群咱们拿他们怎么办呀，你这蠢货？”
“可他们俊啊，”郊狼说。“俊得出奇！养下了他们，天底下的郊狼哪个不眼红咱们！”
“可你自己说了他们人多，”婆娘接着说。
“嗯，是不少，好大一窝，”郊狼说。
“那何不分给咱们联着的各个氏族呢？”老婆子出主意道。“你单枪匹马从来逮不着他们；你单枪匹马连什么都难得逮着一回呢，更别说 Kʻyámakwe 的孩子了。去请你的亲戚帮你，把孩子分给他们。”

===Original===

“Well, now, come to think of it, it is a good plan,” said the Coyote, with his nose on his neck. “If I get up this expedition I’ll be a big chief, won’t I? Hurrah! Here’s for it!” he shouted; and, switching his tail in the face of his wife, he shot out of the hole and ran away to a high rock, where, squatting down with a most important air and his nose lifted high, he cried out:

> “Au hii lâ-â-â-â!

Su Homaya-kwe!

Su Kemaya-kwe!

Su Ayalla-kwe!

Su Kutsuku-kwe!

> [Listen ye all!

Coyotes of the Cedar-canyon tribe!

Coyotes of the Sunflower-stalk-plain tribe!

Coyotes of the Lifted-stone-mountain tribe!

Coyotes of the Place-of-rock-gullies tribe!]

I have instructions for you this day. I have found waif children many﻿—of the Kʻyámakwe, the young. I would steal the waif-children many, of the Kʻyámakwe, the young. I would steal them tomorrow, that they may be adopted of us. I would have your aid in the stealing of the Kʻyámakwe young. Listen ye all, and tomorrow gather in council. Thus much I instruct ye:

> “Coyotes of the Cedar-canyon tribe!

Coyotes of the Sunflower-stalk-plain tribe!

Coyotes of the Lifted-stone-mountain tribe!

Coyotes of the Place-of-rock-gullies tribe!”

===Chinese===

“唔，这么说来，倒是个好主意，”郊狼说，鼻子缩回到脖子上。“这趟远征要是张罗起来，我就是个大头领了，是不是？好哇！就这么办！”它喊道，尾巴一甩抽在婆娘脸上，蹿出洞去，跑到一块高岩上蹲下，摆出十足要人的架子，鼻子高高扬起，大喊：

> “Au hii lâ-â-â-â!

Su Homaya-kwe!

Su Kemaya-kwe!

Su Ayalla-kwe!

Su Kutsaku-kwe!

> [你们都听着！

雪松峡谷部落的郊狼们！

向日葵秆平原部落的郊狼们！

举石山部落的郊狼们！

岩壑之地部落的郊狼们！]

今天我有号令给你们。我发现弃儿许多——是 Kʻyámakwe 的孩子。我要去偷那许多弃儿，Kʻyámakwe 的孩子。我明天就去偷，好叫他们归我们收养。偷 Kʻyámakwe 崽子这事，要借你们的力。你们都听着，明天聚齐议事。我要晓谕你们的就这么多：

> “雪松峡谷部落的郊狼们！

向日葵秆平原部落的郊狼们！

举石山部落的郊狼们！

岩壑之地部落的郊狼们！”

===Original===

It was growing dark, and immediately from all quarters, in dark places under the canyons and arroyos, issued answering howls and howls. You should have seen that crowd of Coyotes the next morning, large and small, old and young﻿—all four tribes gathered together in the plain below Thunder Mountain!
When they had all assembled, the Coyote who had made the discovery mounted an anthill, sat down, and, lifting his paw, was about to give directions with the air of a chief when an ant bit him. He lost his dignity, but resumed it again on the top of a neighboring rock. Again he stuck his nose into the air and his paw out, and with ridiculous assumption informed the Coyotes that he was chief of them all and that they would do well to pay attention to his directions. He then showed himself much more skilful than you might have expected. As you know, the cliff of Thunder Mountain is very steep, especially that part back of the two standing rocks. Well, this was the direction of the Coyote:
“One of you shall place himself at the base of the mountain; another shall climb over him, and the first one shall grasp his tail; and another over them, and his tail shall be grasped by the second, and so on until the top is reached. Hang tight, my friends, everyone of you, and everyone fall in line. Eructate thoroughly before you do so. If you do not, we may be in a pretty mess; for, supposing that anyone along the line should hiccup, he would lose his hold, and down we would all fall!”
So the Coyotes all at once began to curve their necks and swell themselves up and strain and wriggle and belch wind as much as possible. Then all fell into a line and grabbed each other’s tails, and thus they extended themselves in a long string up the very face of Thunder Mountain. A ridiculous little pup was at one end and a good, strong, grizzled old fellow﻿—no other than the chief of the party﻿—at the other.
“Souls of my ancestors! Hang tight, my friends! Hang tight! Hang tight!” said he, when, suddenly, one near the top, in the agitation of the moment, began to sneeze, lost his hold, and down the whole string, hundreds of them, fell, and were completely flattened out among the rocks.

===Chinese===

天渐渐黑了，立刻，四面八方，从峡谷和干河床的暗处，传出一片应答的嗥叫，此起彼伏。第二天早上你真该瞧瞧那群郊狼，大的小的、老的少的——四个部落全聚在雷霆山下的平原上！
等大家都到齐了，那只发现孩子的郊狼登上一座蚁丘坐下，抬起爪子，正要摆出头领的派头发号施令，一只蚂蚁咬了它一口。它的威风垮了，又在旁边一块岩石顶上重新端起来。它又把鼻子翘向天、爪子伸出去，用一种可笑的自负告知众郊狼：它是大家的头领，他们最好留心听它的调遣。随后它露的那一手，倒比你预想的能耐大些。你是知道的，雷霆山的崖壁极陡，尤其是那两块立岩背后的那一段。郊狼的部署是这样的：
“你们一个先伏在山根；另一个从他身上爬上去，头一个要咬紧他的尾巴；再一个又叠上去，尾巴让第二个咬住；如此层层往上，直到够着山顶。都抓牢了，我的朋友们，一个也不许松，一个不落地排成一行。动手之前，先把肚里的气痛痛快快嗝出去。不然的话，咱们可要出大乱子；因为万一队伍里哪个打了个嗝，他就抓不住了，咱们就得全军摔下去！”
于是郊狼们顿时都弯起脖子、鼓起肚子，又挣又扭，拼命把肚子里的风嗝出去。然后一个个排成一列，互相咬住尾巴，就这样在雷霆山正面的崖壁上把自己连成一条长长的串儿。一头是一只可笑的小崽子，另一头是一个结结实实、灰扑扑的老家伙——不是别人，正是这一伙的头领。
“列祖列宗在上！抓牢，朋友们！抓牢！抓牢！”他正说着，突然，靠近顶上的一个一时激动打起喷嚏来，失了口，整条串儿、好几百只，全都摔了下来，在岩石中间摔得扁扁的。

===Original===

The warrior of the *Kâkâ*﻿—he of the Long Horn, with frightful, staring eyes, and visage blue with rage﻿—bow and war-club in hand, was hastening from the sacred lake in the west to rescue the children of the Kʻyámakwe. When he arrived they had been rescued already, so, after storming around a little and mauling such of the Coyotes as were not quite dead, he set to skin them all.
And ever since then you will observe that the dancers of the Long Horn have blue faces, and whenever they arrive in our pueblo wear collars of coyote-skin about their necks. That is the way they got them. Before that they had no collars. It is presumable that that is the reason why they bellow so and have such hoarse voices, having previously taken cold, every one of them, for the want of fur collars.
Thus shortens my story.

===Chinese===

那 Kâkâ 的武士——长角之神，瞪着一双吓人的眼睛，脸气得发蓝——手里拿着弓和战棒，正从西边的圣湖赶来搭救 Kʻyámakwe 的孩子们。等他赶到，孩子们早已脱险；于是他把四下里冲撞了一阵，把那些还没死透的郊狼揍了个够，便动手把他们全剥了皮。
打那以后你就看得到：长角舞者们脸上都画成蓝色，而且每逢他们来到我们镇上，脖子上都围着郊狼皮领子。那领子就是这么来的。在那之前他们没有领子。想来正因如此，他们才那样吼叫、嗓音才那样嘶哑——从前他们个个都着了凉，就因为缺一条毛领子。
我的故事就此收短。

## How the Gopher Raced with the Runners of Kʻiákime / 囊鼠怎样同克亚基梅的赛跑者们赛跑
===Original===

There was a time in the days of the ancients when the runners of Kʻiákime were famed above those of all other cities in the Valley of Shíwina for their strength, endurance, and swiftness of foot. In running the *tikwa*, or kicked-stick race, they overcame, one after another, the runners of Shíwina or Zuni, of Mátsaki or the Salt City, of Pínawa or the Town of the Winds, and in fact all who dared to challenge them or to accept their challenges.
The people of Shíwina and Mátsaki did not give up easily. They ran again and again, only to be beaten and to lose the vast piles of goods and precious things which they had staked or bet; and at last they were wholly disheartened and bereft of everything which without shame a man might exhibit for betting.
So the people of the two towns called a council, and the old men and runners gathered and discussed what could be done that the runners of Kʻiákime might be overcome. They thought of all the wise men and wise beings they knew of; one after another of them was mentioned, and at last a few prevailed in contending that for both wisdom and cunning or craft the Gopher took precedence over all those who had been mentioned. Forthwith a young man was dispatched to find an old Gopher who lived on the side of the hill near which the racecourse began.
He was out sunning himself, and finishing a cellar, when the young man approached him, and he called out: “Ha, grandson! Don’t bother me this morning; I am busy digging my cellars.”
The young man insisted that he came with an important message from his people. So the old Gopher ceased his work, and listened attentively while the young man related to him the difficulties they were in.
Said he: “Go back, my grandson, and tell your people to challenge the runners of Kʻiákime to run the race of the kicked stick with a runner whom they have chosen, a single one, the fourth day from this day; and tell your people, moreover, that I will run the race for them, providing only that the runners of Kʻiákime will permit me to go my own way, on my own road, which as you know runs underground.”
The youth thanked the old Gopher and was about to retire when the fat-sided, heavy-cheeked old fellow called to him to hold on a little. “Mind you,” said he. “Tell your people also that they shall bet for me only two things﻿—red paint and sacred yellow pollen.

===Chinese===

上古之时有一段岁月，克亚基梅（Kʻiákime）的赛跑者以力大、耐久、脚程快著称，希温纳（Shíwina）谷地各城镇的选手无人能及。跑 tikwa（踢棒赛）时，他们把希温纳即祖尼（Zuni）的、马察基（Mátsaki）即盐城的、皮纳瓦（Pínawa）即风之镇的选手一一击败，事实上，凡敢向他们挑战或接受他们挑战的，无一不被打败。
希温纳和马察基的人不肯轻易罢休。他们一赛再赛，只落得回回输，把成堆的财物珍宝押出去输了个精光；到最后彻底灰了心，凡是人不羞于拿出来下注的东西，全都荡然无存了。
于是两镇的人召开议事会，老人们和赛跑者们聚在一起商量：怎样才能把克亚基梅的赛跑者们压下去。他们把知道的智者、灵物挨个数了一遍；一个一个都被提到了，最后有几个人力争：论智慧和狡黠心机，囊鼠（Gopher）在所有被提到者之上。随即派了个年轻人去寻一只老公鼠——他住在赛跑道起点近旁的山坡上。
年轻人到他跟前时，他正在外头晒太阳，收尾一个地窖。他喊道：“哈，孙儿！今儿早上别烦我；我忙着挖地窖呢。”
年轻人执意说他带着族人托付的重要口信来。老公鼠这才停下活计，专心听年轻人讲他们的难处。
他说：“回去吧，我的孙儿，告诉你的族人：向克亚基梅的赛跑者们挑战，跑一场踢棒赛，对手由他们选定——就选一个，单枪匹马的；日子定在自今天起的第四天。还要告诉你的族人：由我来替他们跑这一场，只有一个条件——克亚基梅的赛跑者们得容我走我自己的路、我自己的道，你也知道，那条道打地底下走。”
年轻人谢过老公鼠正要走，这腮帮子厚墩墩、身子圆滚滚的老家伙又叫住他稍等。“记着，”他说。“还要告诉你的族人，只许替我押两样注——红颜料和神圣的黄色花粉。

===Original===

These shall, as it were, be the payment for my exertions, if I win, as I prize this sort of possession above all else.”
The young man returned and reported what the Gopher had said. Thereupon the people of Shíwina and Mátsaki sent a challenge to the people of Kʻiákime for a race, saying: “We bet all that we have against what you have won from us from time to time that our runner, the Gopher, who lives beside the beginning of our racecourse, will beat you in the race, which we propose shall be the fourth day from this day. The only condition we name is, that the Gopher shall be permitted to run in his own way, on his own road, which is underground.”
Right glad were the runners of Kʻiákime to run against anyone proposed by those whom they had so often beaten. They hesitated not a moment in replying that they would run against the Gopher or any other friend of the people of Mátsaki and Shíwina, stipulating only that the Gopher, if he ran underground, should appear at the surface occasionally, that they might know where he was. So it was arranged, and the acceptance of the challenge was reported to the Gopher, and the stipulation also which was named by the runners of Kʻiákime.
That night the old Gopher went to his younger brother, old like himself, heavy-cheeked, gray-and-brown-coated, and dusty with diggings of his cellars. “My younger brother,” said the old Gopher, “the fourth day from this day I am to run a race. I shall start at the beginning of the racecourse of the people of Kʻiákime over here, which is near my home, as you know. There I shall dig two holes; one at the beginning of the racecourse, the other a little farther on. Now, here at your home, near the Place of the Scratching Bushes, do you dig a hole, down below where the racecourse passes your place, off to one side of it, and another hole a little beyond the first. The means by which I shall be distinguished as a racer will be a red plume tied to my head. Do you also procure a red plume and tie it to your head. When you hear the thundering of the feet of the racers, run out and show yourself for a minute, and rush into the other hole as fast as you can.”
“I understand what you would have of me, and right gladly will I do it. It would please me exceedingly to take down the pride of those haughty runners of Kʻiákime, or at least to help in doing it,” replied the younger brother.

===Chinese===

这两样，就算是对我出力的犒赏吧——若是我赢了；因为这类东西，我看得比什么都金贵。”
年轻人回去把囊鼠的话一字不落地报了。于是希温纳和马察基的人向克亚基梅的人下了战书，说：“我们以我们现有的一切，对你们历次从我们手里赢走的一切下注：我们的选手囊鼠——就住在我们的跑道起点旁边——将在这场赛跑中赢你们；日子我们提议定在自今天起的第四天。我们唯一提出的条件是：必须容许囊鼠照他自己的方式、走他自己的道跑，那条道在地底下。”
克亚基梅的赛跑者们巴不得同这两个常败之邦推出的任何选手较量，喜出望外。他们毫不迟疑地答复：囊鼠也好，马察基和希温纳人的任何朋友也好，他们都奉陪；只有一个规定：囊鼠若在地底下跑，须得不时露到地面上来，好叫他们知道他跑到哪儿了。事情就这么讲定了，有人把应战的消息连同克亚基梅赛跑者们提出的这项规定一并报给了囊鼠。
当夜，老公鼠去到他弟弟家——同他一样老、腮帮子一样厚、一身灰褐的皮毛，挖地窖挖得尘土满身。“我的弟弟，”老公鼠说，“自今天起的第四天，我要跑一场赛。起点在这边克亚基梅人的跑道头上，你知道，离我家不远。我要在那里挖两个洞：一个在跑道起点，另一个再往前一点。而你在你家这儿，荆丛之地旁边，跑道经过你那地方的下头、偏向一侧的地方，挖一个洞，再在头一个洞前面不远处挖另一个。赛跑时认出我靠的是我头上绑的一根红羽。你也去弄一根红羽绑在头上。你一听见赛跑者们的脚步雷鸣，就蹿出来露一露面，随即尽快钻进另一个洞里去。”
“你要我做什么我明白了，我千肯万肯。能煞一煞克亚基梅那些傲气冲天的跑者的威风——哪怕只是搭把手——我也乐开了花，”弟弟答道。

===Original===

The old Gopher went on to the Sitting Space of the Red Shell, where dwelt another of his younger brothers precisely like himself and the one he had already spoken to, near whose home the racecourse also ran. To him he communicated the same information, and gave the same directions. Then he went on still farther to the place called Kʻópakʻyan, where dwelt another of his younger brothers. To him also he gave the same directions; and to still another younger brother, who dwelt beneath the base of the two broad pillars of Thunder Mountain, at the last turning-point of the racecourse; and to another brother, who dwelt at the Place of the Burnt Log; and lastly to another brother quite as cunning and inventive as himself, who dwelt just below Kʻiákime where the racecourse turned toward its end. When all these arrangements had been made, the old Gopher went back and settled himself comfortably in his nest.
Bright and early on the fourth day preparations were made for the race. The runners of Kʻiákime had been fasting and training in the sacred houses, and they came forth stripped and begirt for the racing, carrying their stick. Then came the people of Mátsaki and Shíwina, who gathered on the plain, and there they waited. But they waited not long, for soon the old Gopher appeared close in their midst, popping out of the ground, and on his head was a little red plume. He placed the stick which had been prepared for him, on the ground, where he could grab it with his teeth easily, saying: “Of course, you will excuse me if I do not kick my stick, since my feet are so short that I could not do so. On the other hand,” he said to the runners, “you do not have to dig your way as I do. Therefore, we are evenly matched.”
The runners of Kʻiákime, contemptuously laughing, asked him why he did not ask for some privilege instead of talking about things which meant nothing to them.
At last the word was given. With a yell and a spring, off dashed the racers of Kʻiákime, gaily kicking their stick before them. Grabbing his stick in his teeth, into the ground plunged the old Gopher. Fearful lest their runner should be beaten, the people of Shíwina and Mátsaki ran to a neighboring hill, watching breathlessly for him to appear somewhere in the course of the race above the plain. Away over the plain in a cloud of dust swept the runners of Kʻiákime.

===Chinese===

老公鼠又上路，去到红贝坐歇处（Sitting Space of the Red Shell），那里住着另一个弟弟，同他自己、同方才那位一模一样，跑道也从那人家近旁过。他把同样的话交代给这位，作了同样的吩咐。然后他继续往前，到一个叫 Kʻópakʻyan 的地方，那里又住着一个弟弟。对这位他也作了同样的吩咐；再往前还有一个弟弟，住在雷霆山（Thunder Mountain）两根宽大石柱的座底下——跑道最后一个转折点那里；还有一个弟弟住在焦木之地（Place of the Burnt Log）；最后还有一个弟弟，同他一样又奸又巧，住在克亚基梅下方一点、跑道折向终点的地方。这些安排都妥当了，老公鼠才回去，舒舒服服卧进自己的窝里。
第四天一大早，赛前的准备齐备了。克亚基梅的赛跑者们在神屋里持斋操练过了，此刻赤着身子、束好腰带出来赛跑，手里拿着他们的木棒。随后到了马察基和希温纳的人，聚在平原上，就在那里等。可他们没等多久，老公鼠很快就在他们正当中冒了出来，从地底下探出头，头顶上一根小红羽。他把为他备好的木棒搁在地上，搁在牙齿一叼就着的地方，说：“我的脚短得踢不成棒，这一节还请诸位包涵，原谅我不踢棒。话说回来，”他对赛跑者们说，“你们也用不着我这样一路挖着走啊。这么着，咱们谁也不占谁的便宜。”
克亚基梅的赛跑者们轻蔑地笑着，问他为何不干脆讨点别的便宜，倒说这些在他们听来毫无意思的话。
终于号令发出了。克亚基梅的选手们一声呐喊、一跃而起，欢快地把木棒朝前踢着，飞奔而去。老公鼠用牙咬住木棒，一头扎进地里。希温纳和马察基的人惟恐自己的选手落败，奔上一座近旁的小山，屏住呼吸，巴望他在跑道上的什么地方从平原的地面下冒出头来。克亚基梅的选手们卷着一团尘土，横扫过平原，远远去了。

===Original===

They were already far off, when suddenly, some distance before them, out of the ground in the midst of the racecourse, popped the old Gopher, to all appearance, the red plume dusty, but waving proudly on his forehead. After looking round at the runners, into the ground he plunged again. The people of Shíwina and Mátsaki yelled their applause. The runners of Kʻiákime, astounded that the Gopher should be ahead of them, redoubled their efforts. When they came near the Place of the Red Shell, behold! somewhat muddy round the eyes and nose, out popped the old Gopher again, to all appearance. Of course it was his brother, the red plume somewhat heavy with dirt, but still waving on his forehead.
On rushed the runners, and they had no sooner neared Kʻópakʻyan than again they saw the Gopher in advance of them, now apparently covered with sweat﻿—for this cunning brother had provided himself with a little water which he rubbed over his fur and made it all muddy, as though he were perspiring and had already begun to grow tired. He came out of his hole and popped into the other less quickly than the others had done; and the runners, who were not far behind him, raised a great shout and pushed ahead. When they thought they had gained on him, behold! in their pathway, all bedraggled with mud, apparently the same old Gopher appeared, moving with some difficulty, and then disappeared under the ground again. And so on, the runners kept seeing the Gopher at intervals, each time a little worse off than before, until they came to the last turning-place; and just as they reached it, almost in their midst appeared the most bedraggled and worn out of all the Gophers. They, seeing the red plume on his crest, almost obscured by mud and all flattened out, regarded him as surely the same old Gopher.
Finally, the original old Gopher, who had been quietly sleeping meanwhile, roused himself, and besoaking himself from the tip of his nose to the end of his short tail, wallowed about in the dirt until he was well plastered with mud, half closing his eyes, and crawled out before the astonished multitude at the end of the goal, a sorry-looking object indeed, far ahead of the runners, who were rapidly approaching.

===Chinese===

他们已经跑出去老远，忽然，在他们前面不远，跑道正中的地面上，嘣地钻出了老公鼠——看那样子正是他，红羽蒙了尘，却仍骄傲地在额头上飘着。他回头望了望赛跑者们，又一头扎进地里。希温纳和马察基的人高声喝彩。克亚基梅的选手们见囊鼠竟跑在他们前头，惊得加倍卖力。等他们逼近红贝坐歇处，看哪！又嘣地钻出一只老公鼠，眼圈鼻头都糊着泥——看那样子自然还是他。其实那是他的弟弟，红羽叫泥压得沉甸甸，却还在额头上飘着。
选手们拼命往前赶，刚一挨近 Kʻópakʻyan，就又看见囊鼠领先在他们前头，这回似乎浑身是汗——原来这个乖巧的弟弟早备下一点水，抹在毛皮上弄得满身泥泞，好像汗出如浆、已经开始乏了似的。他从洞里出来，钻进另一个洞时比前头几位慢了些；跟得不远的选手们大吼一声，加紧前冲。当他们自以为已经追近，看哪！在他们的道当中，浑身泥浆、看样子还是那只老公鼠又冒了出来，行动颇为吃力，随即又钻入地下。就这样，选手们隔一程就看见一次囊鼠，一次比一次狼狈，一直到了最后一个转折处；他们刚到那里，几乎就在他们当中，冒出了所有囊鼠里最泥泞、最精疲力竭的一只。他们看见他头顶的红羽——几乎被泥糊死、压得扁扁的——认定这必定就是那只老公鼠。
最后，那位本尊老公鼠——方才一直在安安稳稳睡觉——醒了，从鼻尖到短尾巴梢把自己泡了个透湿，在泥土里滚来滚去，滚得满身糊泥，眯缝着眼睛，在终点处惊愕的人群跟前爬了出来——好一副寒磣相，却远远抢在飞快逼近的选手们前头。

===Original===

A great shout was raised by those who were present, and the runners of Kʻiákime for the first time lost all of their winnings, and had the swiftness, or at least all their confidence, taken out of them, as doth the wind lose its swiftness when its legs are broken.
---
Thus it was in the days of the ancients. By the skill and cunning of the Gopher﻿—who, by digging his many holes and pitfalls, is the opponent of all runners, great and small﻿—was the race won against the swiftest runners among the youth of our ancients. Therefore, to this day the young runners of Zuni, on going forth to prepare for a race, take with them the sacred yellow pollen and red paint; and they make for the gophers, round about the racecourse in the country, beautiful little plumes, and they speak to them speeches in prayer, saying: “Behold, O ye Gophers of the plains and the trails, we race! And that we may have thy aid, we give ye these things, which are unto ye and your kind most precious, that ye will cause to fall into your holes and crannies and be hidden away in the dark and the dirt the sticks that are kicked by our opponents.”
Thus shortens my story.

===Chinese===

在场的人爆发出一片欢呼。克亚基梅的赛跑者们生平第一次输光了全部赢头，那股迅捷——至少是全部的自信——被生生夺走，就像风被折了腿，也就失了它的迅疾。
---
上古之时便是这样。凭着囊鼠的巧计与狡黠——他到处挖洞掘阱，是一切赛跑者不拘大小的对头——我们祖先的小伙子中最快的飞毛腿，输掉了那场比赛。所以直到今天，祖尼的年轻赛跑者出发备赛时，都要带上神圣的黄色花粉和红颜料；他们为赛跑道四乡的囊鼠们备下漂亮的小羽毛，向他们念祷告的话，说：“看哪，平原上和道旁的囊鼠们啊，我们要赛跑啦！愿得你们的助力，我们把这几样东西献给你们——对你们和你们的族类最金贵的东西——请你们叫我们对手踢出的木棒掉进你们的洞里隙里，藏进黑暗和泥土里去。”
我的故事就此收短。

## How the Rattlesnakes Came to Be What They Are / 响尾蛇怎样变成今天的模样
===Original===

Know you that long, long ago there lived at Yathlpewʻnan, as live there now, many Rattlesnakes; but then they were men and women, only of a Rattlesnake kind.
One day the little children of one of the houses there wished to go out to play at sliding down the sandbanks south of the Bitter Pond on the other side of our river. So they cried out to their parents: “Let us go, O mother, grandmother, father! and take our little sister to play on the sunny side of the sandbanks.”
“My children,” said the mother, “go if you wish, but be very careful of your little sister; for she is young. Carry her gently on your shoulders, and place her where she will be safe, for she is very small and helpless.”
“Oh, yes!” cried the children. “We love our little sister, don’t we, little one?” said they, turning to the baby girl. Then they took her up in their mantles, and carried her on their shoulders out to the sunny side of the sandbanks; and there they began to play at sliding one after another.
The little girl, immensely delighted with their sport, toddled out from the place where they had set her down, just as one of the girls was speeding down the side of the sand-hill. The little creature ran, clapping her hands and laughing, to catch her sister as she came; and the elder one, trying in vain to stop herself, called out to her to beware; but she was a little thing, and knew not the meaning of her sister’s warning; and, alas! the elder one slid down upon her, knocked her over and rolled her in the sand, crushing her so that she died, and rolling her out very small.
The children all gathered around their little sister, and cried and cried. Finally they took her up tenderly, and, placing her on their shoulders, sang as they went slowly toward home:

> “Tchi-tola tsaaana!

Tchi-tola tsaaana!

Tchi-tola tsaaana!
> Ama ma hama seta!

Ama ma hama seta!”

> Rattlesnake little-little!

Rattlesnake little-little!

Rattlesnake little-little!
> Alas, we bear her!

Alas, we bear her!

===Chinese===

你们可知很久很久以前，雅特普南（Yathlpewʻnan）住着许多响尾蛇（Rattlesnakes），如今那里也还住着响尾蛇；不过那时候他们是人，男人和女人，只是属于响尾蛇一族。
一天，那里一户人家的小孩子们，想到我们河对岸苦池（Bitter Pond）南边的沙堤上去玩滑沙。于是他们朝爹娘喊道：“让我们去吧，哎，妈妈、外婆、爸爸！带我们的小妹妹到沙堤向阳的那边去玩吧。”
“孩子们，”母亲说，“想去就去吧，可千万当心你们的小妹妹；她还小呢。把她轻轻驮在肩上，把她放在稳当的地方，她那么小，一点儿也经不起磕碰。”
“哦，知道啦！”孩子们喊。“我们都疼小妹妹，是不是呀，小不点儿？”他们转过脸对那女婴说。随后他们把她裹进披风，驮在肩上，来到沙堤向阳的那一边；到了那儿，便一个接一个玩起滑沙来。
小姑娘看他们玩得欢，乐不可支，就从他们放下她的地方摇摇摆摆走了出来，恰在此时，一个姐姐正从沙坡上飞滑而下。小家伙又是拍手又是笑，跑着去接滑下来的姐姐；那姐姐怎么也停不住，直喊她当心；可她是个小不点儿，听不懂姐姐警告的意思；唉！那姐姐一下子滑到她身上，把她撞翻，在沙里滚了个圈，把她压得咽了气，滚出来时她已小小的、扁扁的。
孩子们围着小妹妹，哭啊哭啊。最后他们把她轻轻抱起，驮在肩上，一边慢慢往家走，一边唱道：

> “Tchi-tola tsaaana!

Tchi-tola tsaaana!

Tchi-tola tsaaana!
> Ama ma hama seta!

Ama ma hama seta!”

> 响尾蛇小不点儿！

响尾蛇小不点儿！

响尾蛇小不点儿！
> 唉，我们驮着她！

唉，我们驮着她！

===Original===

As they approached the village of the Rattlesnakes, the mother of the little one looked out and saw them coming and heard their song.
“O, my children! my children!” she cried. “Ye foolish little ones, did I not tell ye to beware and to be careful, O, my children?” Then she exclaimed﻿—rocking herself to and fro, and wriggling from side to side at the same time, casting her hands into the air, and sobbing wildly﻿—

> “Ayaa mash toki!

Ayaa mash toki!

Hai! i i i i!”15

and fell in a swoon, still wriggling, to the ground.
When the old grandmother saw them coming, she too said:

> “Ayaa mash toki!

Ayaa mash toki!

Hai! i i i i!”

And as one after another in that village saw the little child, so beloved, brought home thus mutilated and dead, each cried out as the others had cried:

> “Ayaa mash toki!

Ayaa mash toki!

Hai! i i i i!”

and all swooned away; and the children also who were bringing the little one joined in the cry of woe, and swooned away. And when they all returned to life, behold, they could not arise, but went wriggling along the ground, faintly crying, as Rattlesnakes wriggle and cry to this day.
So you see that once﻿—as was the case with many, if not all, of the animals﻿—the Rattlesnakes were a people, and a splendid people too. Therefore we kill them not needlessly, nor waste the lives even of other animals without cause.
Thus shortens my story.

===Chinese===

他们走近响尾蛇的村庄时，小家伙的母亲望出来，看见他们来了，听见了他们的歌。
“哎，我的孩子们！我的孩子们！”她喊道。“你们这些傻乎乎的小家伙，我不是嘱咐过你们要当心、要留神吗，哎，我的孩子们？”随后她喊了起来——身子前后摇晃，左右扭动，双手抛向空中，泣不成声——

> “Ayaa mash toki!

Ayaa mash toki!

Hai! i i i i!”15

喊罢便昏厥过去，还在扭动着，瘫倒在地。
老祖母望见他们来时，也喊道：

> “Ayaa mash toki!

Ayaa mash toki!

Hai! i i i i!”

村里的人一个接一个望见那深受疼爱的小孩这般残损死去地被驮回家来，个个都像旁人一样哭喊：

> “Ayaa mash toki!

Ayaa mash toki!

Hai! i i i i!”

全都昏死过去；驮着小家伙的孩子们也加入这场恸哭，也昏死过去。等他们全都苏醒过来，看哪，他们再也站不起来了，只能贴着地面蜿蜒爬行，微弱地哀鸣，正如响尾蛇到今天还那样爬行、那样哀鸣。
所以你们看，响尾蛇——如同许多（即便不是所有）动物的情形一样——曾经是一个族群，而且是个出色的族群。因此我们不无故杀害他们，也不无缘无故糟蹋哪怕其他动物的性命。
我的故事，就此收梢。

## How the Summer Birds Came / 夏鸟是怎样来到的
===Original===

In the days of the ancients, in the town under Thunder Mountain called Kʻiákime, there lived a most beautiful maiden. But one thing which struck the people who knew her was that she seldom came forth from her room, or went out of her house; never seemed to care for the people around her, never seemed to care to see the young men when they were dancing.
Now, this was the way of it. Through the roof of her room was a little skylight, open, and when it rained, one of the Gods of the Rain descended in the raindrops and wooed this maiden, and married her all unknown to her people; so that she was in his company every time it rained, and when the dew fell at night, on his ladder of water descending he came, and she was very happy, and cared not for the society of men. By-and-by, behold! to the utter surprise of the people, whose eyes could not see this god, her husband, there was a little boy born to her.
Now, he was the child of the gods, and, therefore, before he was many days old, he had begun to run about and speak, and had wonderful intelligence and wonderful strength and vivacity. He was only a month or two old when he was like a child of five or six or eight years of age, and he would climb to the housetop and run down into the plaza and out around the village hunting birds or other small animals. With only his fingers and little stones for weapons, he never failed to slay and bring home these little creatures, and his mother’s house was supplied more than any other house in the town with plumes for sacrifice, from the birds which he captured in this way.
Finally he observed that the older men of the tribe carried bows and arrows, and that the arrows went more swiftly and straighter than the stones he threw; and though he never failed to kill small animals, he found he could not kill the larger ones in that way. So he said to his mother one night: “Oh, mother, where does the wood grow that they make bows of, and where do they get sticks for their arrows? I wish you would tell me.”
But the mother was quite silent; she didn’t like to tell him, for she thought it would lead him away from the town and something would happen to him.

===Chinese===

先民时代，在雷霆山下名叫基亚基梅（Kʻiákime）的镇子里，住着一位极其美丽的少女。但认识她的人有一点想不通：她很少走出自己的房间，也很少出门；似乎从不把周围的人放在心上，年轻人跳舞时，她似乎也从不想去看。
事情原来是这样。她房顶上开着一扇小小的天窗，敞着；每当下雨，一位雨神（Gods of the Rain）便随雨点降下，向这位少女求爱，瞒着她的族人娶了她；于是每逢下雨，她都与他为伴；夜里露水降下时，他踏着水做的梯子降临。她非常快活，也就无意与世人为伍了。不久，看哪！族人们的眼睛看不见她那位神明丈夫，却个个惊愕地发现，她生下了一个小男孩。
这孩子是神明之子，因此生下来没几天，就满地跑了，会说话了，聪明得出奇，力气、活力也出奇。才一两个月大，他就跟五六岁、八岁的孩子一样了，能爬上房顶，跑下广场，绕着村子转，捉鸟或别的小动物。只用手指和小石子当武器，他从不空手，总能杀死这些小生灵带回家来；靠他这样捕来的鸟，他母亲的家里供祭祀用的羽毛，比镇上任何一户都多。
后来他留意到族里的成年男人都带着弓箭，箭飞得比他扔的石头更快更直；虽然他杀小动物从不失手，却发现那样杀不了大兽。于是一天夜里他对母亲说：“哎，妈妈，做弓的木头长在哪儿？箭杆又是从哪儿弄来的？你告诉我吧。”
可母亲一声不吭；她不想告诉他，怕这会把他引出镇子，招来祸事。

===Original===

But he kept questioning her until at last, weary with his importunities, she said: “Well, my little boy, if you go round the cliff here to the eastern side, there is a great hollow in the rocks, and down at the bottom of that hollow is a great cave. Now, around that shelter in the rocks are growing the trees out of which bows are made, and there also grow the bushes from which arrows are cut; they are so plentiful that they could supply the whole town, and furnish all the hunters here with bows and arrows; but they cannot get them, because in the cave lives a great Bear, a very savage being, and no one dares go near there to get timber for the bows or sticks for the arrows, because the Bear would surely devour whoever ventured there. He has devoured many of our people; therefore you must not go there to get these arrows.”
“No, indeed,” said the boy. But at night he lay down with much in his mind, and was so thoughtful that he hardly slept the whole night. He was planning what he would do in the morning.
The next morning his mother was busy about her work, and finally she went down to the spring for some water, and the little boy slipped out of the house, ran down the ladder, went to the riverside, stooped down, and crawled along the bank of the river, until he could get around on the side of the cliff where the little valley of the spring that flows under Thunder Mountain lies. There he climbed up and up until he came to the shelter in the rocks round on the eastern side of Thunder Mountain. The mouth of this hollow was entirely closed with fine yellowwood and oak, the best timber we have for bows, and straight sprouts were growing everywhere out of which arrows could be made.
“Ah, this must be the place,” said the boy, as he looked at it. “I don’t see any Bear. I think I will climb up and see if there is anything to be afraid of, and try if I can cut a stick before the Bear comes out.”
He started and climbed into the mouth of the cavern, and his father, one of the Gods of the Rain, threw a tremendous shaft of lightning, and it thundered, and the cave closed together.
“Ha!” cried the boy. “What in the world is the meaning of this?” Then he stood there a moment, and presently the clouds finished and the cave opened, and all was quiet. He started to go in once more, and down came the lightning again, to remind him that he should not go in there.
“Ha!”

===Chinese===

可他问个不停，母亲拗不过他的纠缠，终于说：“好吧，我的孩子，你要是绕过这里的崖壁到东边去，岩石间有一大块洼地，洼地底下有一个大山洞。那处岩石掩蔽的四周，长着做弓的树，还有削箭杆的灌木；多得足以供全镇使用，够这里所有猎人取弓取箭；可谁也拿不到，因为洞里住着一头大熊（Bear），一个非常凶恶的家伙；谁也不敢到跟前去取弓材和箭杆，因为那熊准会把闯去的人吞掉。他已吞了我们好些族人；所以你千万别到那儿去弄箭。”
“不去，真不去，”男孩说。可夜里他躺下后心事重重，翻来覆去几乎一夜没合眼。他在盘算明早要干什么。
第二天一早，母亲忙着家务，后来又下泉边打水去了，小男孩便溜出家门，跑下梯子，到河边猫下腰，沿着河岸悄悄爬行，一直绕到崖那一侧——雷霆山下泉水流经的小谷就在那里。他从那里爬呀爬呀，爬到了雷霆山东侧的岩石掩蔽处。那洼地的洞口被极好的黄杨木和橡木封得严严实实——那是我们做弓最好的木料——到处长着笔直的嫩条，都可以削箭。
“啊，一定是这儿了，”男孩望着说。“我看不到什么熊。我看我爬上去瞧瞧有没有什么可怕的，试试能不能赶在熊出来之前砍一根枝条。”
他动身往洞口里爬，他的父亲——一位雨神——抛下一道惊人的闪电，雷声轰鸣，洞口合拢了。
“哈！”男孩喊。“这到底是什么意思？”他站了一会儿，不久云散了，洞口又开了，四下安静。他正要再进去，闪电又劈下来，提醒他不该进去。
“哈！”

===Original===

cried the boy again. “What in the world does it mean?” And he rubbed his eyes﻿—it had rather stunned him﻿—and so soon as it had cleared away he tried again, and again for the fourth time.
Finally the god said, “Ah! I have reminded him and he does not heed. He must go his own way.” So the boy climbed into the cave.
No sooner had he got in than it began to get dark, and *Wah!* came the Bear on his hind legs and grabbed the boy and began to squeeze him very tight.
“O my! O my!” cried he. “Don’t squeeze me so hard! It hurts; don’t squeeze me so hard! My mother is one of the most beautiful women you ever saw!”
“Hollo!” exclaimed the Bear. “What is that you say?”
“My mother is one of the most beautiful women you ever saw!”
“Indeed!” said the Bear, as he relaxed his hold. “My son, sit down. What did you come to my house for? I am sure you are very welcome.”
“Why,” said the boy, “I came to get a piece of wood for a bow and sticks for arrows.”
Said the Bear, “I have looked out for this timber for a long time. There is none better in the whole country. Let me tell you what I will do. You don’t look very strong. You haven’t anything to cut the trees down with. I will go myself and cut down a tree for you. I will pick out a good one for a bow; not only that, but I will get fine sticks for arrows, too.”
So he stalked off into the forest, and crack, crack, he smashed the trees down, and, picking out a good one, gnawed off the ends of it and brought it to the boy, then gathered a lot of fine straight sticks for arrow-shafts and brought them.
“There,” said he, “take those home. Do you know how to make a bow, my son?”
“No, I don’t very well,” replied he.
“Well,” said the Bear, “I have cut off the ends; make it about that length. Now take it home, and shave down the inside until it is thin enough to bend quickly at both ends, and lay it over the coals of fire so it will get hard and dry. That is the way to make a good bow.”
“All right,” said the boy; and as he took up the bundle of sticks and the stave for the bow, he said: “Just come along toward night and I will introduce you to my mother.”
“All right,” said the old Bear; “I will be along just about sunset. Then I can look at your bow and see whether you have made it well or not.”

===Chinese===

男孩又喊。“这到底是什么意思？”他揉揉眼睛——那雷把他震得不轻——天一放晴他又试，又试，直到第四次。
末了那位神明说：“唉！我提醒了他，他不理会。那就随他去吧。”于是男孩爬进了洞。
他刚进去，洞里就暗了下来，哇呀！熊用后腿立着扑上来，一把抓住男孩，使劲地勒。
“哎哟！哎哟！”他喊。“别勒这么紧！疼死了；别勒这么紧！我妈妈可是您这辈子见过顶美的女人！”
“哧！”熊喊。“你说什么？”
“我妈妈是您这辈子见过顶美的女人！”
“当真！”熊说着松了劲。“我的孩子，坐下。你上我家来有什么事？你来了真是太欢迎了。”
“是这样，”男孩说，“我来取一段做弓的木头和几根做箭的枝条。”
熊说：“这片林子我看了很久了。全国再没有比这更好的木料。这么办吧。你瞧着也不怎么壮实，又没有家伙砍树。我亲自去给你砍一棵。我挑一棵好弓料；不光如此，我再给你捋些上好的直枝做箭杆。”
于是他大步走进林子，咔嚓，咔嚓，把树扑倒，挑出一棵好的，把两端咬掉，衜给男孩；又拾了一大把上好的直杆做箭，也送了来。
“拿着，”他说，“把这些带回家。你会做弓吗，我的孩子？”
“不太会，”他答。
“这么着，”熊说，“两头我已经咬掉了；就做这么长。带回家去，把里侧削薄，削到两端都能很快弯过来的程度，再放到炭火上架着，让它又硬又干。这样就是一把好弓。”
“好，”男孩说；他抱起那捆枝条和弓料，说：“天擦黑的时候您来一趟吧，我把您引见给我母亲。”
“好，”老熊说；“我日落时分准到。也好看看你的弓，瞧你做得好不好。”

===Original===

So the boy trudged home with his bundle of sticks and his bow stave, and when he arrived there his mother happened to be climbing out, and saw him coming.
“You wretched boy,” she said, “I told you not to go out to the cave! I warrant you have been there where the Bear stays!”
“Oh, yes, my mother; just see what I have brought,” said the boy. “I sold you to the Bear. He will be here to get you this evening. See what I have brought!” and he laid out his bow-timber and arrow-shafts.
“Oh,” said she, “you are the most wretched and foolish of little boys; you pay no attention to what anyone says to you; your mother’s word is nothing but wind in your ears.”
“Just see what I have brought home,” said he. He worked as hard as he could to make his bow, stripped the arrow-shafts, smoothed and straightened them before the fire, and made the points of obsidian﻿—very black it is; very hard and sharp were the points when he placed them on the arrows. Now, after placing the feathers on the arrows, he stood them up on the roof of the house against the parapet in the sunlight to dry; and he had his bow on the other side of the house against the other parapet to dry. He was still at work, toward sunset, when he happened to look up and saw the Bear coming along, slowly, comfortably, rolling over the sand.
“Ah!” said he, “the old man is coming.” He paid no attention to him, however.
Presently the Bear came close to the ladder, and shook it to see if it was strong enough to hold him.
“Thou comest?” asked the boy.
“Yes,” said the Bear. “How have you been all day?”
“Happy,” said the boy.
“How is your mother?”
“Happy,” said the boy, “expecting you.”
So the old Bear climbed up. “Ah, indeed,” said he, as he got over the edge of the house, “have you made the bow?”
“Yes, after a fashion.”
So the Bear went over, raised himself on his hind feet, looked at the bow, pulled it, and said, as he laid it down: “It is a splendid bow. What is this black stuff on these arrows?”
“Obsidian,” answered the boy.
“These points are nothing but black coals,” said the Bear.
“I tell you,” said the boy, “they are good, black, flint arrowheads, hard and sharp as any others.”
“No,” said the other, “nothing but coals.”
“Now, suppose you let me try one of those coals on you,” said the boy.
“All right,” said the Bear.

===Chinese===

于是男孩背着枝条和弓料，一路回了家。到家时他母亲恰好在往外爬，望见了他。
“你这个不争气的孩子，”她说，“我嘱咐过你别去那山洞！我料定你去熊那儿了！”
“可不是嘛，妈妈；瞧我带回来什么了，”男孩说。“我把您卖给熊了。他今晚就来领您。瞧我带回来什么！”他摆出弓料和箭杆。
“唉，”她说，“你是天底下最混账、最傻的孩子；谁的话你都不听；你娘的话在你耳朵里就是一阵风。”
“瞧我带回家什么了，”他说。他拼命赶做他的弓，剥好箭杆，在火前又削又校直，又用黑曜岩磨制箭头——黑得发亮；装上箭时，箭头又硬又尖。他把羽毛安上箭，一排排立在屋顶女墙边晒着；弓靠在屋子另一侧的另一道女墙上晾着。将近日落时他还在忙，一抬头，正好望见熊来了，慢悠悠、舒舒服服，一路在沙里打着滚。
“啊！”他说，“老头儿来了。”可他没理会。
不一会儿，熊走近梯子，摇了摇，看禁得住禁不住他。
“您来了？”男孩问。
“来了，”熊说。“今天过得怎么样？”
“快活，”男孩说。
“你母亲呢？”
“快活，”男孩说，“就等您来呢。”
于是老熊爬了上来。“啊，当真，”他翻上房沿时说，“弓做好了吗？”
“凑合做好了。”
于是熊走过去，后腿立起，端详那张弓，拉了拉，放下时说：“好一张出色的弓。这些箭上黑乎乎的是什么？”
“黑曜岩，”男孩答。
“这些箭头不过是些黑炭，”熊说。
“我跟您说，”男孩说，“这是上好的黑燧石箭头，跟谁家的比都又硬又尖。”
“不，”对方说，“不过是炭。”
“那这么着，您让我拿一块炭在您身上试试，”男孩说。
“好啊，”熊说。

===Original===

He walked over to the other side of the roof and stood there, and the boy took one of the arrows, fitted it to the bow, and let go. It went straight into the heart of the Bear, and even passed through him entirely.
“*Wah!*” uttered the Bear, as he gave a great snort and rolled over on the housetop and died.
“*Ha, ha!*” shouted the boy, “what you had intended to do unto me, thus unto you! Oh, mother!” called he, as he ran to the sky-hole, “here is your husband; come and see him. I have killed him; but, then, he would have me make the experiment,” said the boy.
“Oh, you foolish, foolish, disobedient boy!” said the mother. “What have you been doing now? Are we safe?”
“Oh, yes,” said he; “my stepfather is as passive as if he were asleep.” And he went on and skinned his once prospective stepfather, and then took out his heart and hung it to the crosspiece of the ladder as a sign that the people could go and get all the bow-timber and arrows they pleased.
That night, after the evening meal was over, the boy sat down with his mother, and he said: “By the way, mother, are there any monsters or fearful creatures anywhere round about this country that kill people and make trouble?”
“No,” said the mother, “none whatever.”
“I don’t know about that; I think there must be,” said the boy.
“No, there are none whatever, I tell you,” answered the mother.
The boy began to tumble on the floor, rolling about, playing with his mother’s blankets, and throwing things around, and once in a while he would ask her again the same question, until finally she got very cross with him and said: “Yes, if you want to know, down there in the valley, beyond the great plains of sagebrush, is a den of *Misho* Lizards who are fearful and deadly to everyone who goes near them. Therefore you had better be careful how you run round the valley.”
“What makes them so fearful?” asked he.
“Well,” said she, “they are venomous; they have a way of throwing from their mouths or breath a sort of fluid which, whenever it strikes a person, burns him, and whenever it strikes the eyes it blinds them. A great many people have perished there. Whenever a man arrives at their den they are very polite and greet him most courteously; they say: ‘Come in; sit down right here in the middle of the floor before the fire.’

===Chinese===

他踱到屋顶另一头站定，男孩抽出一支箭，搭上弓，撒手放弦。箭正中熊的心口，竟把他整个穿透了。
“哇呀！”熊低吼一声，在房顶上打个滚，死了。
“哈，哈！”男孩喊，“你想加害我的，就还到你身上！哎，妈妈！”他跑到天窗口喊，“您丈夫在这儿；快来看他。我把他杀了；是他自己要我试这一箭的，”男孩说。
“唉，你这又傻又傻、不听话的孩子！”母亲说。“你又干了什么？我们还有活路吗？”
“有，放心，”他说；“我继父安安静静，跟睡着了似的。”他接着动手剥这位差点当上继父的皮，又掏出他的心，挂在梯子的横档上，作为标记，让人们知道可以去随意取用那里的弓材和箭杆了。
那天晚上，晚饭过后，男孩挨着母亲坐下，说：“对了，妈妈，这一带还有没有别的妖魔、吓人的东西，害人捣乱的？”
“没有，”母亲说，“一个也没有。”
“这我可不信；我觉得准有，”男孩说。
“没有，一个也没有，我告诉你，”母亲答。
男孩开始在地板上打滚，翻来滚去，扯他母亲的毯子玩，把东西乱扔，时不时又问一遍同样的话；末了母亲被他磨得动了气，说：“好吧，你要知道就告诉你：顺着谷地往下，越过大片鼠尾草原，有一窝米肖蜥蜴（Misho），谁走近谁遭殃，又毒又凶。所以你到谷里乱跑，最好当心点。”
“他们怎么这么吓人？”他问。
“唔，”她说，“他们有毒；他们能从嘴里、从呼吸里喷出一种液，沾到人身上就烧人，沾到眼睛就弄瞎眼睛。好多人死在那儿。人一到他们窝里，他们客客气气，恭恭敬敬地招呼，说：‘请进；就在火边屋子正当中坐下吧。’

===Original===

But as soon as the person is seated in their house they gather round the walls and throw this venom on him, and he dies almost immediately.”
“Is it possible?” responded the little boy; and for some reason or other he began to grow sleepy, and said: “Now, let us go to sleep, mother.”
So he lay down and slept. Just as soon as it was light the next morning he aroused himself, dressed, took his bow and arrows, and, placing them in a corner near the ladder, said: “Oh, mother, give me my breakfast; I want to go and shoot some little birds. I would like to have some roasted birds for dinner.”
She gave him his breakfast as quickly as she could, and he ran down the ladder and went to shooting at the birds, until he happened to see that his mother and others were out of sight; then he skulked into the sagebrush and went as straight as he could for the den of the *Misho* Lizards. There happened to be two young ones sunning themselves outside, and they said:
“Ah, my fine little fellow, glad to see you this morning. Come in, come in; the old ones will be very much pleased to entertain you. Come in!”
“Thank you,” said the boy. He walked in, but he felt under his coat to see if a huge lump of rock salt he had was still there.
“Sit right down here,” said the old people. The whole den was filled with these *Misho* Lizards, and they were excessively polite, everyone of them.
The boy sat down, and the old *Misho* said to the young ones: “Hurry up, now; be quick!” And they began to throw their venom at him, and continued until he was all covered with it; but, knowing beforehand, and being the child of the gods, he was prepared and protected, and it did him no harm.
“Thank you, thank you,” said the boy. “I will do the same thing.” Then he pulled out the salt and pushed it down into the fire, where it exploded and entirely used up the whole council of *Misho* Lizards.
“There!” cried the boy. “Thus would you have done unto me, thus unto you.”
He took two fine ones and cut out their hearts, then started for home. When he arrived there, he climbed the ladder and suspended the two hearts beside that of the Bear and went down into the house, saying, “Well, mother, is dinner ready?”
“There now,” said she, “I know it. I saw you hang those hearts up. You have been down there.”
“Yes,” said he, “they are all gone﻿—every solitary one of them.”
“Oh, you foolish, foolish, disobedient fellow!

===Chinese===

可人一在他们屋里坐下，他们就贴着四壁围拢来，把毒液泼到他身上，人几乎当场就死。”
“竟有这样的事！”小男孩应道；不知怎么他犯起困来，说：“好了，妈妈，咱们睡吧。”
他躺下睡了。第二天天刚亮他就爬起来，穿好衣裳，取了弓箭，把它们靠在梯子旁的屋角，说：“哎，妈妈，给我早饭；我要去打小鸟。我想午饭吃烤鸟。”
母亲赶紧给他备了早饭，他跑下梯子射鸟去了，射着射着，见母亲和别人都瞧不见了，便溜进鼠尾草丛，直奔米肖蜥蜴的窝。窝外恰有两只小的在晒太阳，他们说：
“啊，漂亮的小家伙，今儿早上见着你真高兴。进来，进来；老人们一定乐意款待你。进来吧！”
“谢谢，”男孩说。他走了进去，手却伸到外衣底下摸了摸，看他带的那大块岩盐还在不在。
“就坐在这儿吧，”老人们说。整个窝里挤满了米肖蜥蜴，一个个客气得了不得。
男孩坐下，老米肖对小家伙们说：“快点，快！”他们便朝他喷毒，喷了一层又一层，把他整个人都糊满了；可他早有防备，又是神明之子，有护佑，毒伤不了他一根毫毛。
“谢谢，谢谢，”男孩说。“我也照样子回敬回敬。”说着他掏出那块盐，塞进火里；盐轰然炸响，把满窝米肖蜥蜴炸了个精光。
“瞧！”男孩喊。“你们想这样对付我，就还给你们。”
他挑了两只肥美的，剜出心，动身回家。到家后爬上梯子，把两颗心挂在熊心旁边，然后下屋问道：“哎，妈妈，饭好了吗？”
“你瞧，”她说，“我就知道。我看见你挂那些心了。你下那儿去了。”
“去了，”他说，“他们全完了——一只不剩。”
“唉，你这又傻又傻、不听话的东西！

===Original===

I am all alone in the world, and if you should go to some of those fearful places some time and not come back, who would hunt for me? What should I do?” said the mother.
“Don’t be troubled, mother, now,” said the boy. “I don’t think I will go any more. There is nothing else of that kind around, is there, mother?”
“No, there is not,” she replied; “not a thing. There may be somewhere in the world, but there is not anywhere here.”
In the evening, as he sat with his mother, the boy kept questioning and teasing her to tell him of some other monsters﻿—pulling on her skirts and repeating his questions.
“I tell you,” she said, “there are no such creatures.”
“Oh, mother, I know there are,” said he, “and you must tell me about them.”
So he continued to bother her until her patience gave out, and she told him of another monster. Said she: “If you follow that canyon down to the southeast, there is a very, very, very high cliff there, and the trail that goes over that cliff runs close by the side of a precipice.
