True, I have said ‘Be it well’; but before I take your bundle and say ‘thanks,’ I would that you, to prove that you verily love me, should go down into my cornfield, among the lands of the priest-chief, by the side of the river, and hoe all the corn in a single morning. If you will do this, then shall I know you love me; then shall I take of your presents, and happy we will be together.”
“Very well,” replied the young man; “I am willing.”
Then the young girl lighted a bundle of cedar splints and showed him a room which contained a bed of soft robes and blankets, and, placing her father’s hoe near the door, bade the young man “wait happily unto the morning.”
So when she had gone he looked at the hoe and thought: “Ha! if that be all, she shall see in the morning that I am a man.”
At the peep of day over the eastern mesa he roused himself, and, shouldering the wooden hoe, ran down to the cornfields; and when, as the sun was coming out, the young girl awoke and looked down from her housetop, “Aha!” thought she, “he is doing well, but my children and I shall see how he gets on somewhat later. I doubt if he loves me as much as he thinks he does.”
So she went into a closed room. Down in the corner stood a water jar, beautifully painted and as bright as new. It looked like other water jars, but it was not. It was wonderful, wonderful! for it was covered with a stone lid which held down many mayflies and gnats and mosquitoes. The maiden lifted the lid and began to speak to the little animals as though she were praying.
“Now, then, my children, this day fly ye forth all, and in the cornfields by the river there shall ye see a young man hoeing. So hard is he working that he is stripped as for a race. Go forth and seek him.”
“*Tsu‑nu‑nu‑nu*,” said the flies, and “*Tsi‑ni‑ni‑ni*,” sang the gnats and mosquitoes; which meant “Yes,” you know.
“And,” further said the girl, “when ye find him, bite him, his body all over, and eat ye freely of his blood; spare not his armpits, neither his neck nor his eyelids, and fill his ears with humming.”
And again the flies said, “*Tsu‑nu‑nu‑nu*,” and the mosquitoes and gnats, “*Tsi‑ni‑ni‑ni.*” Then, *nu‑u‑u*, away they all flew like a cloud of sand on a windy morning.
“Blood!” exclaimed the young man.

===Chinese===

于是等老人们在墙角睡下，姑娘才低声对年轻人说：“你兴许是爱我的。不错，我说了‘就这么办，好’；可在我收下你的包袱、说‘谢谢’之前，我要你证明你当真爱我：下到我那片玉米田去——就在河边、祭司长的地界里——用一个早晨，把田里的玉米全锄完。你若办得到，我便知道你爱我；那时我就收你的聘礼，咱们欢欢喜喜在一起。”
“很好，”年轻人答，“我愿意。”
于是姑娘点起一束雪松松明，领他看了一间屋子，屋里铺着软皮毛和毯子搭的铺；她把父亲的锄头靠在门边，祝年轻人“安然到早晨”。
她走后，他看着那把锄头想：“哈！要是就这点事，明早她就知道我是个男子汉了。”
东边台地上天刚泛亮，他就起身，扛起木锄，跑下玉米田去。日头初升、姑娘醒来从屋顶往下望时，她想：“啊哈！他干得不坏，可我和我的孩子们倒要看看他过一会儿怎么样。我怀疑他爱我不如他自己以为的那么深。”
于是她走进一间密室。屋角立着一只水瓮，绘得漂亮，亮得像新的一样。它看着同别的水瓮一样，其实不然。它奇妙极了，奇妙极了！因为它盖着一块石头盖子，底下压着许多蜉蝣、蠓虫和蚊子。少女揭开盖子，像祷告似的对小虫子们说起话来。
“那么，我的孩子们，今天你们全体飞出去吧；河边玉米田里，你们会看见一个年轻人在锄地。他干得那么卖力，脱得像要赛跑一样。去吧，找他去。”
“Tsu‑nu‑nu‑nu，”苍蝇们说；“Tsi‑ni‑ni‑ni，”蠓虫蚊子们唱道——你知道，那意思是“是”。
“还有，”姑娘接着说，“你们找到他，就咬他，周身都咬，放开肚子吃他的血；腋窝不许放过，脖颈、眼皮也不许放过，再往他耳朵里灌满嗡嗡声。”
苍蝇们又说“Tsu‑nu‑nu‑nu”，蚊蠓们“Tsi‑ni‑ni‑ni”。随后，nu‑u‑u——，它们一齐飞了出去，像起风早晨的一团沙云。
“血！”年轻人叫起来。

===Original===

He wiped the sweat from his face and said, “The gods be angry!” Then he dropped his hoe and rubbed his shins with sand and slapped his sides. “*Atu!*” he yelled; “what matters﻿—what in the name of the Moon Mother matters with these little beasts that cause thoughts?” Whereupon, crazed and restless as a spider on hot ashes, he rolled in the dust, but to no purpose, for the flies and gnats and mosquitoes sang “*hu‑n‑n*” and “*tsi‑ni‑ni*” about his ears until he grabbed up his blanket and breakfast, and ran toward the home of his fathers.
“*Wa‑ha ha! Ho o!*” laughed a young man in the Tented Pueblo to the north, when he heard how the lover had fared. “*Shoom!*” he sneered. “Much of a man he must have been to give up the maid of Mátsaki for mayflies and gnats and mosquitoes!” So on the very next morning, he, too, said to his old ones: “What a fool that little *boy* must have been. I will visit the maiden of Mátsaki. I’ll show the people of Pínawa what a Hámpasawan man can do. Courage!”﻿—and, as the old ones said “Be it well,” he went as the other had gone; but, pshaw! he fared no better.
After some time, a young man who lived in the River Town heard about it and laughed as hard as the youth of the Tented Pueblo had. He called the two others fools, and said that “girls were not in the habit of asking much when one’s bundle was large.” And as he was a young man who had everything, he made a bundle of presents as large as he could carry; but it did him no good. He, too, ran away from the mayflies and gnats and mosquitoes.
Many days passed before anyone else would try again to woo the maiden of Mátsaki. They did not know, it is true, that she was a Passing Being; but others had failed all on account of mosquitoes and mayflies and little black gnats, and had been more satisfied with shame than a full hungry man with food. “That is sick satisfaction,” they would say to one another, the fear of which made them wait to see what others would do.
Now, in the Ant Hill, which was named Hálonawan,1 lived a handsome young man, but he was poor, although the son of the priest-chief of Hálonawan. He thought many days, and at last said to his grandmother, who was very old and crafty, “*Hó-ta?*”
“What sayest my *nána*?” said the old woman; for, like grandmothers nowadays, she was very soft and gentle to her grandson.

===Chinese===

他抹了把脸上的汗，说：“神明动怒了！”随即丢了锄头，拿沙子搓小腿，拍打两肋。“Atu!”他吼道；“这些叫人心烦意乱的小畜生算怎么回事——看在月亮老娘分上，算怎么回事？”于是他像热灰上的蜘蛛一样又疯又躁，在尘土里打滚，全不管用；苍蝇蠓虫蚊子围着他耳朵唱“hu‑n‑n”和“tsi‑ni‑ni”，直到他抓起毯子和早饭，朝着父辈的家奔去。
“Wa‑ha ha! Ho o!”北边帐篷镇（Tented Pueblo）里一个年轻人听说这位求亲者的遭遇，大笑道。“Shoom!”他嗤道。“为了蜉蝣蠓虫蚊子就丢下马察基的姑娘，他还算个男人！”于是第二天一早，他也对他的长辈们说：“那小‘子’准是个蠢货。我要去拜访马察基的少女。我要让皮纳瓦人瞧瞧汉帕萨万（Hámpasawan）男儿能干什么。胆气来！”——长辈们说了“就这么办，好”，他便照前一位那样去了；可是，呸！他的下场也不见得好。
过了些时日，住在河镇（River Town）的一个年轻人听说了这事，笑得同帐篷镇那位一样响。他骂那两位是蠢货，说“包袱大的人家，姑娘向来不会多要求什么”。他是样样不缺的年轻人，便打点了扛得动的最大一包聘礼；可这对他毫无用处。他也被蜉蝣蠓虫蚊子赶跑了。
许多天过去，再没有人敢去向马察基少女求婚。他们固然不知道她是一位超凡的生灵；可前面那些人全都栽在蚊子、蜉蝣和小黑蠓上，落得一身臊回去——那份“受用”，比饿汉吃上一顿饱饭还足。“那种受用，恶心得很。”他们彼此这样说；这层畏惧叫他们只敢等看别人的动静。
且说在名叫哈洛纳万（Hálonawan）1的蚁丘镇上，住着一位英俊的年轻人；他虽是哈洛纳万祭司长的儿子，却很穷。他思量了许多天，最后对他那年迈而乖巧的祖母说：“Hó-ta?（怎么说？）”
“我的 nána（孙儿）说什么？”老妇人问；因为同如今的祖母们一样，她对孙儿是顶温柔和气的。

===Original===

“I have seen the maiden of Mátsaki and my thoughts kill me with longing, for she is passing beautiful and wisely slow. I do not wonder that she asks hard tasks of her lovers; for it is not of their bundles that she thinks, but of themselves. Now, I strengthen my thoughts with my manliness. My heart is hard against weariness, and I would go and speak to the beautiful maiden.”
“*Yo á!* my poor boy,” said the grandmother. “She is as wonderful as she is wise and beautiful. She thinks not of men save as brothers and friends; and she it is, I bethink me, who sends the mayflies and gnats and mosquitoes, therefore, to drive them away. They are but disguised beings, and beware, my grandson, you will only cover yourself with shame as a man is covered with water who walks through a rainstorm! I would not go, my poor grandchild. I would not go,” she added, shaking her head and biting her lips till her chin touched her nose-tip.
“Yes, but I must go, my grandmother. Why should I live only to breathe hard with longing? Perhaps she will better her thoughts toward me.”
“Ah, yes, but all the same, she will test thee. Well, go to the mountains and scrape bitter bark from the finger-root; make a little loaf of the bark and hide it in your belt, and when the maiden sends you down to the cornfield, work hard at the hoeing until sunrise. Then, when your body is covered with sweat-drops, rub every part with the root-bark. The finger-root bark, it is bitter as bad salt mixed in with bad water, and the ‘horn-wings’ and ‘long-beaks’ and ‘blue-backs’ fly far from the salt that is bitter.”
“Then, my gentle grandmother, I will try your words and thank you,”﻿—for he was as gentle and good as his grandmother was knowing and crafty. Even that day he went to the mountains and gathered a ball of finger-root. Then, toward evening, he took a little bundle and went up the trail by the riverside to Mátsaki. When he climbed the ladder and shouted down the mat door: “*Shé!* Are ye within?” the people did not answer at once, for the old ones were angry with their daughter that she had sent off so many fine lovers. But when he shouted again they answered:
“*Hai*, and *Ée*, we are within. Be yourself within.”
Then without help he went down the ladder, but he didn’t mind, for he felt himself poor and his bundle was small.

===Chinese===

“我见过马察基的少女了，我的思念熬得我要死，因为她美得超凡，又端庄矜持。她向求亲者出难题，我一点也不奇怪；因为她想的不是他们的包袱，而是他们本人。如今我以我的男子气概坚强我的意志。我的心不怕劳累，我要去同那美丽的少女说话。”
“Yo á! 我苦命的孩子，”祖母说。“她又奇妙，又聪明，又美丽。她把男人只当兄弟朋友看待；我想起来，那放蜉蝣蠓虫蚊子的正是她，为的是把这些人赶走。它们不过是些化了装的小生灵。当心啊，我的孙儿，你只会像冒雨赶路的人浇一身水那样，浇一身臊！我是不去的，我苦命的孙儿。我是不去的。”她补一句，连连摇头，咬着嘴唇，下巴都快碰到鼻尖了。
“是，可我非去不可，祖母。我何必活着只为渴望得喘不上气？兴许她会对我回心转意呢。”
“啊，是呀，可她照样要试你。这样吧：上山去，刮些指根草的苦皮回来；拿树皮捏一个小饼，藏在你腰带里。等少女打发你下玉米田，赶在日出前拼命锄。锄到浑身是汗珠，就拿指根草皮把周身每一处都擦到。指根草的皮，苦得像坏水掺了坏盐；‘角翅儿’‘长喙儿’‘蓝背儿’们闻到苦盐味，飞得远远的。”
“那么，我好脾气的祖母，我就照您的话试试，谢谢您。”——因为他同祖母的足智多谋一样，温良敦厚。当天他就上了山，采回一团指根草。傍晚时分，他带了小小一个包袱，沿河边小道上去，到了马察基。他攀上梯子，朝席门下喊：“Shé! 家里有人吗？”人们没有立刻应声，因为老人们正生女儿的气——她气跑了那么多好求亲者。可他又喊了一声，他们便答了：
“Hai，Ée，我们在家。你也请进来吧。”
于是他不用人扶，自己下了梯子；他并不在意，因为他自觉穷，包袱也小。

===Original===

As he entered the firelight he greeted the people pleasantly and gravely, and with thanks took the seat that was laid for him.
Now, you see, the old man was angry with the girl, so he did not tell her to place cooked things before him, but turned to his old wife.
“Old one,” he began﻿—but before he had finished the maiden arose and brought rich venison stew and flaky *héwe*, which she placed before the youth where the fire’s brightness would fall upon it, with meat broth for drink; then she sat down opposite him and said, “Eat and drink!” Whereupon the young man took a roll of the wafer-bread and, breaking it in two, gave the girl the larger piece, which she bashfully accepted.
The old man raised his eyebrows and upper lids, looked at his old wife, spat in the fireplace, and smoked hard at his cigarette, joining the girl in her invitation by saying, “Yes, have to eat well.”
Soon the young man said, “Thanks,” and the maiden quickly responded, “Eat more,” and “Have eaten.”
After brushing the crumbs away the girl sat down by her mother, and the father rolled a cigarette for the young man and talked longer with him than he had with the others.
After the old ones had stretched out in the corner and begun to “scrape their nostrils with their breath,” the maiden turned to the young man and said: “I have a cornfield in the lands of the priest-chief, down by the river, and if you truly love me, I would that you should hoe the whole in a single morning. Thus may you prove yourself a man, and to love me truly; and if you will do this, happily, as day follows day, will we live each with the other.”
“*Hai-í!*” replied the young man, who smiled as he listened; and as the young maiden looked at him, sitting in the fading firelight with the smile on his face, she thought: “Only possibly. But oh! how I wish his heart might be strong, even though his bundle be not heavy nor large.
“Come with me, young man, and I will show you where you are to await the morning. Early take my father’s hoe, which stands by the doorway, and go down to the cornfield long before the night shadows have run away from Thunder Mountain”﻿—with which she bade him pass a night of contentment and sought her own place.

===Chinese===

他走进火光里，欢欢喜喜又庄重地向众人问好，道了谢，在给他铺好的座位上坐下。
你瞧，老头子正生女儿的气，所以没有吩咐她把熟食摆到客人面前，倒转向他的老伴。
“老人家——”他刚开口，少女已经起身，端来浓香的炖鹿肉和一层层的 héwe 薄饼，摆在年轻人面前火光正亮的地方，还有肉汤给他喝；然后她在他对面坐下，说：“请吃请喝！”于是年轻人取了一卷薄饼，掰成两半，把大的那半递给姑娘；姑娘羞答答地接了。
老人扬起眉毛和上眼皮，看了看老伴，朝火塘里啐了一口，狠狠抽着烟，随着姑娘的话头添了一句：“是，得好好吃。”
不多时年轻人说“谢谢”，少女赶忙回一句“再吃点”，又道“那是吃好了”。
姑娘扫去饼屑，在母亲身边坐下；父亲给年轻人卷了支烟，同他攀谈，谈得比同前头几位都久。
待老人们在墙角躺下，开始“用鼻息刮鼻孔”，少女转向年轻人说：“我在祭司长的地界里有片玉米田，在河边。你要是真爱我，我要你用一个早晨把整片田锄完。这样你可以证明你是个男子汉，真心爱我；你若办到，咱们就欢欢喜喜、一天又一天地相守。”
“Hai-í!”年轻人应道，听着时脸上带笑。少女望着他坐在渐暗的火光里、脸上含笑，心想：“兴许是真心的。啊！但愿他的心够坚强，哪怕他的包袱不重不大。
“跟我来，年轻人，我领你去歇息等天亮的地方。一早拿上我父亲的锄头，就立在门边；趁夜的影子还没从雷霆山（Thunder Mountain）上退尽，就下到玉米田去。”说完，她祝他一夜安然，自去安歇了。

===Original===

When all was still, the young man climbed to the sky-hole and in the starlight asked the gods of the woodlands and waters to give strength to his hands and power to his prayer-medicine, and to meet and bless him with the light of their favor; and he threw to the night-wind meal of the seeds of earth and the waters of the world with which those who are wise fail not to make smooth their trails of life. Then he slept till the sky of the day-land grew yellow and the shadows of the night-land grew gray, and then shouldered his hoe and went down to the cornfield. His task was not great, for the others had hoed much. Where they left off, there he fell to digging right and left with all his strength and haste, till the hard soil mellowed and the earth flew before his strokes as out of the burrows of the strongest-willed gophers and other digging creatures.
When the sun rose the maiden looked forth and saw that his task was already half done. But still she waited. As the sun warmed the day and the youth worked on, the dewdrops of flesh stood all over his body and he cast away, one after the other, his blanket and sash and even his leggings and moccasins. Then he stopped to look around. By the side of the field grew tall yellow-tops. He ran into the thicket and rubbed every part of his body, yea, even the hair of his head and his ear-tips and nostrils, with the bark of the finger-root. Again he fell to work as though he had only been resting, and wondered why the mayflies and gnats and mosquitoes came not to cause him thoughts as they had the others. Yet still the girl lingered; but at last she went slowly to the room where the jar stood.
“It is absurd,” thought she, “that I should hope it or even care for it; it would indeed be great if it were well true that a young man should love me so verily as to hold his face to the front through such a testing.” Nevertheless, she drew the lid off and bade her strange children to spare him no more than they had the others.

===Chinese===

万籁俱寂后，年轻人爬上天口，在星光下祈求林地与水泽的诸神赐他双手以力气、赐他的祈祷之药以神效，求他们降临，以恩宠之光照拂他；又向夜风撒出大地种子之粉与世界之水——智者行路，从不忘以它们铺平人生的道途。然后他一觉睡到天国之天转黄、夜国之影转灰，便扛起锄头下玉米田去。他的活计不算重，因为前头的人已锄掉了大半。从他们停下的地方，他左右开弓、全力以赴地刨下去，直到硬土松软、泥土在他锄头前飞溅，像从意志最坚的囊鼠和别的掘地生灵的洞穴里喷出来一样。
日头升起，少女往外一望，见他的活计已完成一半。可她还是等。太阳晒暖了天，年轻人干个不停，肉身的汗珠周身直冒；他把毯子、腰带、连护腿和鹿皮鞋都一件件甩掉了。然后他停下来四下望。田边长着高高的黄顶花丛。他跑进花丛，拿指根草的树皮把周身每一处都擦了——连头发、耳尖、鼻孔都擦了。他又埋头干起来，像方才不过是歇了口气似的，纳闷为什么蜉蝣蠓虫蚊子不像对别人那样来烦他。
可姑娘还在拖延；末了她慢慢走到立着水瓮的那间屋子。
“我还抱着指望，甚至把这事放在心上，这真荒唐，”她想，“可要是当真有个年轻人爱我到这般地步——经得起这样一场试炼还把脸朝着前方——那可真是了不得。”话虽如此，她还是掀开盖子，吩咐她那些怪孩子：对他一点也别比对其余几位客气。

===Original===

All hasty to feast themselves on the “waters of life,” as our old grandfathers would say for blood, again they rushed out and hummed along over the cornfields in such numbers that they looked more like a wind-driven sandstorm than ever, and “*tsi‑ni‑ni‑i, tso-no-o*,” they hummed and buzzed about the ears of the young man when they came to him, so noisily that the poor fellow, who kept at work all the while, thought they were already biting him. But it was only fancy, for the first mayfly that did bite him danced in the air with disgust and exclaimed to his companions, “*Sho‑o‑o‑m‑m!*” and “*Us-á!*” which meant that he had eaten something nasty, that tasted as badly as vile odors smell. So not another mayfly in the throng would bite, although they all kept singing their song about his ears. And to this day mayflies are careful whom they bite, and dance a long time in the air before they do it.
Then a gnat tried it and gasped, “*Weh!*” which meant that his stomach had turned over, and he had such a sick headache that he reeled round and round in the air, and for that reason gnats always bite very quickly, for fear their stomachs will turn over, and they will reel and reel round and round in the air before doing it.
Finally, long-beak himself tried it, and, as long-beak hangs on, you know, longer than most other little beasts, he kept hold until his two hindlegs were warped out of shape; but at last he had to let go, too, and flew straight away, crying, “*Yá kotchi!*” which meant that something bitter had burned his snout. Now, for these reasons mosquitoes always have bent-up hindlegs, which they keep lifting up and down while biting, as though they were standing on something hot, and they are apt to sing and smell around very cautiously before spearing us, and they fly straight away, you will notice, as soon as they are done.
Now, when the rest of the gnats and mosquitoes heard the words of their elder brothers, they did as the mayflies had done﻿—did not venture, no, not one of them, to bite the young lover. They all flew away and settled down on the yellow-tops, where they had a council, and decided to go and find some prairie-dogs to bite. Therefore you will almost always find mayflies, gnats, and mosquitoes around prairie-dog holes in summer time when the corn is growing.

===Chinese===

一窝蜂要去享用“生命之水”——我们的老祖宗就是这样说血的——它们再度冲出去，掠过玉米田，嗡嗡营营，数目之多，比先前更像一场刮风的沙暴。“tsi‑ni‑ni‑i, tso‑no‑o”，它们围着年轻人耳朵哼着叫着，声音吵得那可怜的家伙——手上活计没停——当真以为自己已经被咬了。其实只是错觉：头一只下口的蜉蝣恶心得在空中直打转，对同伴们喊“Sho‑o‑o‑m‑m!”“Us-á!”——意思是它吃到了什么脏东西，味道坏得就像臭气闻起来那样。于是虫群里再没有第二只蜉蝣肯下口，尽管它们照旧围着他耳朵唱它们的歌。所以直到今天，蜉蝣叮人前总要挑三拣四，先在空中盘旋好一阵才下口。
随后一只蠓虫试了试，倒抽一口气：“Weh!”——意思是它翻胃了，头疼得厉害，在空中晕头转向地转圈。正因如此，蠓虫下口总是飞快，就怕胃一翻，还没来得及咬就先在空中转起圈来。
最后连长喙儿本尊也亲自出马。长喙儿你晓得，比别的小畜生都咬得久；它咬住不放，直咬得两条后腿都变了形；可到头来它也只得松口，径直飞走了，一路喊着“Yá kotchi!”——意思是有什么苦东西烧了它的嘴。就为这些缘故，蚊子至今弓着两条后腿，叮人时不停抬起放下，好像踩在什么烫东西上；它们扎我们之前，总要小心翼翼地又唱又嗅；而且一扎完，你就注意到，立刻径直飞走。
其余的蠓虫蚊子听了兄长们的话，都学蜉蝣的样——不敢，真真一只也不敢，去咬那年轻的恋人。它们全飞走了，落在黄顶花丛上，开了个会，决定去找些草原犬鼠叮咬。因此，夏天玉米生长的时节，你几乎总能在犬鼠洞周围发现蜉蝣、蠓虫和蚊子。

===Original===

So the young man breathed easily as he hoed hard to finish his task ere the noonday, and when the maiden looked down and saw that he still labored there, she said to herself: “Ah, indeed he must love me, for still he is there! Well, it *may be*, for only a little longer and they will leave him in peace.” Hastily she placed venison in the cooking-pot and prepared fresh *héwe* and sweetened bread, “for *maybe*,” she still thought, “and then I will have it ready for him.”
Now, alas! you do not know that this good and beautiful maiden had a sister, alas!﻿—a sister as beautiful as herself, but bad and double-hearted; and you know when people have double hearts they are wizards or witches, and have double tongues and paired thoughts﻿—such a sister elder had the maiden of Mátsaki, alas!
When the sun had climbed almost to the middle of the sky, the maiden, still doubtful, looked down once more. He was there, and was working among the last hills of corn.
“Ah, truly indeed he loves me,” she thought, and she hastened to put on her necklaces and bracelets of shells, her earrings as long as your fingers﻿—of turquoises﻿—and her fine cotton mantles with borders of stitched butterflies of summerland, and flowers of the autumn. Then she took a new bowl from the stick-rack in the corner, and a large many-colored tray that she had woven herself, and she filled the one with meat broth, and the other with the *héwe* and sweetbread, and placing the bowl of meat broth on her head, she took the tray of *héwe* in her hand, and started down toward the cornfield by the riverside to meet her lover and to thank him.
Witches are always jealous of the happiness and good fortune of others. So was the sister of the beautiful maiden jealous when she saw the smile on her *háni’s* face as she tripped toward the river.
“*Ho há!*” said the two-hearted sister. “*Tém-ithlokwa thlokwá! Wananí!*” which are words of defiance and hatred, used so long ago by demons and wizards that no one knows nowadays what they mean except the last one, which plainly says, “Just wait a bit!” and she hastened to dress herself, through her wicked knowledge, exactly as the beautiful maiden was dressed. She even carried just such a bowl and tray; and as she was beautiful, like her younger sister, nobody could have known the one from the other, or the other from the one.

===Chinese===

年轻人松了口气，加紧锄地，要在晌午前收工。少女往下望见他还在劳作，自语道：“啊，他必定是爱我的，因为他还在那儿！唔，也许吧——再过一小会儿，它们就会放过他了。”她连忙往锅里添了鹿肉，备好新鲜的 héwe 和甜面包，“万一呢，”她仍在想，“万一成了，我好给他预备着。”
唉，可是！你还不知道呢：这位贤淑美丽的少女有一个姐姐，唉！——姐姐同她一样美，却心术不正、生了双心；你知道，人一生双心，就是巫男巫女，生着双舌、成对的念头——马察基的少女摊上的就是这么一位姐姐，唉！
太阳快爬到中天时，少女仍不放心，又往下望了一眼。他在，正在最后一垅垅玉米间劳作。
“啊，他当真是爱我的，”她想。她赶紧戴上贝壳的项链和手镯、绿松石的长耳坠——长得像你的手指——披上精美的棉披肩，襟边绣着夏之国的蝴蝶和秋天的花朵。然后她从墙角的木架上取下一只新碗，又取一只她自己编织的大彩盘，一只盛上肉汤，一只摆好 héwe 和甜面包；她把肉汤碗顶在头上，一手端着薄饼盘，动身下到河边的玉米田，去迎她的恋人，去谢他。
巫辈总是嫉妒别人的幸福与好运。美丽的少女轻快地往河边去时，她姐姐看见她脸上的笑，也嫉妒起来。
“Ho há!”双心的姐姐说。“Tém-ithlokwa thlokwá! Wananí!”——这些是挑衅与憎恨的话，魔鬼和巫师们很久很久以前就用的，如今没人知道什么意思了，只知道最后一个，明明白白是“你且等着！”她凭邪术急忙穿戴，穿戴得同美丽妹妹一模一样。她甚至顶着一样的碗、端着一样的盘；她又同妹妹一样美，谁也分不出哪是这一个、哪是那一个。

===Original===

Then she passed herself through a hoop of magic yucca, which made her seem not to be where she was, for no one could see her unless she willed it.
Now, just as the sun was resting in the middle of the sky, the young man finished the field and ran down to the river to wash. Before he was done, he saw the maiden coming down the trail with the bowl on her head and the tray in her hand; so he made haste, and ran back to dress himself and to sit down to wait for her. As she approached, he said: “Thou comest, and may it be happily,”﻿—when lo! there appeared two maidens exactly alike; so he quickly said, “Ye come.”
“*E*,” said the maidens, so nearly together that it sounded like one voice; but when they both placed the same food before him, the poor young man looked from one to the other, and asked:
“Alas! of which am I to eat?”
Then it was that the maiden suddenly saw her sister, and became hot with anger, for she knew her wicked plans. “Ah, thou foolish sister, why didst thou come?” she said. But the other only replied:
“Ah, thou foolish sister, why didst *thou* come?”
“Go back, for he is mine-to-be,” said the maiden, beginning to cry.
“Go back, for he is mine-to-be,” said the bad one, pretending to cry.
And thus they quarrelled until they had given one another smarting words four times, when they fell to fighting﻿—as women always fight, by pulling each other’s hair, and scratching, and grappling until they rolled over each other in the sand.
The poor young man started forward to part them, but he knew not one from the other, so thinking that the bad one must know how to fight better than his beautiful maiden wife, he suddenly caught up his stone-weighted hoe, and furiously struck the one that was uppermost on the head, again and again, until she let go her hold, and fell back, murmuring and moaning: “Alas! that thus it should be after all, after all!” Then she forgot, and her eyes ceased to see.
While yet the young man looked, lo! there was only the dying maiden before him; but in the air above circled an ugly black Crow, that laughed “*kawkaw, kawkaw, kawkaw!*” and flew away to its cave in Thunder Mountain.
Then the young man knew. He cried aloud and beat his breast; then he ran to the river and brought water and bathed the blood away from the maiden’s temples; but alas! she only smiled and talked with her lips, then grew still and cold.

===Chinese===

然后她从一只魔法丝兰编的环里钻过去，那环叫人看不出她在哪儿，因为除非她愿意，谁也瞧不见她。
且说日头正歇在中天时，年轻人锄完了田，跑到河边去洗。还没洗完，他望见少女顶着碗、端着盘沿小道下来；他赶紧跑回去穿好衣裳，坐下等她。她走近了，他说：“你来了，愿你来得欢喜。”——可瞧！眼前竟出现了一模一样的两位少女；他赶忙改口说：“你们来了。”
“E，”两位少女应道，齐得像出自一条喉咙；可两人把同样的饭食摆到他面前时，可怜的年轻人看看这个、望望那个，问：
“唉！我该吃哪一位的？”
就在这时，少女猛然看见了姐姐，气得满脸通红，因为她识破了那恶毒的算计。“啊，你这蠢姐姐，你来做什么？”她说。可另一个只回她：
“啊，你这蠢姐姐，你来做什——么？”
“回去，他是我的，”少女说，哭起来。
“回去，他是我的，”坏姐姐说，装作哭。
她们就这样吵下去，互相甩了四回尖刻话，便动了手——像女人们打架那样，互相揪头发、抓挠、扭作一团，在沙地里滚来滚去。
可怜的年轻人抢上前去劝解，可他分不清谁是谁；他心想，那坏女人打架总该比他美丽的少女妻子在行，便突然抄起那把压着石头的锄头，照着压在上面的那个头上，一下又一下狠狠砸去，直到她松了手，仰面倒下，喃喃呻吟着：“唉！怎到头来、到头来竟是这样！”然后她没了声息，眼睛再也不看了。
年轻人还愣着，瞧啊！面前躺着的只有那垂死的少女；头顶上空，一只丑陋的黑乌鸦打着旋，“kawkaw, kawkaw, kawkaw!”地笑，飞回雷霆山它的洞穴去了。
年轻人这才明白。他放声大哭，捶打胸膛；又奔到河边取来水，洗去少女鬓角的血；可是，唉！她只动了动嘴唇笑了笑，随后便凉了，静了。

===Original===

Alone, as the sun travelled toward the land of evening, wept the young man over the body of his beautiful wife. He knew naught but his sad thoughts. He took her in his arms, and placed his face close to hers, and again and again he called to her: “Alas, alas! my beautiful wife; I loved thee, I love thee. Alas, alas! Ah, my beautiful wife, my beautiful wife!”
When the people returned from their fields in the evening, they missed the beautiful maiden of Mátsaki; and they saw the young man, bending low and alone over something down in the lands of the priest-chief by the river, and when they told the old father, he shook his head and said:
“It is not well with my beautiful child; but as They (the gods) say, thus must all things be.” Then he smiled﻿—for the heart of a priest-chief never cries﻿—and told them to go and bring her to the plaza of Mátsaki and bury her before the House of the Sun; for he knew what had happened.
So the people did as their father had told them. They went down at sunset and took the beautiful maiden away, and wrapped her in mantles, and buried her near the House of the Sun.
But the poor young man knew naught but his sad thoughts. He followed them; and when he had made her grave, he sat down by her earth bed and would not leave her. No, not even when the sun set, but moaned and called to her: “Alas, alas! my beautiful wife; I loved thee, I love thee; even though I knew not thee and killed thee. Alas! Ah, my beautiful wife!”
---
“*Shonetchi!*” (“There is left of my story.”) And what there is left, I will tell you some other night.

===Chinese===

太阳往黄昏之国行去，年轻人独自守着他美丽妻子的尸身哭泣。他什么都不想了，只想着伤心事。他把她搂在怀里，把脸贴上她的脸，一遍又一遍唤她：“唉，唉！我美丽的新娘；我爱过你，我爱着你。唉，唉！啊，我美丽的新娘，我美丽的新娘！”
傍晚人们从田里归来，发现马察基的美丽少女不见了；他们又看见那年轻人独自俯在河边祭司长地界里的什么东西上。人们告诉了老父亲，他摇摇头说：
“我的好孩子不妙了；可既然‘他们’（诸神）这样说了，万事万物就只能这样。”说罢他笑了——因为祭司长的心是从不哭的——吩咐众人去把她抬到马察基的广场，葬在太阳之屋（House of the Sun）前；因为发生了什么，他全知道。
人们照父亲的吩咐办了。日落时他们下去，抬走了美丽的少女，用披肩裹好，葬在太阳之屋近旁。
可这可怜的年轻人什么都不想了，只想着伤心事。他一路跟着；坟安好后，他坐在她的土床边，不肯离开。是啊，连太阳落山也不肯走，只是哀号着唤她：“唉，唉！我美丽的新娘；我爱过你，我爱着你；哪怕我不认得你、害死了你。唉！啊，我美丽的新娘！”
---
“Shonetchi!（我的故事还有剩。）”剩下的那点，我换个晚上再讲给你们。

### (Told the Second Night) / （第二夜所讲）
===Original===

“*Sonahtchi!*”
“*Sons shonetchi!*” (“There is left of my story”;) but I will tell you not alone of the Maid of Mátsaki, because the young man killed her, for he knew not his wife from the other. It is of the Red Feather, or the Wife of Mátsaki that I will tell you this sitting.
Even when the sun set, and the hills and houses grew black in the shadows, still the young man sat by the grave-side, his hands rested upon his knees and his face buried in them. And the people no longer tried to steal his sad thoughts from him; but, instead, left him, as one whose mind errs, to wail out with weeping: “Alas, alas! my beautiful wife; I loved thee, I love thee; even though I knew not thee and killed thee! Alas! Ah, my beautiful wife!”
But when the moon set on the western hills, and the great snowdrift streaked across the mid-sky, and the night was half gone, the sad watcher saw a light in the grave-sands like the light of the embers that die in the ashes. As he watched, his sad thoughts became bright thoughts, for the light grew and brightened till it burned the dark grave-sands as sunlight the shadows. Lo! the bride lay beneath. She tore off her mantles and raised up in her grave-bed. Then she looked at the eager lover so coldly and sadly that his bright thoughts all darkened, for she mournfully told him: “Alas! Ah, my lover, my husband knew not me from the other; loved me not, therefore killed me; even though I had hoped for love, loved me not, therefore killed me!”
Again the young man buried his face in his hands and shook his head mournfully; and like one whose thoughts erred, again he wailed his lament: “Alas, alas! my beautiful bride! I do love thee; I loved thee, but I did not know thee and killed thee! Alas! Ah, my beautiful bride, my beautiful bride!”
At last, as the great star rose from the sky-land, the dead maiden spoke softly to the mourning lover, yet her voice was sad and strange: “Young man, mourn thou not, but go back to the home of thy fathers. Knowest thou not that I am another being? When the sky of the day-land grows yellow and the houses come out of the shadows, then will the light whereby thou sawest me, fade away in the morn-light, as the blazes of late councils pale their red in the sunlight.” Then her voice grew sadder as she said: “I am only a spirit; for remember, alas!

===Chinese===

“Sonahtchi!”
“Sons shonetchi!”（“我的故事还有剩。”）不过这一回我不单讲马察基的少女——年轻人因为认不出妻子、错手打死了她——这一坐，我要讲的是红羽，也就是马察基的妻子。
太阳落了，丘峦和房舍隐进黑影里，年轻人仍坐在坟边，双手拄膝，脸埋在手里。人们不再去惊扰他的伤心事，只把他当作神智失常的人，任他哭着哀号：“唉，唉！我美丽的新娘；我爱过你，我爱着你；哪怕我不认得你、害死了你！唉！啊，我美丽的新娘！”
可是当月亮落到西山上，雪河般的银河横过中天、黑夜过去一半时，伤心的守望人看见坟沙里透出一团光，像灰烬里将熄的炭火。他望着望着，伤心事亮起来了，因为那光越长越亮，照得漆黑的坟沙亮堂堂的，像阳光照散阴影。看哪！新娘就躺在下面。她扯下披肩，从坟床里坐起身。她望着那急切的恋人，望得那样冷、那样悲，他心头的亮光全暗了下去，因为她哀哀地告诉他：“唉！啊，我的恋人，我的丈夫认不出我，错当了别人；他不爱我，所以杀了我；我指望过他的爱，可他不爱我，所以杀了我！”
年轻人又把脸埋进手里，悲伤地摇头；像神智失常的人一样，又哀声唱起他的挽歌：“唉，唉！我美丽的新娘！我当真爱着你；我爱过你，可我不认得你，害死了你！唉！啊，我美丽的新娘，我美丽的新娘！”
终于，大星从天国升起，死去的少女柔声对哀悼的恋人说话了，可她的嗓音又悲伤又陌生：“年轻人，别哀伤了，回你父辈的家去吧。你不知道我已是另一种生灵吗？等天国之天泛黄、房舍从阴影里现出来，你见我的这点光，就会在晨光里消散，像深夜议事会的火光到了日头底下就褪尽它们的红。”随后她的声音更悲了：“我只是一缕魂灵；因为记住啊，唉！

===Original===

ah, my lover, my husband knew not me from the other﻿—loved me not, therefore killed me; even though I had hoped for love, loved me not, therefore killed me.”
But the young man would not go until, in the gray of the morning, he saw nothing where the light had appeared but the dark sand of the grave as it had been. Then he arose and went away in sorrow. Nor would he all day speak to men, but gazed only whither his feet stepped and shook his head sadly like one whose thoughts wandered. And when again the houses and hills grew black with the shadows, he sought anew the fresh grave and sat down by its side, bowed his head and still murmured: “Alas, alas! my beautiful wife, I loved thee, though I knew not thee, and killed thee. Alas! Ah, my beautiful wife!”
Even brighter glowed the light in the grave-sands when the night was divided, and the maiden’s spirit arose and sat in her grave-bed, but she only reproached him and bade him go. “For,” said she, “I am only a spirit; remember, alas! ah, my lover, my husband knew not me from the other; loved me not, therefore killed me; even though I had hoped for love, loved me not, therefore killed me!”
But he left only in the morning, and again when the dark came, returned to the grave-side.
When the light shone that night, the maiden, more beautiful than ever, came out of the grave-bed and sat by her lover. Once more she urged him to return to his fathers; but when she saw that he would not, she said: “Thou hadst better, for I go a long journey. As light as the wind is, so light will my feet be; as long as the day is, thou canst not my form see. Know thou not that the spirits are seen but in darkness? for, alas! ah, my lover, my husband knew not me from the other; loved me not, therefore killed me; even though I had hoped for love, loved me not, therefore killed me!”
Then the young man ceased bemoaning his beautiful bride. He looked at her sadly, and said: “I do love thee, my beautiful wife! I do love thee, and whither thou goest let me therefore go with thee! I care not how long is the journey, nor how hard is the way. If I can but see thee, even only at night time, then will I be happy and cease to bemoan thee. It was because I loved thee and would have saved thee; but alas, my beautiful wife! I knew not thee, therefore killed thee!”
“Alas! Ah, my lover; and Ah!

===Chinese===

啊，我的恋人，我的丈夫认不出我，错当了别人——他不爱我，所以杀了我；我指望过他的爱，可他不爱我，所以杀了我。”
可年轻人不肯走，直到晨光灰白里，那发光的地方什么也不剩，只有坟上一团黑沙，同原先一样。他才起身，满怀悲伤地走了。他一整天不同人说话，只盯着脚下走路，悲伤地摇头，像神思恍惚的人。等到房舍和丘峦又被阴影染黑，他又寻到那座新坟，坐在旁边，垂着头，仍喃喃道：“唉，唉！我美丽的妻子，我爱过你，可我不认得你，害死了你。唉！啊，我美丽的妻子！”
夜过半时，坟沙里的光更亮了，少女的魂灵起身，坐在坟床上；可她只是责备他，催他回去。“因为，”她说，“我只是一缕魂灵；记住啊，唉！啊，我的恋人，我的丈夫认不出我，错当了别人；他不爱我，所以杀了我；我指望过他的爱，可他不爱我，所以杀了我！”
可他到早晨才走，天一黑，又回到坟边。
那夜光亮起时，少女比先前更美，从坟床里出来，坐在恋人身边。她又劝他回父辈家去；见他不肯，她便说：“你还是回去的好，因为我要走一程远路。风有多轻，我的脚就有多轻；白日有多长，你也见不着我的形影。你不知道魂灵只有在黑暗里才看得见吗？因为，唉！啊，我的恋人，我的丈夫认不出我，错当了别人；他不爱我，所以杀了我；我指望过他的爱，可他不爱我，所以杀了我！”
于是年轻人不再哀叹他的新娘。他悲伤地望着她，说：“我当真爱你，我美丽的妻子！我当真爱你，你到哪里，就让我跟你到哪里！路多长我不管，路多难我也不管。只要能见着你——哪怕只在夜里——我就欢喜，就不再哀叹。那正是因为我爱你、想救你；可是唉，我美丽的妻子！我不认得你，所以害死了你！”
“唉！啊，我的恋人；还有啊，”

===Original===

how I loved thee; but I am a spirit, and thou art unfinished. But if thou thus love me, go back when I leave thee and plume many prayer-sticks. Choose a light, downy feather and dye it with ocher. Wrap up in thy blanket a lunch for four daylights; bring with thee much prayer-meal; come to me at midnight and sit by my grave-side, and when in the eastward the day-land is lighting, tie over my forehead the reddened light feather, and when with the morning I fade from thy vision, follow only the feather until it is evening, and then thou shalt see me and sit down beside me.”
So at sunrise the young man went away and gathered feathers of the summer birds, and cut many prayer-sticks, whereon he bound them with cotton, as gifts to the Fathers. Then he found a beautiful downy feather plucked from the eagle, and dyed it red with ocher, and tied to it a string of cotton wherewith to fasten it over the forehead of the spirit maiden. When night came, he took meal made from parched corn and burnt sweetbread, and once more went down to the plaza and sat by the grave-side.
When midnight came and the light glowed forth through the grave-sands, lo! the maiden-spirit came out and stood by his side. She seemed no longer sad, but happy, like one going home after long absence. Nor was the young man sad or single-thoughted like one whose mind errs; so they sat together and talked of their journey till the day-land grew yellow and the black shadows gray, and the houses and hills came out of the darkness.
“Once more would I tell thee to go back,” said the maiden’s spirit to the young man; “but I know why thou goest with me, and it is well. Only watch me when the day comes, and thou wilt see me no more; but look whither the plume goeth, and follow, for thou knowest that thou must tie it to the hair above my forehead.”
Then the young man took the bright red plume out from among the feathers of sacrifice, and gently tied it above the maiden-spirit’s forehead.
As the light waved up from behind the great mountain the red glow faded out from the grave-sands and the youth looked in vain for the spirit of the maiden; but before him, at the height of one’s hands when standing, waved the light downy feather in the wind of the morning.

===Chinese===

我多么爱过你；可我是魂灵，你却尚未完工。但你既然这样爱我，那么在我离开你之后，回去削许多祈祷杖，多多地插上羽。拣一根轻软的绒羽，用赭石染红。用毯子包上四个白天的干粮；随身多带祈祷粉；半夜到我这里来，坐在我坟边；等到东边天国发亮，把那染红的轻羽系上我的额头；晨光里我从你眼前消散后，你就只跟着那根羽毛走，走到天黑，那时你就会看见我，在我身边坐下。”
于是日出时年轻人回去，采了夏鸟的羽毛，削了许多祈祷杖，用棉线把羽毛绑上去，作为献给父神们的礼物。然后他寻到一根从鹰身上拔下的漂亮绒羽，用赭石染红，系上一条棉线，好缚在魂灵少女的额上。夜里，他带上烤玉米磨的粉和烤甜饼，再一次下到广场，坐在坟边。
半夜到了，光从坟沙里透出来，看哪！少女的魂灵出来，立在他身边。她似乎不再悲伤，倒很快活，像久别还乡的人。年轻人也不再悲伤、不再一根筋地失魂落魄；他们坐在一起，说着路上的事，直到天国泛黄、黑影转灰，房舍和丘冈从黑暗里现出来。
“我本想再劝你回去，”少女的魂灵对年轻人说；“可我知道你为什么跟我走，也好。只是白日来时你要看着我，你就再也看不见我了；只看那绒羽往哪儿飘，就跟着走，因为你知道，必须把它系在我额前的头发上。”
于是年轻人从祭羽堆里取出那根亮红的绒羽，轻轻系在少女魂灵的额前。
光从大山背后涌起时，坟沙里的红光褪尽了，年轻人徒然寻找少女的魂灵；可在他面前，一个站立的人举手那么高的地方，清晨的风里飘着那根轻软的绒羽。

===Original===

Then the plume, not the wife, rose before him, like the plumes on the head of a dancer, and moved through the streets that led westward, and down through the fields to the river. And out through the streets that led westward, and down on the trail by the river, and on over the plains always toward the land of evening, the young man followed close the red feather; but at last he began to grow weary, for the plume glided swiftly before him, until at last it left him far behind, and even now and then lost him entirely. Then, as he hastened on, he called in anguish:
“My beautiful bride! My beautiful bride! Oh, where art thou?”
But the plume, not the wife, stopped and waited. And thus the plume and the young man journeyed until, toward evening, they came to the forests of sweet-smelling piñons and cedars. As the night hid the hills in the shadows, alas! the plume disappeared, but the young man pressed onward, for he knew that the plume still journeyed westward. Yet at times he was so weary that he almost lost the strength of his thoughts; for he ran into trees by the trail-side and stumbled over dry roots and branches. So again and again he would call out in anguish: “My beautiful wife! My beautiful bride! Oh, where art thou?”
At last, when the night was divided, to his joy he saw, far away on the hilltop, a light that was red and grew brighter like the light of a campfire’s red embers when fanned by the wind of the nighttime. And like a star that is rising or setting, the red light sat still on the hilltop. So he ran hastily forward, until, as he neared the red light, lo! there sat the spirit of the beautiful maiden; and as he neared her, she said:
“Comest thou?” and “How hast thou come to the evening?”
As she spoke she smiled, and motioned him to sit down beside her. He was so weary that he slept while he talked to her; but, remember, she was a spirit, therefore she slept not.
Just as the morning star came up from the day-land, the maiden rose to journey on, and the young man, awaking, followed her. But as the hills came out of the shadows, the form of the maiden before him grew fainter and fainter, until it faded entirely, and only the red plume floated before him, like the plume on the head of a dancer.

===Chinese===

于是那绒羽——而不是妻子——在他前头升起，像舞者头上插的羽冠，穿过朝西的街巷，下到田间，直到河边。年轻人沿着朝西的街巷出去，沿河边小道，越过平原，一路向着黄昏之国，紧紧跟着那根红羽；可他渐渐乏了，因为绒羽在他前面滑得飞快，最后把他远远甩在后面，有时干脆叫他整个跟丢。他一路焦急地喊：
“我美丽的新娘！我美丽的新娘！哦，你在哪里？”
可那绒羽——而不是妻子——停下来等他。绒羽和年轻人就这样赶路，直到傍晚，来到一片香气扑鼻的矮松和雪松林。夜色把山峦藏进阴影，唉！绒羽不见了；可年轻人仍往前赶，因为他知道绒羽还在向西行。只是他时时乏得几乎连念头都撑不住；他一头撞在道旁的树上，绊在干树根和断枝上。于是他一遍又一遍哀声喊：“我美丽的妻子！我美丽的新娘！哦，你在哪里？”
终于，夜过半时，他欢喜地望见远处山顶上一点红光，越来越亮，像夜里风煽着营火红炭时的光。那红光像一颗初升或西沉的星，定定地停在山顶上。他急忙奔过去；临近那红光时，看哪！美丽的少女的魂灵正坐在那里；他走近，她说：
“你来了？你怎么来到黄昏之地的？”
她说话时面带笑，招手让他在身边坐下。他乏得一边同她说话一边就睡着了；可是记住，她是魂灵，所以不睡。
启明星刚从天国上来，少女起身继续赶路，年轻人醒来跟上。可山峦从阴影里现出时，前方少女的形影越来越淡、越来越淡，终于完全消隐，只剩那根红羽在他前面飘，像舞者头上的羽冠。

===Original===

Far ahead and fast floated the plume, until it entered a plain of lava filled with sharp crags; yet still it went on, for the maiden’s spirit moved over the barriers as lightly as the down of dead flowers in autumn. But alas! the young man had to seek his way, and the plume again left him far behind, until he was forced to cry out: “Ah, my beautiful bride, do wait for me, for I love thee, and will not turn from thee!” Then the plume stopped on the other side of the crags and waited until the poor young man came nearer, his feet and legs cut and bleeding, and his wind almost out. Then the trail was more even, and led through wide plains; but even thus the young man could scarce keep the red plume in sight. But at night the maiden awaited him in a sheltered place, and they rested together beneath the cedars until daylight. Then again she faded out in the daylight, and the red plume led the way.
For a long time the trail was pleasant, but toward evening they came to a wide bed of cactus, and the plume passed over as swiftly as ever, but the young man’s moccasins were soon torn and his feet and legs cruelly lacerated with the cactus spines; yet still he pursued the red plume until the pain seemed to sting his whole body, and he gasped and wailed: “Ah, my beautiful wife, wait for me; do wait, for I love thee and will not leave thee!” Then the plume stopped beyond the plain of cactus and waited until he had passed through, but not longer, for ere he had plucked all the needles of the cactus from his bleeding feet, it floated on, and he lifted himself up and followed until at evening the maiden again waited and bade him “Sit down and rest.”
That night she seemed to pity him, and once more spoke to him: “*Yo á!* My lover, my husband, turn back, oh, turn back! for the way is long and untrodden, and thy heart is but weak and is mortal. I go to the Council of Dead Ones, and how can the living there enter?”
But the youth only wept, and begged that she let him go with her. “For, ah,” said he, “my beautiful wife, my beautiful bride, I love thee and cannot turn from thee!”
And she smiled only and shook her head sadly as she replied: “*Yo á!* It shall be as thou willest. It may be thy heart will not wither, for tomorrow is one more day onward, and then down the trail to the waters wherein stands the ladder of others, shall I lead thee to wait me forever.”

===Chinese===

绒羽远远地在前头飞快飘着，飘进了一片满是尖利巉岩的熔岩平原；它照样一路过去，因为少女的魂灵越过这些障碍，轻得像秋天枯花上的绒毛。可是唉！年轻人只好自己寻路，绒羽又把他远远甩下，逼得他喊出声来：“啊，我美丽的新娘，等等我，因为我爱你，不会离开你！”于是绒羽在巉岩那头停下，等那可怜的年轻人赶上前来——他脚腿割破流血，气几乎喘不上。再往前路平了些，穿过宽阔的平原；即便这样，年轻人也勉强才望得住那根红羽。入夜，少女在一处避风的地方等他，他们一同在雪松下歇到天明。然后她又随日光消隐，红羽领路。
很长一段时间，路走得很顺；可傍晚时分他们来到一片宽阔的仙人掌地。绒羽照样一掠而过，年轻人的鹿皮鞋很快就破了，脚和腿叫仙人掌刺扎得血肉模糊；可他仍追着那根红羽，直到疼得仿佛全身都在刺痛，他喘着气哀求：“啊，我美丽的妻子，等等我；千万等等，因为我爱你，不会离开你！”于是绒羽在仙人掌平原那头停下，等他穿过去，可也只等这一程——他还没把流血脚上的刺拔完，绒羽又飘了起来；他撑起身子跟上去，直到傍晚，少女又等他，说“坐下歇歇吧”。
那夜她似乎怜惜他了，又对他说：“Yo á! 我的恋人，我的丈夫，回头吧，哦，回头吧！路长而无人迹，你的心还弱，还是肉身的。我要去亡者议会（Council of Dead Ones），活人怎么进得去？”
可年轻人只是哭，求她带他同去。“因为，啊，”他说，“我美丽的妻子，我美丽的新娘，我爱你，不能离开你！”
她只是笑笑，悲伤地摇头，答道：“Yo á! 就依你的心意吧。也许你的心不会枯萎，因为明天再走一天，然后顺着小道下到立着‘他人之梯’的水边，我会领你在那里，等我，永远。”

===Original===

At mid-sun on the day after, the plume led the way straight to a deep canyon, the walls of which were so steep that no man could pass them alive. For a moment the red plume paused above the chasm, and the youth pressed on and stretched his hand forth to detain it; but ere he had gained the spot, it floated on straight over the dark canyon, as though no ravine had been there at all; for to spirits the trails that once have been, even though the waters have worn them away, still are.
Wildly the young man rushed up and down the steep brink, and despairingly he called across to the plume: “Alas! ah, my beautiful wife! Wait, only wait for me, for I love thee and cannot turn from thee!” Then, like one whose thoughts wandered, he threw himself over the brink and hung by his hands as if to drop, when a jolly little striped Squirrel, who was playing at the bottom of the canyon, happened to see him, and called out: “*Tsithl! Tsithl!*” and much more, which meant “*Ah hai! Wananí!*” “You crazy fool of a being! You have not the wings of a falcon, nor the hands of a Squirrel, nor the feet of a spirit, and if you drop you will be broken to pieces and the moles will eat up the fragments! Wait! Hold hard, and I will help you, for, though I am but a Squirrel, I know how to think!”
Whereupon the little chit ran chattering away and called his mate out of their house in a rock-nook: “Wife! Wife! Come quickly; run to our corn room and bring me a hemlock, and hurry! hurry! Ask me no questions; for a crazy fool of a man over here will break himself to pieces if we don’t quickly make him a ladder.”
So the little wife flirted her brush in his face and skipped over the rocks to their storehouse, where she chose a fat hemlock and hurried to her husband who was digging a hole in the sand underneath where the young man was hanging. Then they spat on the seed, and buried it in the hole, and began to dance round it and sing﻿—

> “Kiäthlä tsilu,

Silokwe, silokwe, silokwe;

Kiʻai silu silu,

Tsithl! Tsithl!”

Which meant, as far as anyone can tell now (for it was a long time ago, and partly squirrel talk),

> “Hemlock of the

Tall kind, tall kind, tall kind,

Sprout up hemlock, hemlock,

Chit! Chit!”

===Chinese===

第三天日到中天，绒羽径直领路到一道深峡谷，谷壁陡得没有人能活着越过去。红羽在深渊上方停了一停，年轻人抢上前伸手要留住它；可他还没赶到，它已经飘过黑黢黢的峡谷，仿佛那里根本没有一道沟壑；因为对魂灵来说，从前走过的路，哪怕流水早已把它磨蚀殆尽，依然存在。
年轻人在陡崖边上狂乱地跑上跑下，绝望地朝对面的绒羽喊：“唉！啊，我美丽的新娘！等等，只求你等等我，因为我爱你，不能离开你！”然后，像神智失常的人，他从崖边纵身探出去，双手吊着，眼看就要撒手——恰巧一只快活的条纹小松鼠（Squirrel）正在谷底玩耍，一抬头看见了他，喊道：“Tsithl! Tsithl!”还说了许多，意思是“Ah hai! Wananí!”“你这疯了蠢货！你没有隼的翅膀，没有松鼠的爪子，没有魂灵的脚，一撒手你就要摔成碎片，鼹鼠要把碎片吃个精光！等着！抓牢了，我来帮你；别看我不过是只松鼠，我可是会动脑子的！”
说罢小家伙喋喋地跑开了，从岩缝的家里喊出他的老伴：“老婆子！老婆子！快来；跑咱家玉米仓去，给我取一颗铁杉籽来，快些！快些！别问为什么；这边的疯蠢货要是咱们不赶紧给他搭架梯子，就要摔个粉碎了。”
于是小老伴把大尾巴一甩，扫他一脸，蹦跳着越过石头到他们的储仓，挑了颗肥硕的铁杉籽，急急送到丈夫跟前；丈夫正在那年轻人悬吊处的沙地里刨坑。两口子朝种子啐了口唾沫，埋进坑里，围着它边跳边唱——

> “Kiäthlä tsilu,

Silokwe, silokwe, silokwe;

Kiʻai silu silu,

Tsithl! Tsithl!”

这歌如今谁也只能猜个大概（毕竟是很久以前的事了，还带着一半松鼠话），意思是：

> “铁杉籽，

长高个，长高个，长高个；

快发芽，铁杉，铁杉，

吱！吱！”

===Original===

And every time they danced around and sang the song through, the ground moved, until the fourth time they said “*Tsithl! Tsithl!*” the tree sprouted forth and kept growing until the little Squirrel could jump into it, and by grabbing the topmost bough and bracing himself against the branches below, could stretch and pull it, so that in a short time he made it grow as high as the young man’s feet, and he had all he could do to keep the poor youth from jumping right into it before it was strong enough to hold him. Presently he said “*Tsithl! Tsithl!*” and whisked away before the young man had time to thank him. Then the sad lover climbed down and quickly gained the other side, which was not so steep; before he could rest from his climb, however, the plume floated on, and he had to get up and follow it.
Just as the sun went into the west, the plume hastened down into a valley between the mountains, where lay a beautiful lake; and around the borders of the lake a very ugly old man and woman, who were always walking back and forth across the trails, came forward and laughed loudly and greeted the beautiful maiden pleasantly. Then they told her to enter; and she fearlessly walked into the water, and a ladder of flags came up out of the middle of the lake to receive her, down which she stepped without stopping until she passed under the waters. For a little﻿—and then all was over﻿—a bright light shone out of the water, and the sound of many glad voices and soft merry music came also from beneath it; then the stars of the sky and the stars of the waters looked the same at each other as they had done before.
“Alas!” cried the young man as he ran to the lakeside. “Ah, my beautiful wife, my beautiful wife, only wait, only wait, that I may go with thee!” But only the smooth waters and the old man and woman were before him; nor did the ladder come out or the old ones greet him. So he sat down on the lakeside wringing his hands and weeping, and ever his mind wandered back to his old lament: “Alas! alas! my beautiful bride, my beautiful wife, I love thee; I loved thee, but I knew not thee and killed thee!”
Toward the middle of the night once more he heard strange, happy voices. The doorway to the Land of Spirits opened, and the light shot up through the dark green waters from many windows, like sparks from a chimney on a dark, windless night.

===Chinese===

他们每围着跳一圈、把歌唱一遍，地就动一动；唱到第四遍说“Tsithl! Tsithl!”时，树苗破土而出，一个劲儿往上长，长到小松鼠能跳进去；他攀住顶枝、蹬住下面的枝桠，又抻又拽，不多时就把树拽得长到了年轻人的脚底下；他费了好大的劲，才拦住那可怜的家伙不等树扎得牢就往里跳。随后他喊了一声“Tsithl! Tsithl!”，年轻人还没来得及道谢，他早已一溜烟没影了。伤心的恋人爬下树，很快上了对岸——那一边不很陡；可他还没喘匀气，绒羽又飘了起来，他只好起身再跟。
太阳落进西边时，绒羽急急下到群山间的一道山谷，谷里卧着一泓美丽的湖。湖岸边，一对丑陋至极的老头老太——他们总在道上走来走去——迎上前来，高声笑着，快快活活向美丽的少女问好。然后他们叫她进去；她毫无惧色地走进水里，一挂菖蒲编的梯子从湖心升上来接她，她顺级而下，不曾停步，直到没入水下。片刻——随即一切归于平静——水中透出一道亮光，许多欢快的说话声和轻柔美妙的乐声也从水底传来；然后天上的星和水里的星又像先前那样彼此对望。
“唉！”年轻人奔到湖边喊。“啊，我美丽的妻子，我美丽的妻子，只求你等等，等等，让我跟你同去！”可他面前只有平滑的水面和那一对老人；梯子不再升起，老人们也不迎他。他坐在湖边绞着手哭，心里又翻出他的老挽歌：“唉！唉！我美丽的新娘，我美丽的妻子，我爱你；我爱过你，可我不认得你，害死了你！”
夜半时分，他又听见了奇怪而欢快的声响。灵界（Land of Spirits）的门开了，亮光从许多窗口穿过墨绿的水射上来，像无风的黑夜里烟囱里迸出的火星。

===Original===

Then the ladder again ascended, and he saw the forms of the dead pass out and in, and heard the sounds of the *Kâkâ*, as it danced for the gods. The comers and goers were bright and beautiful, but their garments were snow-white cotton, stitched with many-colored threads, and their necklaces and bracelets were of dazzling white shells and turquoises unnumbered. Once he ventured to gain the bright entrance, but the water grew deep and chilled him till he trembled with fear and cold. Yet he looked in at the entrances, and lo! as he gazed he caught sight of his beautiful bride all covered with garments and bright things. And there in the midst of the *Kâkâ* she sat at the head of the dancers. She seemed happy and smiled as she watched, and youths as bright and as happy came around her, and she seemed to forget her lone lover.
Then with a cry of despair and anguish he crawled to the lake-shore and buried his face in the sands and rank grasses. Suddenly he heard a low screech, and then a hoarse voice seemed to call him. He looked, and a great Owl flew over him, saying: “*Muhaí! Hu hu! Hu hu!*”
“What wilt thou?” he cried, in vexed anguish.
Then the Owl flew closer, and, lighting, asked: “Why weepest thou, my child?”
He turned and looked at the Owl and told it part of his trouble, when the Owl suddenly twisted its head quite around﻿—as owls do﻿—to see if anyone were near; then came closer and said: “I know all about it, young man. Come with me to my house in the mountain, and if thou wilt but follow my counsel, all will yet be well.” Then the Owl led the way to a cave far above and bade him step in. As he placed his foot inside the opening, behold! it widened into a bright room, and many Owl-men and Owl-women around greeted him happily, and bade him sit down and eat.
The old Owl who had brought him, changed himself in a twinkling, as he entered the room, and hung his owl-coat on an antler. Then he went away, but presently returned, bringing a little bag of medicine. “Before I give thee this, let me tell thee what to do, and what thou must promise,” said he of the owl-coat.
The young man eagerly reached forth his hand for the magic medicine.
“Fool!” cried the being; “were it not well, for that would I not help thee. Thou art too eager, and I will not trust thee with my medicine of sleep.

===Chinese===

随后梯子又升上来，他看见亡者的形影出出进进，听见卡卡舞（Kâkâ）为诸神起舞的乐声。来往的人影又亮又美，可他们穿的是雪白的棉衣，绣着五彩丝线，项链手镯是耀眼的白贝和不计其数的绿松石。有一次他壮着胆子想凑近那亮堂堂的入口，可是水越来越深，寒气逼得他又怕又抖。可他仍朝入口里张望，瞧啊！他一眼望见了他美丽的新娘，满身衣饰和亮闪闪的东西。在卡卡舞的中央，她坐在舞者的上首。她看着像是很快活，含笑观望；一些同她一样亮、一样快活的青年围着她，她像是把她孤零零的恋人忘掉了。
他绝望哀痛地喊了一声，爬到湖岸，把脸埋进沙地和乱草里。忽然他听见一声低低的尖叫，随后一个嘶哑的嗓音似在唤他。他抬头，一只大猫头鹰（Owl）从他头顶飞过，说：“Muhaí! Hu hu! Hu hu!”
“你要做什么？”他又恼又痛地喊。
猫头鹰飞近些，落下来问：“你为什么哭，我的孩子？”
他转过脸看了看猫头鹰，把他的伤心事说了一半；猫头鹰忽然把头拧了个整圈——猫头鹰都这样——看看近处有没有人；然后凑近些说：“我全知道，年轻人。跟我到我山里的家去；只要你肯听我的主意，一切还有指望。”于是猫头鹰领路，到了高处一个山洞，叫他进去。他一脚跨进洞口，看哪！洞口展宽成一间亮堂的屋子，许多猫头鹰人、猫头鹰妇人围上来欢欢喜喜招呼他，请他坐下吃东西。
领他来的老猫头鹰一进屋，一眨眼就变了身，把羽衣挂上鹿角架。随后他出去了，不多时回来，拿来一小袋药。“在我把药给你之前，先让我告诉你该做什么、该许下什么诺。”穿羽衣的那位说。
年轻人急不可待，伸手就要接那神药。
“蠢货！”那生灵喝道；“要不是事有可为，我才不帮你。你太性急，我不敢把我的睡药放心交给你。

===Original===

Thou shalt sleep here, and when thou awakest thou shalt find the morning star in the sky, and thy dead wife before thee on the trail toward the Middle Ant Hill. With the rising sun she will wake and smile on thee. Be not foolish, but journey preciously with her, and not until ye reach the home of thy fathers shalt thou approach her or kiss her; for if thou doest this, all will be as nothing again. But if thou doest as I counsel thee, all will be well, and happily may ye live one with the other.”
He ceased, and, taking a tiny pinch of the medicine, blew it in the face of the youth. Instantly the young man sank with sleep where he had been sitting, and the beings, putting on their owl-coats, flew away with him under some trees by the trail that led to Mátsaki and the Ant Hill of the Middle.
Then they flew over the lake, and threw the medicine of sleep in at the windows, and taking the plumed prayer-sticks which the young man had brought with him, they chose some red plumes for themselves, and with the others entered the home of the *Kâkâ*. Softly they flew over the sleeping fathers and their children (the gods of the *Kâkâ* and the spirits) and, laying the prayer-plumes before the great altar, caught up the beautiful maiden and bore her over the waters and woodlands to where the young man was still sleeping. Then they hooted and flew off to their mountain.
As the great star came out of the day-land, the young man awoke, and lo! there before him lay his own beautiful wife. Then he turned his face away that he might not be tempted, and waited with joy and longing for the coming out of the sun. When at last the sun came out, with the first ray that brightened the beautiful maiden’s face, she opened her eyes and gazed wildly around at first, but seeing her lonely lover, smiled, and said: “Truly, thou lovest me!”
Then they arose and journeyed apart toward the home of their fathers, and the young man forgot not the counsel of the Owl, but journeyed wisely, till on the fourth day they came in sight of the Mountain of Thunder and saw the river that flows by Salt City.
As they began to go down into the valley, the maiden stopped and said: “*Hahuá*, I am weary, for the journey is long and the day is warm.” Then she sat down in the shadow of a cedar and said: “Watch, my husband, while I sleep a little; only a little, and then we will journey together again.”

===Chinese===

你就睡在这儿；等你醒来，会看见晨星在天上，你死去的妻子就在你面前，在通往世界中央蚁丘（Middle Ant Hill）的小道上。太阳升起时她会醒来，对你微笑。别犯傻，好好同她赶路；不到你父辈的家门，不许你亲近她、亲吻她；你要是犯了这一条，一切又要落空。可你要是照我嘱咐的做，一切都会好，你们可以欢欢喜喜相守。”
他说完，捻了一小撮药，吹在年轻人脸上。年轻人顿时在坐着的地方沉沉睡去。众生灵披上羽衣，把他夹带着飞到马察基和中央蚁丘去的小道旁几棵树下。
然后他们飞过湖面，把睡药从窗口撒进去，又取了年轻人随身带来的插羽祈祷杖，给自己挑了几根红羽，带着其余的进了卡卡之家。他们轻轻飞过沉睡的父神和他们的孩子们（卡卡诸神与众魂灵），把祈祷羽摆在大祭坛前，抱起美丽的少女，越过水面和林地，飞到年轻人仍在酣睡的地方。随后他们呜地叫了一声，飞回山里去了。
大星从天国出来时，年轻人醒了，看哪！他美丽的新娘就躺在面前。他把脸转开，免得受诱惑，怀着欢喜和渴望等太阳出来。太阳终于出来，第一道光落在美丽少女脸上，她睁开眼，先是一阵茫然四顾，可看见她孤零零的恋人，就笑了，说：“你当真是爱我的！”
于是他们起身，相隔着朝父辈的家赶路。年轻人不忘猫头鹰的嘱咐，一路谨慎；第四天，他们望见了雷霆山（Mountain of Thunder），看见了流经盐城的那条河。
他们开始下到谷里时，少女停下来，说：“Hahuá，我乏了，路长，天又热。”她在雪松荫里坐下，说：“守着我，我的丈夫，让我睡一小会儿；就一小会儿，然后咱们再一起赶路。”

===Original===

And he said: “Be it well.”
Then she lay down and seemed to sleep. She smiled and looked so beautiful to the longing lover that he softly rose and crept close to her. Then, alas! he laid his hand upon her and kissed her.
Quickly the beautiful maiden started. Her face was all covered with sadness, and she said, hastily and angrily: “Ah, thou shameless fool! I now know! Thou lovest me not! How vain that I should have hoped for thy love!”
With shame, indeed, and sorrow, he bent his head low and covered his face with his hands. Then he started to speak, when an Owl flew up and hooted mournfully at him from a treetop. Then the Owl winged her way to the westward, and ever after the young man’s mind wandered.
Alas! alas! Thus it was in the days of the ancients. Maybe had the young man not kissed her yonder toward the Lake of the Dead, we would never have journeyed nor ever have mourned for others lost. But then it is well! If men and women had never died, then the world long ago had overflown with children, starvation, and warring.
Thus shortens my story.

===Chinese===

他说：“就这么办，好。”
于是她躺下，像是睡了。她微笑着，在那渴望的恋人眼里美极了，他不由得轻轻起身，悄悄凑近她。唉！他把手搭在她身上，吻了她。
美丽的少女猛然惊起。她满脸悲容，又急又怒地说：“啊，你这不知羞的蠢货！我如今明白了！你并不爱我！我竟指望你的爱，多么痴！”
他当真又羞又痛，把头垂得低低的，双手捂住脸。他正要开口，一只猫头鹰飞来，在树顶上冲他哀声啼叫。随后那猫头鹰振翅向西去了；从此，年轻人的神智就恍恍惚惚了。
唉！唉！上古之时便是这样。兴许，要是年轻人没在死者之湖那头吻她，我们就永远不必远行、永远不必为失去的亲人哀恸。可这样也好！倘若男人女人从不曾死，这世界早就让儿女、饥馑和战乱挤溢满了。
我的故事就此收短。

## The Maiden the Sun Made Love To, and Her Boys Or, The Origin of Anger / 太阳所爱的少女和她的儿子们；或：愤怒的起源
===Original===

Let it be about a person who lived in the Home of the Eagles (Kʻiákime), under the Mountain of Thunder, that I tell you today. So let it be. It was in the ancient, long-forgotten times. It was in the very ancient times beyond one’s guessing. There lived then, in this town, the daughter of a great priest-chief, but she had never, never, never since she was a little child, come forth from the doorway of the house in which she dwelt. No one there in that town had ever seen her; even her own townspeople had never seen her.
Now, day after day at noon-time, when the Sun stood in the mid-heavens, he would look down from the sky through a little window in the roof of her house. And he it was who instant was her lover, and who, descending upon the luminously yellow trail his own rays created, would talk to her. And he was her only companion, for she knew not her own townspeople, neither had she seen them since she was a child. None save only her parents ever saw her.
“Wonder what the cacique’s child looks like,” the people would say to one another. “She never comes out; no one has seen her since she was a little child.” And so at last they schemed to get a look at her. One said: “I have it! Let us have a dance for her. Then it may be she will deign to come forth.”
The young man who spoke was chief of the dances, and why should he not suggest such a thing? So, his friends and followers agreeing, they began to make plumes of macaw feathers﻿—beautiful plumes they were﻿—for the Plume dance. They set a day, and on that day, in the morning, they danced, with music and song, in the plaza before the house of the great priest-chief where the girl lived. They looked along the top of the house in vain; the girl was not there; only her old parents sat on the roof.
“Oh! I’m so thirsty!” cried the chief of the dance, for he it was who wanted to see the girl.
“Run right in and get a drink,” said the girl’s old ones. So the young man climbed the ladder and went into the first room. There was no water there; then he went into the second room, but there was no water there; then into the third room, but still he found no water. He looked all around, but saw nothing of the priest-chief’s daughter. All the same, she was back in the fourth room, sitting there just as if no dance were going on in the plaza, weaving away at her beautiful trays of colored splints.

===Chinese===

今天我要给你们讲的，是住在雷霆山下鹰之乡（Kʻiákime）的一个人。就这么讲吧。那是古老而久被遗忘的时代，是远古得无从猜度的时代。那时，这镇上住着一位大祭司首领的女儿，可她打从小孩子时起，就从来没有、从来没有、从来没有走出过自家家门。镇上没人见过她；连她自己的镇民也没见过她。
日复一日，每到正午，太阳悬在中天时，他就从天上透过她家屋顶的一扇小天窗往下望。而他正是顷刻间便成为她情郎的那位；他沿着自己的光线铺成的、亮晃晃的金黄路径降下来，与她说话。他是她唯一的伴，因为她不认得自己的镇民，从小也没见过他们。只有她的父母见过她。
“不知道首领家的孩子长什么样，”人们彼此议论。“她从不出来；打小就没人见过她。”于是他们终于想法子要看她一眼。有人说：“有了！咱们为她办一场舞会吧。说不定她就肯出来了。”
说话的年轻人是舞会之首，他有什么不能提的？于是朋友们随从们一商量，便开始用金刚鹦鹉的羽毛制作羽饰——真是漂亮的羽饰——为羽舞做准备。他们定下日子。那天早晨，他们在少女所住的大祭司首领家宅前的广场上，伴着乐声与歌声跳起舞来。他们朝房顶张望，白费劲；少女不在，只有她年迈的父母坐在屋顶上。
“哎呀，渴死我了！”舞会之首喊道，因为正是他想见那少女。
“跑进来喝口水吧，”少女家的老人说。于是年轻人爬上梯子，进了第一间屋。那里没有水；他进了第二间屋，也没有水；又进第三间，还是没找到水。他四下看，祭司首领的女儿影儿也没有。可她就在最里面第四间屋坐着，仿佛广场上根本没有什么舞会在进行，只管编织她那彩篾的美丽托盘。

===Original===

Well, the young man went back; they finished their dance, but no one saw anything of the priest-chief’s daughter; and when the dancers all returned to their ceremonial chamber they said to one another: “Alas! although we danced for her, she came not out to see us!”
Now, in reality, the Sun, who was her lover, and came down each day on a ray of his own light to visit her, loved her so much he would not that she should come forth from her house and be seen of men. Therefore he set an Eagle upon the housetop in a great cage to watch her. He was a very wise old Eagle. He could understand every word that the people said. And he it was that she fed and watered from day to day. Now, the dancers in the ceremonial chamber asked: “What shall we do?”
“Why, let us dance again,” said the chief of the dances, “and if we do not succeed, yet again.” They did as he said, but with no better success than before; so at last the two Warrior Priests of the Bow grew angry, and although they were the girl’s father’s own warriors, they ordered the Warrior festival, or *Óinahe* dance. “Surely,” said they, “she will come forth, and if not, let her perish, for how can she refuse the delight of the great *Óinahe*, where each young man dances and masks himself according to his fancy?”
So, one night the two warriors went out and called to the people to make ready and be happy, for in four days they should dance the *Óinahe*. When they had done calling, they descended, and the people said to one another: “Surely she will come out when we dance the *Óinahe*, for she will be delighted with it, and we shall yet see her. She was very beautiful when she was a little girl.” Then both of the warriors climbed to the top of Thunder Mountain, where Áhaiyúta and his brother, Mátsailéma, the Gods of War, and their grandmother lived in the middle of the summit. As they approached the presence of the two gods, they exclaimed: “*She-e!*”
“*Hai!*” the gods replied.
“Our fathers, how is it that ye are, these many days?” they asked, and the Twain replied: “We are happy. Come in; sit down”; and they placed a couple of stools for the warriors. “What is it that ye would of us?” they continued; “for it would be strange if ye came up to our house for nothing.”
“True it is,” replied the warriors.

===Chinese===

年轻人只好回去了；他们跳完了舞，谁也没见到祭司首领的女儿。舞者们回到祭祀屋，彼此叹道：“唉！我们为她跳舞，她却不出来看我们！”
其实，太阳——她的情郎，每天乘着自己的一道光线下来看她——太爱她了，不愿她走出家门被世人看见。因此他在她家房顶上安置了一只鹰（Eagle），关在大笼里看守她。那是一只极有智慧的老鹰，人们说的话他句句都懂。她天天喂他食、喂他水。这时祭祀屋里的舞者们问：“我们该怎么办？”
“唔，再跳一回呗，”舞会之首说，“不成，就再跳。”他们照他说的做了，成效不比上回好；末了，两位弓箭武士祭司动了怒，他们虽是那少女父亲自家的武士，却下令举办武士节，即奥伊纳赫（Óinahe）舞。“这回她准出来，”他们说，“再不出来，就让她死——伟大的奥伊纳赫，人人任意装扮戴面具起舞，她怎能拒绝这份欢愉？”
于是一天夜里，两位武士出去晓谕众人，让大家准备、欢喜起来，因为四天之后要跳奥伊纳赫。他们晓谕完毕下了房顶，人们彼此说：“跳奥伊纳赫时她准会出来，她一定会喜欢的，咱们就能见着她了。她小时候可俊了。”随后两位武士爬上雷霆山顶，阿海尤塔（Áhaiyúta）和他弟弟马赛莱马（Mátsailéma）这两位战神，同他们的祖母住在山顶正中。他们走近两位神明跟前，喊道：“谢——咦！”
“嗨！”二神应道。
“我们的父啊，这些天你们可安好？”他们问。二神答道：“我们很快活。进来，坐下。”他们为两位武士安了两只凳子。“你们找我们何事？”二神接着说；“无事却上我们家来，那才怪呢。”
“正是，”武士们答道。

===Original===

“It is in our hearts as your two chosen children﻿—as the war-priests of our nation﻿—that our people should be made happy as the days of the year go by; and we therefore think over all the beautiful dances, and now and then command that the most fitting of them shall appear. Now, our children, the people of the Home of the Eagles, are anxious to see our child, the daughter of the priest-chief, who has not come forth from her house, and whom we have never seen since she was a little girl. We have thought to order your dance of the *Óinahe*, and we would that without fail our daughter should be made to come forth or else die; therefore, our fathers, we have come to consult ye and to ask your advice.”
“Aha!” cried the Twain. “Then ye are anxious that this should be, are ye?”
“Yes,” they replied.
“Well, it shall come to pass as ye wish it, and the girl must die if she come not forth at the bidding of the *Óinahe*!”
“Aha!” ejaculated they both. “Thanks!”
“Yea, it shall be as ye wish. Make our days for us﻿—name the times for preparation, and we shall be with ye to lead the *Óinahe*. The first time our dance will come forth, and the second time our dance will come forth, and the third time our dance will come forth, but the fourth time our dance comes forth, it will happen as ye wish it. It will certainly be finished as ye wish it.”
“Well! Thanks; we go!” (goodbye).
“Go ye,” said the gods to their children; and they went.
The Eagle was very unhappy with all this. He knew it all, for he understood everything that was said. Next morning he hung his head at the window with great sadness; so the girl, after she had eaten her morning meal, took some dainty bits to the window and said: “Why are you so unhappy? See, I have brought you some food. Eat!”
“I will not eat; I cannot eat,” replied the Eagle.
“Why not?” asked she. “I will not harm you; I am happy; I love you just as much as ever.”
“Alas, alas! my mother,” said the Eagle. “It is not with thoughts of myself that I am unhappy, but your father’s two war-priests are anxious that their children shall be made happy, and their children, the people of our town under the mountain, are longing to see you. They have said to one another that you never come forth; they have never seen you. Therefore they have ordered the *Óinahe*, that you may be tempted out.

===Chinese===

“我们心里想的是——身为你们拣选的两个孩子、我们国家的战祭司——该让我们的百姓随着一年年日子过去常保欢喜；所以我们把种种美舞都想了一遍，时时命其中最合宜的上场。如今我们的孩子，鹰之乡的百姓，渴望见一见我们的孩子、祭司首领的女儿；她从不出家门，我们打她小时候就没见过她。我们想下令办你们的奥伊纳赫舞，而且定要让我们这位女儿出来，否则便死；因此，我们的父啊，我们来请教你们，请你们拿个主意。”
“啊哈！”二神喊。“这么说，你们巴望这事成，是吧？”
“是，”他们答。
“好，定如你们所愿；她若不奉奥伊纳赫之命出来，就必须死！”
“啊哈！”两人齐声喊。“多谢！”
“是，定如你们所愿。为我们定日子吧——指定准备的天数，我们届时与你们同在，领奥伊纳赫之舞。我们的舞第一次出场，第二次出场，第三次出场；到第四次出场，事情就照你们的愿望成就。必定照你们的愿望了结。”
“好！多谢；我们去了！”（道别）
“去吧，”二神对他们的孩子说；他们便去了。
这一切让鹰非常难过。他全知道，因为人们说的话他全懂。第二天早晨，他把头垂在天窗口，十分悲伤；少女吃过早饭，便拿了些精美的吃食到窗口，说：“你怎么这样不快活？瞧，我给你带了吃的。吃吧！”
“我不吃；吃不下，”鹰答。
“怎么啦？”她问。“我不会害你；我快活着呢；我还跟从前一样疼你。”
“唉，唉！我的母亲，”鹰说。“我不是为自己难过。是你们父亲的两位战祭司，一心要让他们的孩子们欢喜；他们的孩子——我们山下镇上的百姓——渴望见你。他们彼此说，你从不出来；他们从没见过你。所以他们办了奥伊纳赫，好把你引出来。

===Original===

They went up to the home of Áhaiyúta and his younger brother, where they live with their grandmother, on the top of Thunder Mountain, and the two gods have said to them: ‘It shall come to pass as ye wish it.’ Therefore they will dance, and on the fourth day of their dancing it shall come to pass as they wish it. Indeed, it shall happen, my poor mother, that you shall be no more. Alas! I can do nothing; you can do nothing; why should I tarry longer with you? You must loosen my bonds and let me free.”
“As you like,” said the girl. “I suppose it must be as you say.” Then she loosened the Eagle’s bonds, and, straight as the pathway of an arrow, away he flew upward into the sky﻿—even toward the zenith where the Sun rested at noon-time, and whither he soon arrived himself.
“Thou comest,” said the Sun.
“I do, my father. How art thou these many days?” said the Eagle to the Sun.
“Happy. Here, sit down.” There was a blanket already placed for him, and thereupon he sat; but he never looked to the right nor to the left, nor yet about the Sun-father’s splendid home. He said not a word. He only drooped his head, so sad was he.
“What is it, my child?” asked the Sun. “I suppose thou hast some errand, else why shouldst thou come? Surely it is not for nothing that thou wouldst come so far to see me.”
“Quite true,” answered the Eagle. “Alas! my child; alas, my mother! Day after day down in the home under the mountain the people dance that they may tempt her forth; yet she has never appeared. So her father’s war-priests are angry and have at last been to see the Twain in their home on Thunder Mountain, and the Twain have commanded that soon it shall come to pass as the people wish or that our beautiful maiden shall perish. Even tomorrow it shall be; so have the Twain said; and when the fourth dance comes out it shall come to pass, and our beautiful maiden shall be no more; thus have the Twain said. I cannot enrich my mother, the daughter of the priest-chief, thy beautiful child, with words of advice, with aid of mine own will; hence come I unto thee. What shall I do?”
“What shalt thou do?” repeated the Sun. “I know it is all as thou hast said. Know I not all these things? The Twain, whose powers are surpassed only by mine own, have they not commanded that it shall be? What shalt thou do but descend at once?

===Chinese===

他们上了阿海尤塔和他弟弟的家，二神同祖母住在雷霆山顶；二神对他们说：‘定如你们所愿。’所以他们要跳舞了，跳到第四天，事就要照他们的心愿成就。真的会发生那样的事，我可怜的母亲——你将不在了。唉！我无能为力；你也无能为力；我又何必在你这儿多耽搁？你必须解开我的绑绳，放我走。”
“随你吧，”少女说。“我想必得依你。”于是她解开了鹰的绑绳，鹰像离弦之箭的路径一样笔直，向上一飞冲天——直奔正午太阳安歇的天顶，不久便到了。
“你来了，”太阳说。
“来了，我的父。这些天您可安好？”鹰对太阳说。
“快活。来，坐下。”那里早已为他铺好一张毯子，他坐下了；但他既不左顾右盼，也不去看太阳之父辉煌的居所。他一言不发，只是垂着头，伤心极了。
“怎么了，我的孩子？”太阳问。“我想你必有来意，不然何必前来？大老远跑来看我，总不会没事吧。”
“正是，”鹰答。“唉！我的孩子；唉，我的母亲！山下家中的百姓日复一日地跳舞，想引她出来；可她从不露面。她父亲的战祭司们恼了，终于上了雷霆山二神的家；二神已下令：要么很快遂百姓所愿，要么我们的美少女灭亡。甚至就在明天；二神是这样说的；第四次舞蹈出场时，事就成就，我们的美少女就不在了；二神是这样说的。我无法用劝告的话、用我自己的力量帮助我的母亲——祭司首领的女儿、你美丽的孩子；所以我到你这儿来。我该怎么办？”
“你该怎么办？”太阳重复道。“我知道一切如你所说。这些事我岂有不知？二神，其权能唯逊于我，不是已经下令如此了吗？你除了立刻下去，还能怎么办？

===Original===

Tell her to bathe herself and put on her finest garments tomorrow morning. Then, when the time comes, mount her upon thy shoulders and bear her up to me. Only possibly thou wilt have the great good fortune to reach my house with her. Possibly in thy journey hither it shall come to be, alas! as the Twain have said; for have not they said it should be, and are they not above all things else powerful?”
“Well, we’ll try to come.”
“But I will watch thee when thou art about to reach the mid-heavens.”
“Well, I go,” said the Eagle, rising.
“Very well,” responded the Sun; “happily mayest thou journey.” And the Eagle began to descend.
Meanwhile the daughter of the priest-chief opened the sky-hole and placed a sacred medicine-bowl half full of water on the floor where the sunlight would shine into it, and where it would reflect the sky, and there she sat looking intently down into the water. By-and-by the Eagle came in sight, and she saw his shadow in the water.
Just then the Sun drew his shield from his face. Oh! how hot it was down there on the earth. The sky was ablaze with light, and no one dared to look at it; and the sands grew so hot that they burned the moccasins of those who walked upon them. Everybody ran into the houses, and the Eagle spread his wings and gently descended, for he too was hot. And when he came near to the house, the girl let him in and welcomed him.
“Thou comest, father,” said she.
He only drooped his head and flapped his wings, unable even to speak, so hot was he.
She saw that he was near to fainting. Therefore she fanned him﻿—made cool wind for him with the basket tray and her mantle﻿—and sprinkled cold water upon his head.
“Thou hast been to the home of our father?” she asked, when he had recovered.
“Yes,” replied the Eagle.
“What has he advised that we should do?” asked she.
“This,” said the Eagle; “tomorrow morning at the dawn of day thou wilt arise and bathe thyself. Then at sunrise thou shalt put on thy finest garments. The dance will come forth; and then it will come forth the second time, and the third time, and again it will come the fourth time. Then I will mount thee upon my shoulders and bear thee away toward the Sun, who will be waiting for us. It may be that we shall have the good fortune to reach his home; and it may be that we shall get only a little way when everything shall come to pass unhappily and thou wilt be no more.”

===Chinese===

告诉她明日清晨沐浴，穿上她最美的衣裳。时候一到，把她驮上你的双肩，带到我这儿来。只是，你能不能带着她到达我家，就要看你的大造化了。也许在来我这儿的路上，唉！就会照二神所说的成就；他们不是说了吗？他们的权能岂不是超越一切？”
“好，我们尽力来。”
“你快到中天时，我会注视你们。”
“好，我去了，”鹰说着起身。
“很好，”太阳应道；“愿你一路平安。”鹰开始下降。
与此同时，祭司首领的女儿打开天窗，在阳光照得进来的地板上放了一只盛了半盂水的神圣药碗，碗中映着天空；她坐在那里，凝神望着水面。不久，鹰进入了视野，她看见了他的影子映在水中。
就在这时，太阳把盾牌从脸上移开了。哎呀！地面上多么炎热。天空一片炽亮，谁也不敢仰视；沙地烫得烧脚，走在上面的人鹿皮鞋都烤焦了。人人都躲进屋里；鹰张开翅膀，缓缓降落，因为他也热坏了。他走近房舍，少女放他进来，欢迎他。
“你回来了，父亲，”她说。
他只垂着头、扑扇着翅膀，话都说不出来，热成那样。
她见他快要晕倒了，便给他打扇——用篮盘和披风为他扇出凉风——又往他头上洒凉水。
“你到我们父亲的家中去了？”他缓过来后，她问。
“去了，”鹰答。
“他嘱咐我们怎么办？”她问。
“这样，”鹰说；“明晨天一亮你就起身沐浴。日出时穿上你最华美的衣裳。舞蹈要出场；第一次出场，第二次，第三次，接着第四次出场。那时我把你驮上肩，朝太阳带你去，他会在那儿等我们。也许我们有大造化，能到他家里；也许我们走不出多远，一切就不幸应验，你就不在了。”

===Original===

That is what he said to her.
It grew night. The girl collected all the basket-trays that she had made for her father’s sacred plumes; these by the firelight she spread out, and then began to divide them into different heaps.
Now, her parents, who were sitting in the next room, heard her until it was late at night, and they said to each other: “Wonder what it is that keeps our daughter up?” So the old priest-chief arose and entered her room.
“My child, art thou not at rest yet?” asked he.
“No,” replied she. “I am dividing the trays I have made for thee. These,” said she, pointing to a heap of yellow ones, “shall pertain to the north-land; these, the blue, to the west-land; the red to the land of the south, the white to the east, the variegated to the upper regions, and the black to the regions below. For tomorrow, beloved father, thou shalt see me no more.”
“It is well,” said the father, for he was a great priest and knew the will of the gods, and to this he always said: “It is well. What, therefore, should I say?” So the old man left her.
Then as morning approached she bathed herself. And the Eagle, looking down, said: “My child, my mother, lie down and rest thyself, for we are about to undertake a long journey. Never fear; I will wake thee at the right time.” So she lay down and slept. The Eagle perched himself above her and watched for the dawn.
By-and-by the great star arose. Then he knew that the Sun would soon follow it, and he said: “Mother, arise! dress thyself, for the time is near at hand.”
Outside on the housetops called the two war-priests to their children:

> “Hasten, hasten! Prepare for the dance!

Hasten, hasten! Eat for the dance!

Hasten, hasten, our children all!”

===Chinese===

他就是这么对她说的。
天黑了。少女把为父亲的神羽编的所有篮盘收拢来，借着火光摊开，开始分成一堆一堆。
她的父母在隔壁屋听见她忙到深夜，彼此说：“咱们闺女熬夜忙什么呢？”老祭司首领便起身进了她的屋。
“孩子，还不歇着？”
“不歇，”她答。“我在分给你编的盘子。这些，”她指着一堆黄色的说，“归于北方之地；这些蓝的，归于西方之地；红的归于南方之地，白的归于东方，斑斓的归于上方诸境，黑的归于下方诸境。因为明天，亲爱的父亲，你就再见不着我了。”
“那就这样吧，”父亲说；他是大祭司，懂得神明的旨意，对这样的事他总说：“好。我还有什么可说的呢？”老人便离开了她。
天将亮时，她沐浴了。鹰往下望着说：“我的孩子，我的母亲，躺下歇歇吧，我们要赶很远的路。别怕；到时候我会叫醒你。”于是她躺下睡了。鹰栖在她上方，守着天明。
不久，那颗大星升起来了。他知道太阳随后就到，便说：“母亲，起来！穿上衣裳，时候到了。”
外面屋顶上，两位战祭司朝他们的孩子们喊：

> “快快！准备跳舞！

快快！吃饱跳舞！

快快，我们所有的孩子！”

===Original===

Then the girl went into another room and brought forth her finest dresses, and these, garment after garment, she put on﻿—not one dress, but many. Upon her shoulders she placed four mantles of snow-white embroidered cotton. Then she said to the Eagle: “Wait a moment; I have yet to think of our children in the Home of the Eagles.” Therefore she brought forth her basket-bowls of fine meal with which she had been accustomed to powder her face. There was meal of the yellow corn, the blue cornmeal, the red cornmeal, the white cornmeal, the speckled cornmeal, and the black cornmeal. “See,” said she, as she regarded the various vessels of meal; “my children, by means of these shall ye beautify flesh; by means of these be precious against evil; by means of these shall ye finish preciously your roads of life. I am to be no more. Far off and to an unknown region go I. Possibly I may reach it, and live; probably not reach it, and die. These do I leave as your inheritance. My children, goodbye.”40
Then the Eagle descended. The drum began to sound outside; the dance was coming﻿—for the first time, mind you, not the fourth. Then said the Eagle, as he lowered himself: “Place thyself upon my back; grasp me by the shoulders.” And the girl did as she was bidden. She reclined herself lengthwise on the back of the Eagle, and grasped with her left hand his shoulders. “Now, place one foot on one of my thighs and the other on the other.” She placed one foot on one of his thighs and the other on the other; and the Eagle spread his tail and raised it that she might not fall off. “All ready?” asked he, as the drum of the coming dance sounded outside.
“Yes,” said the girl; and they arose.
“Open the wicket!” and *shoa!* the Eagle spread his wings and away off up into the sky he sprang with the maiden. Round and round, round and round, they circled in the sky, but those below saw nothing as they danced in the shadows of the great houses. The dancers retired. Then they came forth again. Again they retired and came forth. Then the girl said: “Father, slower. Let me sing a farewell song to my people, my children of Earth, that they may know I am going.”
The Eagle spread his wings and sailed gently through the air as the maiden sang. Then the people in the plaza below heard the song, and said: “Alas, alas! ye Twain!” said they to the two gods who led the dance.

===Chinese===

于是少女进了另一间屋，取出她最美的衣裙，一件接一件地穿上——不是一件，而是许多件。肩上又披了四条雪白的绣花棉披肩。然后她对鹰说：“稍等；我还得想着我们鹰之乡的孩子们。”于是她取出那些她惯用来扑脸的细面篮钵。有黄玉米面、蓝玉米面、红玉米面、白玉米面、斑玉米面、黑玉米面。“看，”她望着那一钵钵面粉说；“我的孩子们，靠这些，你们将为肉体添美；靠这些，你们将珍贵地抵御凶邪；靠这些，你们将珍贵地走完生命的路途。我要不在了。我去的地方很远，是未知的疆域。也许我到得了，活着；多半到不了，死去。这些留给你们作遗产。我的孩子们，别了。”40
随后鹰降下来。外面鼓声响起；舞蹈来了——记住，是第一次，不是第四次。鹰一面往下俯，一面说：“伏在我背上；抓住我的双肩。”少女依言而行。她顺着鹰的背伏下，左手抓住他的双肩。“现在，一只脚踩我一条腿。”她一只脚踩上他一条腿，另一只脚踩上另一条；鹰展开并竖起尾巴，好叫她不致滑落。“都齐了？”外面传来舞蹈临近的鼓声，他问。
“齐了，”少女说；他们腾空而起。
“打开天窗！”喇！鹰张开双翅，带着少女一跃冲天。他们在天上转了一圈又一圈，一圈又一圈；下面的人在大房屋的阴影里跳舞，什么也没瞧见。舞者退场。他们又出场。又退场，又出场。这时少女说：“父亲，慢些。让我对我的族人、我大地的孩子们，唱一支告别歌，好叫他们知道我要走了。”
鹰张开翅膀，缓缓地在空中滑翔，少女唱起歌来。下面广场上的人们听见了歌声，说：“唉，唉！你们二位！”他们对着领舞的二神喊。

===Original===

“Our mother, our child, away off through the skies goes she! Ye are fools that ye have let her escape and deceive us!”
Some listened to the song and learned it. Others did not. For the third time the dancers came forth. “Once more have we to dance,” said the two gods. “Where are they now?”
“In the mid-heavens,” said the people.
“Take it easily, my child,” said the Eagle. “Once more are they to come forth. Possibly we will yet have the great good fortune to reach the home of our father.” And they sped along through the air, nearer and nearer to the home of the Sun-father, while the dancers below danced harder and harder﻿—many so joyful that they listened not to the complainings of the people around, but danced only more vigorously.
Then the dancers retired and came out for the fourth and last time. In the van danced the two gods, their faces blackened with the paint of war, their hands bearing bows and arrows with which to destroy the daughter of the priest-chief.
Yes, they were almost there. Now, the Eagle’s heart was high with hope. When the two gods below reached the center of the plaza they turned to the people and asked: “Where are they? Where have they gone?”
“There they are in the skies﻿—almost there,” replied the people.
“Humph!” responded the gods. “Suppose they *are* almost there; they shall never reach the home of our father!”
“Now, then, hurry, brother younger!” exclaimed the elder; “with which hand wilt thou draw the arrow?”
“With thy hand, my right,” said the younger.
“Very well; with thy hand, my left,” said the elder.41
So they drew their medicine-pointed arrows to the heads. *Tsi‑ni‑i‑i!* sang the arrows as they shot through the air. Soon they reached the home of the Sun, crossed one another over his face, and shot downward more swiftly than ever toward the coming Eagle and the maiden. “Alas! my mother, my child,” said the Sun as the arrows flew past him and from him, “thou art no more.” And the arrows shot downward on their course.
*Tsook!* sang the arrow of the elder god as it pierced the back of the girl and entered her heart. *Tso‑ko!* sang the arrow of the younger as it struck in the middle of her back.
“Alas! my mother, my mother,” cried the Eagle, “it is over, alas, alas!” said he, as she released her hold, and, fainting, he left her to fall through the air.

===Chinese===

“我们的母亲，我们的孩子，她远远地飞过天去了！你们是傻子，竟让她逃脱，骗过了我们！”
有人听了那歌，学会了；有人没学会。舞者第三次出场。“我们还须跳一回，”二神说。“他们这会儿在哪儿？”
“在中天，”人们说。
“放从容些，我的孩子，”鹰说。“他们还要出场一回。也许我们还有大造化，能到我们父亲的家里。”他们在空中疾飞，越来越接近太阳之父的居所；下面的舞者越跳越急——许多人欢喜得只顾跳，全不理会周围人的哀叹，跳得愈发起劲。
随后舞者退场，第四次、也是最后一次出场。二神在队伍前头舞蹈，脸上涂着战漆，手里持着弓箭，要用它们除灭祭司首领的女儿。
是的，他们差不多快到了。这时鹰的心中希望高涨。下面两位神到了广场中央，转身问众人：“他们在哪儿？去哪儿了？”
“在那边天上——就快到了，”人们答。
“哼！”二神应道。“就算快到了；他们永远到不了我们父亲的家！”
“那么，快点，弟弟！”兄长喊；“你用哪只手拉箭？”
“用你的手，我的右手，”弟弟说。
“很好；用你的手，我的左手，”兄长说。41
于是他们把药镞箭拉到了满头。箭破空而行，唱着：嘰——咿——咿——！不久箭到了太阳的家门，在他脸上方交叉而过，比来时更快地朝迎面而来的鹰和少女直射下去。“唉！我的母亲，我的孩子，”箭从他身边飞过、离他而去时，太阳说，“你不在了。”箭循着路径继续下射。
嘯！兄长之箭穿透少女后背、扎进她心里，箭这样唱着。嘯嘎！弟弟之箭射进她背脊正中，箭这样唱着。
“唉！我的母亲，我的母亲，”鹰哭喊，“完了，唉，唉！”他说着，她松开了手；鹰晕了过去，任她从空中坠落。

===Original===

Over and over, this way and that, fell the beautiful maiden; and as the people strained their eyes, nearer and nearer to the town neath the mountain she fell. Soon, over and over, this way and that, she came falling even with the top of the mountain.
Then the people rushed past one another out of the plaza toward the place where they thought she would strike. And just over there below the Home of the Eagles, where the Waters of the Coyote gush forth from the cliff-base, fell the beautiful maiden.
Then there were born twin children﻿—two wee infants who rolled off into the rubbish and were concealed under sticks and stones.
Down rushed the people, and an Acoma spectator seized her body. “Mine!” cried he, triumphantly, as he raised the body above him.
“Thine!” cried the people, for they had lost the beautiful maiden.
“Ours!” cried the Acomas, one to another, who had come to witness the dances. “Great good fortune this day has smiled on us.” And they bore her body away to their pueblo in the east.
Now, under the other end of Thunder Mountain was the home of the Badgers, and an old Badger who lived there was out hunting. After the people had again gathered in the city, he passed near the Waters of the Coyote and heard the voices of the infants crying among the rubbish.
“Ah!” said he, “I hear the cry of children. My little boys, my little girls,” cried he, “whichever ye may be”; and he hastily searched and found them where they were rolling about and crying among the refuse. “Twins!” cried he. “Boys! Somebody has left them here. Soon he will come back to reclaim them. Let me walk away for a few moments.”
So he walked all around, but found no traces of the parents, only the tracks of many men who had gathered near.
“Mine!” said he, as he trotted back; and with soft grass he rubbed them till they were free from the mud and refuse. “Thanks, thanks! Splendid! Children have I, and boys at that, and when I am older grown they will take from me the cares of the chase. Goodness! Thanks!
