Then she was about to rest when suddenly the boys pulled the strings, and the demon sprang up before her, seemingly as well as ever. Again the old woman fell to, but her strokes kept getting feebler and feebler, her breath shorter and shorter, until her wind went out and she fell to the ground.
How the boys did laugh and roll on the ground when the old grandmother moaned: “Alas! alas! This day﻿—my day﻿—light is﻿—cut off﻿—and my wind of life﻿—fast going.”
The old woman covered her head with her tattered mantle; but when she found that Átahsaia did not move, she raised her eyes and looked through a rent. There were her two grandsons rolling and kicking on the grass and holding their mouths with both hands, their eyes swollen and faces red with laughter. Then she suddenly looked for the demon. There lay the skin, all torn and battered out of shape.
“So ho! you pesky wretches; that’s the way you treat me, is it? Well! never again will I help you, never!” she snapped, “nor shall you ever live with me more!” Whereupon the old woman jumped up and hobbled away.
But little did the brothers care. They laughed till she was far away, and then said one to the other: “It is done!”
---
Since that time, the grandmother has gone, no one knows where. But Áhaiyúta and Mátsailéma are the bright stars of the morning and evening, just in front of and behind the Sun-father himself.

===Chinese===

老太太喊道；她跑得比先前更快，直到跑得够近，看清她可怜的孙子快累瘫了，马赛莱马连战棒都丢了。“把脚站定——孩子们——等——一下，”老太太喘着，勃然大怒，抡起拨火棍扑向那皮人，一顿猛捣，捣得干草里尘土直冒，那皮子疼得似的缩成一团。
马赛莱马在草里打滚蹬腿；阿海尤塔也笑得撑不住，只好让塞了草的魔头瘫倒在地。可老太太不肯停。她一边痛打魔头，一边咒他食人的心，骂他追她孙子就落得这下场，骂一句这个、骂一句那个，啪！啪！不住手，直到她认定这妖怪准死了。她正要歇口气，两个孩子忽然一扯线，魔头在她跟前蹦了起来，看上去安然如初。老太太又扑上去打；可她的棍越来越轻，气越来越短，终于一口气没上来，瘫倒在地。
老太太呻吟着“唉呀！唉呀！今天——我的日子——亮光——被铰断了——我生命的气——快没了”，两个孩子笑得在地下打滚。
老太太用破披风蒙住头；可她发觉阿塔赛亚不动了，便抬起眼从破洞里望。只见两个孙子正在草里打滚蹬腿，双手捂着嘴，眼睛笑得肿了，脸笑得通红。她猛地去找那魔头。皮子瘫在那儿，打得稀烂，走了形。
“好啊！你们这两个小讨债鬼；就这么作弄我，是吧？好！我再不管你们了，永远不管！”她厉声说，“也永远别想再跟我住！”说罢老太太跳起来，一瘸一拐走了。
兄弟俩才不在乎呢。他们笑到她走远，才彼此说一声：“成了！”
---
打那时起，祖母走了，没人知道去了哪里。可阿海尤塔和马赛莱马成了晨星和昏星，恰恰在太阳之父本人的前头和后头。

===Original===

Yet their spirits hover over their shrines on Thunder Mountain and the Mount of the Beloved, they say, or linger over the Middle of the World, forever to guide the games and to guard the warriors of the Land of Zuni. Thus it was in the days of the ancients.
Thus shortens my story.

===Chinese===

人们说，他们的魂灵还盘桓在雷霆山和受爱者之山的神祠上，或流连于世界的中央，永远引导祖尼之地的游戏、守护祖尼之地的武士。先民时代，事情就是这样。
我的故事，就此收梢。

## The Cock and the Mouse / 公鸡与老鼠
===Original===



===Chinese===

​

### Note / 说明
===Original===

While on their pilgrimage to the “Ocean of Sunrise” in the summer of 1886, three Zunis﻿—Pálowahtiwa, Waíhusiwa, and Héluta﻿—with Mr. Cushing, were entertaining their assembled friends at Manchester-by-the-Sea with folk tales, those related by the Indians being interpreted by Mr. Cushing as they were uttered. When Mr. Cushing’s turn came for a story he responded by relating the Italian tale of “The Cock and the Mouse” which appears in Thomas Frederick Crane’s *Italian Popular Tales*. About a year later, at Zuni, but under somewhat similar circumstances, Waíhusiwa’s time came to entertain the gathering, and great was Mr. Cushing’s surprise when he presented a Zuni version of the Italian tale. Mr. Cushing translated the story as literally as possible, and it is here reproduced, together with Mr. Crane’s translation from the Italian, in order that the reader may not only see what transformation the original underwent in such a brief period, and how well it has been adapted to Zuni environment and mode of thought, but also to give a glimpse of the Indian method of folktale making.
—﻿Editor.

===Chinese===

1886年夏天，三位祖尼人——帕洛瓦蒂瓦（Pálowahtiwa）、瓦伊胡西瓦（Waíhusiwa）和赫卢塔（Héluta）——同卡欣先生一起，前往“日出之洋”朝圣。在海滨曼彻斯特（Manchester-by-the-Sea），他们为聚会的朋友们讲民间故事助兴，印第安人一边讲，卡欣先生一边随口译出。轮到卡欣先生讲故事时，他讲了意大利故事《公鸡与老鼠》，该故事见于托马斯·弗雷德里克·克莱恩（Thomas Frederick Crane）的《意大利民间故事》（Italian Popular Tales）。约一年之后，在祖尼，情形多少相似，轮到瓦伊胡西瓦为聚会助兴；他竟端出一个祖尼版的意大利故事，卡欣先生大为惊讶。卡欣先生把这个故事尽可能逐字地译了出来，在此连同克莱恩先生从意大利文译出的译文一并重刊，使读者不仅可以看到原故事在这样短的时期里经历了怎样的变形、被何等巧妙地改造成了祖尼的环境与思维方式，也可以约略窥见印第安人制作民间故事的方法。
——编者

### Italian Version / 意大利版本
===Original===

Once upon a time there were a cock and a mouse. One day the mouse said to the cock: “Friend Cock, shall we go and eat some nuts on yonder tree?” “As you like.” So they both went under the tree and the mouse climbed up at once and began to eat. The poor cock began to fly, and flew and flew, but could not come where the mouse was. When it saw that there was no hope of getting there, it said: “Friend Mouse, do you know what I want you to do? Throw me a nut.” The mouse went and threw one and hit the cock on the head. The poor cock, with its head all broken and covered with blood, went away to an old woman. “Old aunt, give me some rags to cure my head.” “If you will give me two hairs I will give you the rags.” The cock went away to a dog. “Dog, give me two hairs; the hairs I will give the old woman; the old woman will give me rags to cure my head.” “If you will give me a little bread,” said the dog, “I will give you the hairs.” The cock went away to a baker. “Baker, give me bread; I will give bread to the dog; the dog will give hairs; the hairs I will carry to the old woman; the old woman will give me rags to cure my head.” The baker answered: “I will not give you bread unless you give me some wood.” The cock went away to the forest. “Forest, give me some wood; the wood I will carry to the baker; the baker will give me some bread; the bread I will give to the dog; the dog will give me hairs; the hairs I will carry to the old woman; the old woman will give me rags to cure my head.” The forest answered: “If you will bring me a little water, I will give you some wood.” The cock went away to a fountain. “Fountain, give me water; water I will carry to the forest; forest will give wood; wood I will carry to the baker; baker will give bread; bread I will give dog; dog will give hairs; hairs I will give old woman; old woman will give rags to cure my head.” The fountain gave him water; the water he carried to the forest; the forest gave him wood; the wood he carried to the baker; the baker gave him bread; the bread he gave to the dog; the dog gave him the hairs; the hairs he carried to the old woman; the old woman gave him the rags; and the cock cured his head.

===Chinese===

从前有一只公鸡和一只老鼠。一天，老鼠对公鸡说：“公鸡朋友，咱们去吃那边那棵树上的坚果好吗？”“随你的便。”于是他们双双来到树下，老鼠立刻爬上去吃了起来。可怜的公鸡飞呀飞，飞了又飞，就是够不到老鼠待的地方。见没有指望上去，它便说：“老鼠朋友，你知道我想请你做什么吗？扔给我一颗坚果。”老鼠过去扔下一颗，正砸在公鸡头上。可怜的公鸡，头砸破了，满是血，去找一位老婆婆。“老婆婆，给我几块布片，好治我的头。”“你给我两根头发，我就给你布片。”公鸡去找狗。“狗，给我两根头发；头发我给老婆婆；老婆婆给我布片治头。”狗说：“你给我一点面包，我就给你头发。”公鸡去找面包师。“面包师，给我面包；面包我给狗；狗给头发；头发我带给老婆婆；老婆婆给我布片治头。”面包师答：“你不给我柴，我就不给你面包。”公鸡去找森林。“森林，给我柴；柴我带给面包师；面包师给我面包；面包我给狗；狗给头发；头发我带给老婆婆；老婆婆给我布片治头。”森林答：“你给我带点水来，我就给你柴。”公鸡去找泉水。“泉水，给我水；水我带给森林；森林给柴；柴我带给面包师；面包师给面包；面包我给狗；狗给头发；头发我给老婆婆；老婆婆给布片治我的头。”泉水给了它水；水它带给森林；森林给了它柴；柴它带给面包师；面包师给了它面包；面包它给了狗；狗给了它头发；头发它带给老婆婆；老婆婆给了它布片；公鸡治好了它的头。

### Zuni Version / 祖尼版本
===Original===

Thus it was in the Town of the Floods Abounding,38 long ago. There lived there an old woman, so they say, of the *Italia-kwe*,39 who, in the land of their nativity, are the parental brothers of the Mexicans, it is said. Now, after the manner of that people, this old woman had a *Tâkâkâ* Cock which she kept alone so that he would not fight the others. He was very large, like a turkey, with a fine sleek head and a bristle-brush on his breast like a turkey-cock’s too, for the *Tâkâkâ*-kind were at first the younger brothers of the Turkeys, so it would seem.
Well, the old woman kept her Cock in a little corral of tall close-set stakes, sharp at the top and wattled together with rawhide thongs, like an eagle-cage against a wall, only it had a little wicket also fastened with thongs. Now, try as he would, the old *Tâkâkâ* Cock could not fly out, for he had no chance to run and make a start as turkeys do in the wilds, yet he was ever trying and trying, because he was meat-hungry﻿—always anxious for worms;﻿—for, although the people of that village had abundant food, this old woman was poor and lived mainly on grain-foods, wherefore, perforce, she fed the old *Tâkâkâ* Cock with the refuse of her own eatings. In the morning the old woman would come and throw this refuse food into the corral cage.
Under the wall near by there lived a Mouse. He had no old grandmother to feed him, and he was particularly fond of grain food. When, having eaten his fill, the old Cock would settle down, stiff of neck and not looking this side nor that, but sitting in the sun *kâ‑tâ‑kâ‑tok*‑ing to himself, the little Mouse would dodge out, steal a bit of tortilla or a crumb, and whisk into his hole again. Being sleepy, the *Tâkâkâ* Cock never saw him, and so, day after day the Mouse fared sumptuously and grew overbold. But one day, when corn was ripe and the Cock had been well fed and was settling down to his sitting nap, the Mouse came out and stole a particularly large piece of bread, so that in trying to push it into his hole he made some noise and, moreover, had to stop and tunnel his doorway larger.
The Cock turned his head and looked just as the Mouse was working his way slowly in, and espied the long, naked tail lying there on the ground and wriggling as the Mouse moved to and fro at his digging.
“Hah! By the Grandmother of Substance, it is a worm!”

===Chinese===

上古之时，多水之镇（Town of the Floods Abounding）38就是这样。据说那里住着一位 Italia-kwe39的老妇人——据说这族人，在他们出生的故土上，是墨西哥人的亲兄弟。且说按那族人的习俗，这老妇人养着一只塔卡卡（Tâkâkâ）鸡，单独圈养，免得它同别的鸡打架。它个头很大，像只火鸡，头颈油光水滑，胸口的鬃毛像公火鸡的刷子——因为塔卡卡一族，起初似乎是火鸡们的弟弟。
老妇人把她的鸡关在一只小围栏里：密密栽着一圈高木桩，桩头削尖，用生牛皮条编扎，像靠墙的鹰笼，只是还开着一扇小门，也用皮条拴住。这老塔卡卡鸡怎么扑腾也飞不出去，因为它没有机会像野地里的火鸡那样助跑起飞；可它试了又试，因为它馋肉吃——老惦记着虫子；——那村里的人虽然吃食丰足，这老妇人却穷，主要靠粮食过活，因此只好拿自己吃剩的残渣喂这老塔卡卡鸡。早晨，老妇人来把残食扔进围栏笼里。
近旁墙根下住着一只老鼠。他没有老祖母喂养他，又特别爱吃粮食。等老公鸡吃饱蹲下来，脖子僵僵的，东也不望、西也不望，坐在太阳底下独自 kâ‑tâ‑kâ‑tok 地念叨时，小老鼠就蹿出来，偷一角玉米饼或一点饼屑，滋溜又钻回洞里。塔卡卡鸡瞌睡着，从没看见他；就这样，老鼠天天吃得肥甘，胆子越来越大。可是有一天，玉米熟了，公鸡也喂得饱饱的，正要蹲下打盹，老鼠出来偷了特别大的一块饼，往洞里塞时弄出了响动，而且还不得不停下把洞口掏大些。
公鸡回过头，正看见老鼠慢慢往洞里挤，一眼睺见那光溜溜的长尾巴拖在地上，随着老鼠来回刨土直扭动。
“哈！凭富足老祖母起誓，是条虫子！”

===Original===

cackled the Cock, and he made one peck at the Mouse’s tail and bit it so hard that he cut it entirely off and swallowed it at one gulp.
The Mouse, squeaking “Murder!” scurried down into his sleeping-place, and fell to licking his tail until his chops were all pink and his mouth was drawn down like a crying woman’s; for he loved his long tail as a young dancer loves the glory of his long hair, and he cried continually: “*Weh tsu tsu, weh tsu tse, yam hok ti-i-i!*” and thought: “Oh, that shameless great beast! By the Demon of Slave-creatures, I’ll have my payment of him! For he is worse than an owl or a nighthawk. They eat us all up, but he has taken away the very mark of my mousehood and left me to mourn it. I’ll take vengeance on him, will I!”
So, from that time the Mouse thought how he might compass it, and this plan seemed best: He would creep out some day, all maimed of tail as he was, and implore pity, and thus, perchance, make friends for a while with the *Tâkâkâ* Cock. So he took seed-down, and made a plaster of it with nut-resin, and applied it to the stump of his tail. Then, on a morning, holding his tail up as a dog does his foot when maimed by a cactus, he crawled to the edge of his hole and cried in a weak voice to the *Tâkâkâ*:

> “Ani, yoa yoa! Itâ-akʻya Mosa,

Motcho wakʻya,

Oshe wakʻya,

Ethl hâ asha ni ha. Ha na, yoa, ha na!”

> Look you, pity, pity! Master of Food Substance,

Of my maiming,

Of my hunger,

I am all but dying. Ah me, pity, ah me!

===Chinese===

公鸡咯咯叫着，朝鼠尾巴就是一啄，咬得那么狠，整条咬了下来，一口吞了。
老鼠“吱——杀人啦！”尖叫着蹿下睡洞，趴下来舔他的尾巴，舔得嘴岔子通红，嘴撇得像哭腔的女人；因为他爱他的长尾巴，就像年轻舞人爱他长发上的荣光；他哭个不停：“Weh tsu tsu, weh tsu tse, yam hok ti-i-i!”心想：“哼，这不要脸的大畜生！凭奴畜之魔起誓，我要叫他偿还！他比猫头鹰、夜鹰还坏。他们把我们整个吃掉，他却夺走了我鼠辈的标记，叫我抱恨。我要报仇，一定要！”
从此老鼠就盘算怎么成这件事，这个主意最好：哪天拖着没了尾巴的残躯爬出去，乞人怜悯，这样兴许能同塔卡卡鸡好上一阵。于是他取来种絮，用坚果树脂调成膏，糊在尾巴的断茬上。然后，一天早晨，他像被仙人掌扎伤的狗抬着脚那样翘着尾巴，爬到洞口，有气无力地朝塔卡卡哀告：

> “Ani, yoa yoa! Itâ-akʻya Mosa,

Motcho wakʻya,

Oshe wakʻya,

Ethl hâ asha ni ha. Ha na, yoa, ha na!”

> 听哪，可怜，可怜！食禄之主啊，

我遭了伤残，

我受了饥饿，

我快要死了。唉我，可怜，唉我！

===Original===

Whereupon he held up his tail, which was a safe thing to do, you see, for it no longer looked like a worm or any other eatable.
Now, the *Tâkâkâ* was flattered to be called a master of plenty, so he said, quite haughtily (for he had eaten and could not bend his neck, and felt proud, withal), “Come in, you poor little thing, and eat all you want. As if I cared for what the like of you could eat!” So the Mouse went in and ate very little, as became a polite stranger, and thanking the Cock, bade him good day and went back to his hole.
By-and-by he came again, and this time he brought part of a nutshell containing fine white meat. When he had shouted warning of his coming and entered the corral cage, he said: “Comrade father, let us eat together. Of this food I have plenty, gathered from yonder high nut-tree which I climb every autumn when the corn is ripe and cut the nuts therefrom. But of all food yours I most relish, since I cannot store such in my cellar. Now, it may be you will equally relish mine; so let us eat, then, together.”
“It is well, comrade child,” replied the Cock; so they began to eat.
But the Cock had no sooner tasted the nut than he fairly chuckled for joy, and having speedily made an end of the kernel, fell to lamenting his hard lot. “Alas, ah me!” he said. “My grandmother brings me, on rare days, something like to this, but picked all too clean. There is nought eatable so nice. Comrade little one, do you have plenty of this kind, did you say?”
“Oh, yes,” replied the Mouse; “but, you see, the season is near to an end now, and when I want more nuts I must go and gather them from the tree. Look, now! Why do you not go there also? That is the tree, close by.”
“Ah me, I cannot escape, woe to me! Look at my wings,” said the Cock, “they are worn to bristles﻿—and as to the beard on my breast, my chief ornament, alas! it is all crumpled and uneven, so much have I tried to fly out and so hard have I pushed against the bars. As for the door, my grandmother claps that shut and fastens it tightly with thongs, be you sure, as soon as ever she finishes the feeding of me!”
“Ha! ha!” exclaimed the Mouse. “If that’s all, there’s nothing easier than to open that. Look at my teeth; I even crack the hard nuts with these scrapers of mine! Wait!” He ran nimbly up the wicket and soon gnawed through the holding-string. “There!

===Chinese===

说完他翘起尾巴——你瞧，这么做稳当得很，因为它看着再也不像虫子或别的什么吃食了。
塔卡卡一听被唤作“食禄之主”，心里受用，便相当傲慢地说（他吃饱了，脖子弯不下，还自觉体面）：“进来吧，你这小可怜，想吃多少吃多少。你这样的还能吃掉我多少东西不成！”于是老鼠进去，斯斯文文只吃了一点点，像个体面的生客，谢过公鸡，道了日安，回洞去了。
过了些时他又来了，这回带来半个坚果壳，盛着精白的果仁。他喊过通报到来的话、进了围栏笼，说：“同志老爹，咱们一起吃吧。这吃食我有的是——从那边那棵高高的坚果树上采来的；每年秋天玉米熟时我都爬上去，把坚果从树上咬下来。可所有的吃食里，我最馋你这种，因为这种东西我窖里存不住。呢，兴许你也同样馋我这种；那咱们就一起吃吧。”
“这就好，同志小友。”公鸡答；于是他们吃起来。
公鸡一尝坚果，欢喜得直咯咯；三口两口把果仁吃尽，随即哀叹起自己的苦命。“唉，苦哇！”他说，“我祖母难得的日子也给我捎来一点这样的东西，可都捾得太干净了。天底下没有比这更好吃的了。小同志，你是说，这种东西你有很多？”
“哦，多的是，”老鼠答；“不过你瞧，季节眼看要完了，我再要坚果，就得亲自上树去采。看哪！你怎么不也去那儿？那就是那棵树，就在近旁。”
“唉我，我逃不出去，苦哇！瞧我的翅膀，”公鸡说，“都磨成刷子毛了——还有我胸口的须髯，我最神气的装饰，唉！全揉皱了、长短不齐了，我往外飞了那么多次，把栏杆顶得那么狠。至于门，我祖母一喂完我，哳地就关上，皮条捆得死死的，你放心！”
“哈！哈！”老鼠喊，“就这么点事？再没有比开那扇门更容易的了。瞧我的牙；我用这副刮刀连硬坚果都咬得开！等着！”他敏捷地蹿上小门，不多时就把拴绳咬断了。“喏！”

===Original===

comrade father; push open the door, you are bigger than I, and we will go nutting.”
“Thanks this day,” cried the Cock, and shoving the wicket open, he ran forth cackling and crowing for gladness.
Then the Mouse led the way to the tree. Up the trunk he ran, and climbed and climbed until he came to the topmost boughs. “Ha! the nuts are fine and ripe up here,” he shouted.
But the *Tâkâkâ* fluttered and flew all in vain; his wings were so worn he could not win even to the lowermost branches. “Oh! have pity on me, comrade child! Cut off some of the nuts and throw them down to me, do! My wings are so worn I cannot fly any better than the grandmother’s old dog, who is my neighbor over there.”
“Be patient, be patient, father!” exclaimed the Mouse. “I am cracking a big one for you as fast as I can. There, catch it!” and he threw a fat nut close to the Cock, who gleefully devoured the kernel and, without so much as thanks, called for more.
“Wait, father,” said the Mouse. “There! Stand right under me, so. Now, catch it; this is a big one!” Saying which the Mouse crawled out until he was straight over the Cock. “Now, then,” said he, “watch in front!” and he let fall the nut. It hit the Cock on the head so hard that it bruised the skin off and stunned the old *Tâkâkâ* so that he fell over and died for a short time, utterly forgetting.
“*Té mi thlo kô thlo kwa!*” shouted the Mouse, as he hurried down the tree. “A little waiting, and lo! What my foe would do to me, I to him do, indeed!” Whereupon he ran across, before ever the Cock had opened an eye, and gnawed his bristles off so short that they never could grow again. “There, now!” said the Mouse. “Lo! thus healed is my heart, and my enemy is even as he made me, bereft of distinction!” Then he ran back to his cellar, satisfied.
Finally the Cock opened his eyes. “Ah me, my head!” he exclaimed. Then, moaning, he staggered to his feet, and in doing so he espied the nut. It was smooth and round, like a brown egg. When the Cock saw it he fell to lamenting more loudly than ever: “Oh, my head! *Tâ‑kâ‑kâ‑kâ‑â‑â!*” But the top of his head kept bleeding and swelling until it was all covered over with welts of gore, and it grew so heavy, withal, that the *Tâkâkâ* thought he would surely die. So off to his grandmother he went, lamenting all the way.
Hearing him, the grandmother opened the door, and cried: “What now?”

===Chinese===

同志老爹，把门推开吧，你比我大，咱们采坚果去。”
“今日谢谢！”公鸡喊，推开小门，咯咯喔喔地欢叫着跑了出去。
于是老鼠领路到树前。他顺树干跑上去，爬呀爬，一直爬到最高的枝头。“哈！这上头的坚果又肥又熟！”他喊。
可塔卡卡扑腾了又扑腾，全是白费；翅膀磨得太狠，连最低的枝也够不着。“哦！可怜可怜我吧，同志小友！切几颗坚果扔下来给我，行行好！我的翅膀磨坏了，飞得还不如祖母那只老狗——它就住我那边。”
“耐心，耐心，老爹！”老鼠喊，“我正拼命给你磕一颗大的呢。喏，接住！”他把一颗肥坚果丢到公鸡跟前，公鸡欢天喜地吞了果仁，连谢都不谢一声，只顾讨更多的。
“等等，老爹，”老鼠说，“喏！就站在我正下方，对，就这样。现在接住；这是颗大的！”说着老鼠爬出去，直到正正当当悬在公鸡头顶。“这就来了，”他说，“看前面！”他松开坚果。坚果砸在公鸡头上，砸得那么狠，皮都砸脱了，老塔卡卡被打得昏死过去，倒地“死”了一小会儿，什么都忘了。
“Té mi thlo kô thlo kwa!”老鼠喊着，急忙溜下树。“稍等片刻，瞧！仇人要对我做的，我倒对他做成了！”随即趁公鸡还没睁眼，他跑过去把公鸡胸口的鬃毛啫得精短，再也不能长回来。“这会儿瞧！”老鼠说，“瞧！我的心这样治好了，我的仇人落得同他给我做的一样——没了体面！”说完他心满意足地跑回窖里去了。
公鸡终于睁开眼。“唉我，我的头！”他喊。随后他哀哼着摇摇晃晃站起来，一低头瞧见了那颗坚果：光溜溜圆滚滚，像只褐色的蛋。公鸡一见，哀得更响了：“哦，我的头！Tâ‑kâ‑kâ‑kâ‑â‑â!”可头顶血肿不消，越肿越高，满是紫黑的血楞，沉得塔卡卡以为自己必死无疑。于是他一路哀号着去见祖母。
祖母听见他，开了门，喊：“怎么啦？”

===Original===

“Oh, my grandmother, ah me! I am murdered!” he answered. “A great, round, hard seed was dropped on my head by a little creature with a short, one-feathered tail, who came and told me that it was good to eat and﻿—oh! my head is all bleeding and swollen! By the light of your favor, bind my wound for me lest, alas, I die!”
“Served you right! Why did you leave your place, knowing better?” cried the old woman. “I will not bind your head unless you give me your very bristles of manhood, that you may remember your lesson!”
“Oh! take them, grandmother!” cried the Cock; but when he looked down, alas! the beard of his breast, the glory of his kind, was all gone. “Ah me! ah me! What shall I do?” he again cried. But the old woman told him that unless he brought her at least four bristles she would not cure him, and forthwith she shut the door.
So the poor Cock slowly staggered back toward his corral, hoping to find some of the hairs that had been gnawed off. As he passed the little lodge of his neighbor, the Dog, he caught sight of old *Wahtsita’s* fine muzzle-beard. “Ha!” thought he. Then he told the Dog his tale, and begged of him four hairs﻿—“only four!”
“You great, pampered noisemaker, give me some bread, then, fine bread, and I will give you the hairs.” Whereupon the Cock thought, and went to the house of a Trader of Foodstuffs; and he told him also the tale.
“Well, then, bring me some wood with which I may heat the oven to bake the bread,” said the Trader of Foodstuffs.
The Cock went to some Woods near by. “Oh, ye Beloved of the Trees, drop me dry branches!” And with this he told the Trees his tale; but the Trees shook their leaves and said: “No rain has fallen, and all our branches will soon be dry. Beseech the Waters that they give us drink, then we will gladly give you wood.”
Then the Cock went to a Spring near by﻿—and when he saw in it how his head was swollen and he found that it was growing harder, he again began to lament.
“What matters?” murmured the Beloved of the Waters.
Then he told them the tale also.
“Listen!” said the Beings of Water. “Long have men neglected their duties, and the Beloved of the Clouds need payment of due no less than ourselves, the Trees, the Food-maker, the Dog, and the Old Woman. Behold! no plumes are set about our border!

===Chinese===

“哦，祖母，唉我！我遭杀害了！”他答，“一个小畜生，长着短短的、一根毛的尾巴，往我头上砸了一颗又大又圆又硬的籽，还先来告诉我说那东西好吃——哦！我的头全是血，肿起来了！凭您恩宠的光，替我包扎伤口吧，免得我，唉，死了！”
“活该！明知道好歹，为什么离开你的地方？”老妇人喊，“不把你那男子汉的鬃毛给我，我就不给你包头，好叫你记住这个教训！”
“哦！拿去吧，祖母！”公鸡喊；可他一低头，唉！胸口的须髯，他一族的荣光，全没了。“唉我！唉我！我可怎么办？”他又哭。可老妇人说，不给他至少四根鬃毛就不治他，说罢砰地关了门。
可怜的公鸡摇摇晃晃慢慢挪回围栏，指望找到几根被啫下来的毛。路过邻居狗的小屋时，他一眼望见老瓦奇特塔（Wahtsita）漂亮的口鼻须。“哈！”他想。于是他把事情讲给狗听，求他给四根毛——“就四根！”
“你这娇惯出来的大嗓门，先给我点面包——好面包，我就给你毛。”于是公鸡想了想，去找一位食料商人（Trader of Foodstuffs）家；也把事情讲给他听。
“那么，给我弄些柴来，我好生炉子烤面包。”食料商人说。
公鸡去了近旁的树林。“哦，树之受爱者们（Beloved of the Trees），给我掉些干枝吧！”说着把事情讲给树听；可树们摇着叶子说：“一直没有下雨，我们的枝子眼看都要干了。去恳求水们给我们喝的，我们就乐意给你柴。”
于是公鸡去了近旁的一处泉——他往水里一照，看见自己的头肿成什么样，又发现它越肿越硬，便又哀哭起来。
“怎么了？”水之受爱者们（Beloved of the Waters）低声问。
于是他把事情也讲给他们听。
“听着！”众水生灵说，“人类久已疏怠了他们的本分；云之受爱者们（Beloved of the Clouds）同我们一样、同树们、同制食者、同狗和老妇人一样，都该得到应份的酬报。瞧！我们四边连一根祭羽都没插！”

===Original===

Now, therefore, pay to them of thy feathers﻿—four floating plumes from under thy wings﻿—and set them close over us, that, seen in our depths from the sky, they will lure the Beloved of the Clouds with their rain-laden breaths. Thus will our stream-way be replenished and the Trees watered, and their Winds in the Trees will drop thee dead branches wherewith thou mayest make payment and all will be well.”
Forthwith the *Tâkâkâ* plucked four of his best plumes and set them, one on the northern, one on the western, one on the southern, and one on the eastern border of the Pool. Then the Winds of the Four Quarters began to breathe upon the four plumes, and with those Breaths of the Beloved came Clouds, and from the Clouds fell Rain, and the Trees threw down dry branches, and the Wind placed among them Red-top Grass, which is light and therefore lightens the load it is among. And when the Cock returned and gathered a little bundle of fagots, lo! the Red-top made it so light that he easily carried it to the Food-maker, who gave him bread, for which the Dog gave him four bristles, and these he took to the old Grandmother.
“Ha!” exclaimed she. “Now, child, I will cure thee, but thou hast been so long that thy head will always be welted and covered with proud-flesh, even though healed. Still, it must ever be so. Doing right keeps right; doing wrong makes wrong, which, to make right, one must even pay as the sick pay those who cure them. Go now, and bide whither I bid thee.”
When, after a time, the Cock became well, lo! there were great, flabby, bloodred welts on his head and blue marks on his temples where they were bruised so sore. Now, listen:
It is for this reason that ever since that time the medicine masters of that people never give cure without pay; never, for there is no virtue in medicine of no value. Ever since then cocks have had no bristles on their breasts﻿—only little humps where they ought to be;﻿—and they always have bloodred crests of meat on their heads. And even when a hen lays an egg and a *tâkâkâ* cock sees it, he begins to *tâ‑kâ‑kâ‑â* as the ancient of them all did when he saw the brown nut. And sometimes they even pick at and eat these seeds of their own children, especially when they are cracked.

===Chinese===

所以现在，从你的羽毛里付出四根——翅膀底下飘轻的绒羽——紧贴着我们安放；从天空中俯瞰我们的深处望见它们，它们载雨的气息就会引得云之受爱者们降下雨来。这样，我们的水道就会重新盈满，树们得饮，树间的风们自会掉给你枯枝，你拿去酬报，一切就都好了。”
塔卡卡当即拔下四根顶好的绒羽，一根安在水池北缘，一根西缘，一根南缘，一根东缘。于是四方之风开始向四根绒羽吹拂，受爱者们的气息引来了云，云降下雨，树们抛下枯枝，风还在枝柴间放了红顶草——它轻，所以叫它所混的柴担也轻。公鸡回来拾了一小捆柴，瞧！红顶草把柴担弄得轻飘飘的，他轻轻松松担到制食者那里；制食者给了他面包，面包换来狗的四根须毛，他把毛带给了老祖母。
“哈！”她喊，“这就对了，孩子，我这就治你；可你耽搁太久，头上的血楞要留一辈子，结着紫红的疤肉，哪怕好了也在。不过，也只能如此。行正得正，行误生误；误了要归正，就得像病人酬谢医者那样付出代价。去吧，照我吩咐你的地方待着去。”
过了一阵公鸡好了，瞧！他头上起了紫红肥大的血楞，太阳穴上留着青痕，是当初磕得最狠的地方。且听：
正因如此，从那时起，那族人的医药师们治病没有不取酬的；从来没有，因为不值钱的药没有效力。也正从那时起，公鸡胸口再也不长鬃毛——只留下几个该长毛的小丘；他们头上也永远长着血红的肉冠。甚至母鸡下了蛋，塔卡卡公鸡一见，就 tâ-kâ-kâ-â 地叫起来，同他们的老祖宗当年看见那颗褐色坚果时一模一样。有时他们甚至啄食自己孩子的这些“籽”，尤其是蛋壳裂了的时候。

===Original===

As for mice, we know how they went into the meal-bags in olden times and came out something else, and, getting smoked, became *tsothliko-ahâi*, with long, bare tails. But that was before the Cock cut the tail of the *tsothliko* Mouse off. Ever since he cried in agony: “*Weh tsu yii weh tsu!*” like a child with a burnt finger, his children have been called *Wehtsutsukwe*, and wander wild in the fields; hence field-mice to this day have short tails, brown-stained and hairy; and their chops are all pink, and when you look them in the face they seem always to be crying.
Thus shortens my story.

===Chinese===

至于老鼠们，我们知道，古时他们钻进粮袋，出来就变了样，被烟一熏，成了 tsothliko-ahâi，拖着光光的长尾巴。可那是在公鸡咬断 tsothliko 老鼠尾巴之前。自从他像烫了手指的小孩那样惨叫“Weh tsu yii weh tsu!”以来，他的孩子们就被叫作 Wehtsutsukwe，在田野里野跑；所以直到今天，田鼠尾巴短，带着褐色、生着毛；嘴岔子通红，你端详它们的脸，它们像永远在哭。
我的故事就此收短。

## The Coyote and the Beetle / 郊狼与甲虫
===Original===

In remote times, after our ancients were settled at Middle Ant Hill, a little thing occurred which will explain a great deal.
My children, you have doubtless seen Tip-beetles. They run around on smooth, hard patches of ground in spring time and early summer, kicking their heels into the air and thrusting their heads into any crack or hole they find.
Well, in ancient times, on the pathway leading around to Fat Mountain, there was one of these Beetles running about in all directions in the sunshine, when a Coyote came trotting along. He pricked up his ears, lowered his nose, arched his neck, and stuck out his paw toward the Beetle. “Ha!” said he, “I shall bite you!”
The Beetle immediately stuck his head down close to the ground, and, lifting one of his antennae deprecatingly, exclaimed: “Hold on! Hold on, friend! Wait a bit, for the love of mercy! I hear something very strange down below here!”
“Humph!” replied the Coyote. “What do you hear?”
“Hush! hush!” cried the Beetle, with his head still to the ground. “Listen!”
So the Coyote drew back and listened most attentively. By-and-by the Beetle lifted himself with a long sigh of relief.
“*Okwe!*” exclaimed the Coyote. “What was going on?”
“The Good Soul save us!” exclaimed the Beetle, with a shake of his head. “I heard them saying down there that tomorrow they would chase away and thoroughly chastise everybody who defiled the public trails of this country, and they are making ready as fast as they can!”
“Souls of my ancestors!” cried the Coyote. “I have been loitering along this trail this very morning, and have defiled it repeatedly. I’ll cut!” And away he ran as fast as he could go.
The Beetle, in pure exuberance of spirits, turned somersaults and stuck his head in the sand until it was quite turned.
Thus did the Beetle in the days of the ancients save himself from being bitten. Consequently the Tip-beetle has that strange habit of kicking his heels into the air and sticking his head in the sand.
Thus shortens my story.

===Chinese===

远古之时，我们的祖辈在中央蚁丘安顿下来之后，发生过一件小事，却能说明许多事情。
孩子们，你们一定见过叩头虫。春天和初夏，它们在平滑坚硬的地面上四处跑，后脚踢向空中，头往它找到的任何缝隙或洞穴里钻。
上古时候，绕往胖山（Fat Mountain）的小道上，正有一只这样的甲虫在阳光下四处乱窜，一只郊狼小跑着过来。它竖起耳朵，压低鼻子，弓起脖子，把爪子伸向甲虫。“哈！”它说，“我要咬你！”
甲虫立刻把头紧贴地面，举起一根触角作告饶状，喊道：“且慢！且慢，朋友！看在老天的份上，稍等一下！我听见这底下有什么东西在响，怪得很！”
“哼！”郊狼应道，“你听见什么了？”
“嘘！嘘！”甲虫喊，头仍贴着地，“听！”
于是郊狼退后，极专心地听着。过了一阵，甲虫长舒一口气，抬起身来。
“Okwe!”郊狼喊，“底下怎么了？”
“好心的灵保佑！”甲虫摇着头喊，“我听见他们在底下说，明天要把弄脏这国中大小道路的家伙统统赶走、狠狠收拾，他们正拼命做准备呢！”
“列祖的魂灵啊！”郊狼喊，“今儿一大早我就在这条道上逛荡，撒了一路脏！我得赶紧跑！”说罢它撒腿飞奔而去。
甲虫乐不可支，翻起跟头，一头扎进沙里，直到把头都别了过去。
上古之时，甲虫就是这样免遭一咬的。所以叩头虫至今留着那个怪习惯：后脚踢向空中，头往沙里钻。
我的故事就此收短。

## The Coyote and the Locust / 郊狼与蝉
===Original===

In the days of the ancients, there lived south of Zuni, beyond the headland of rocks, at a place called Suski-ashokton (“Rock Hollow of the Coyotes”), an old Coyote. And this side of the headland of rocks, in the bank of a steep arroyo, lived an old Locust, near where stood a piñon tree, crooked and so bereft of needles that it was sunny.
One day the Coyote went out hunting, leaving his large family of children and his old wife at home. It was a fine day and the sun was shining brightly, and the old Locust crawled out of his home in the loam of the arroyo and ascended to one of the bare branches of the piñon tree, where, hooking his feet firmly into the bark, he began to sing and play his flute. The Coyote in his wanderings came along just as he began to sing these words:

> “Tchumali, tchumali, shohkoya,

Tchumali, tchumali, shohkoya!

Yaamii heeshoo taatani tchupatchinte,

Shohkoya,

Shohkoya!”

> Locust, locust, playing a flute,

Locust, locust, playing a flute!

Away up above on the pine-tree bough, closely clinging,

Playing a flute,

Playing a flute!

“Delight of my senses!” called out the Coyote, squatting down on his haunches, and looking up, with his ears pricked and his mouth grinning; “Delight of my senses, how finely you play your flute!”
“Do you think so?” said the Locust, continuing his song.
“Goodness, yes!” cried the Coyote, shifting nearer. “What a song it is! Pray, teach it to me, so that I can take it home and dance my children to it. I have a large family at home.”
“All right,” said the Locust. “Listen, then.” And he sang his song again:

> “Tchumali, tchumali, shohkoya,

Tchumali, tchumali, shohkoya!

Yaamii heeshoo taatani tchupatchinte,

Shohkoya,

Shohkoya!”

“Delightful!” cried the Coyote. “Now, shall I try?”
“Yes, try.”
Then in a very hoarse voice the Coyote half growled and half sang (making a mistake here and there, to be sure) what the Locust had sung, though there was very little music in his repetition of the performance.

> “Tchu u-mali, tchumali﻿—shohshoh koya,

Tchu tchu mali, tchumali shohkoya,

Yaa mami he he shoo ta ta tante tchup patchin te,

Shohkoya,

Shohkoya!”

===Chinese===

上古之时，祖尼（Zuni）以南、石岬那头，有个地方叫苏斯基-阿硕克顿（Suski-ashokton，意为“郊狼岩谷”），住着一只老郊狼。石岬这一头，一道陡峭干河床的岸里住着一只老蝉，近旁立着一棵歪脖子矮松，针叶落得精光，洒满阳光。
一天，郊狼外出打猎，把一大窝孩子和老件儿留在家。天气晴朗，阳光灿烂，老蝉从干河床溯土里的家中爬出来，攀上矮松的一根秃枝，把脚牢牢钩进树皮，开始唱歌吹笛。郊狼东游西荡，正赶上他开口唱起这支歌：

> “Tchumali, tchumali, shohkoya,

Tchumali, tchumali, shohkoya!

Yaamii heeshoo taatani tchupatchinte,

Shohkoya,

Shohkoya!”

> 蝉啊，蝉啊，吹着笛，

蝉啊，蝉啊，吹着笛！

高高地攀在松枝上，紧紧贴着，

吹着笛，

吹着笛！

“悦我耳目的！”郊狼喊道，蹲坐下来，仰头望着，耳朵竖着，嘴咧着；“悦我耳目的，你的笛子吹得多好啊！”
“你当真觉得？”蝉说，歌不停口。
“天哪，当真！”郊狼喊着，往近处挪。“多好的一支歌！求求你，教给我吧，好叫我带回家去，边唱边舞我的孩子们。我家里人口可多呢。”
“好吧，”蝉说，“那你就听好了。”于是他把那支歌又唱了一遍：

> “Tchumali, tchumali, shohkoya,

Tchumali, tchumali, shohkoya!

Yaamii heeshoo taatani tchupatchinte,

Shohkoya,

Shohkoya!”

“妙极了！”郊狼喊。“这回我能试试吗？”
“能，试吧。”
于是郊狼用一副沙哑嗓子，半吼半唱（自然，错儿这里那里不少）地把蝉唱的唱了一遍，只是他那演唱里实在没多少音乐。

> “Tchu u-mali, tchumali—shohshoh koya,

Tchu tchu mali, tchumali shohkoya,

Yaa mami he he shoo ta ta tante tchup patchin te,

Shohkoya,

Shohkoya!”

===Original===

“Ha!” laughed he, as he finished; “I have got it, haven’t I?”
“Well, yes,” said the Locust, “fairly well.”
“Now, then, let us sing it over together.”
And while the Locust piped shrilly the Coyote sang gruffly, though much better than at first, the song.
“There, now,” exclaimed he, with a whisk of his tail; “didn’t I tell you?” and without waiting to say another word he whisked away toward his home beyond the headland of rocks. As he was running along the plain he kept repeating the song to himself, so that he would not forget it, casting his eyes into the air, after the manner of men in trying to remember or to say particularly fine things, so that he did not notice an old Gopher peering at him somewhat ahead on the trail; and the old Gopher laid a trap for him in his hole.
The Coyote came trotting along, singing: “*Shohkoya, shohkoya*,” when suddenly he tumbled heels over head into the Gopher’s hole. He sneezed, began to cough, and to rub the sand out of his eyes; and then jumping out, cursed the Gopher heartily, and tried to recall his song, but found that he had utterly forgotten it, so startled had he been.
“The lubber-cheeked old Gopher! I wish the pests were all in the Land of Demons!” cried he. “They dig their holes, and nobody can go anywhere in safety. And now I have forgotten my song. Well, I will run back and get the old Locust to sing it over again. If he can sit there singing to himself, why can’t he sing it to me? No doubt in the world he is still out there on that piñon branch singing away.” Saying which, he ran back as fast as he could. When he arrived at the piñon tree, sure enough, there was the old Locust still sitting and singing.
“Now, how lucky this is, my friend!” cried the Coyote, long before he had reached the place. “The lubber-cheeked, fat-sided old Gopher dug a hole right in my path; and I went along singing your delightful song and was so busy with it that I fell headlong into the trap he had set for me, and I was so startled that, on my word, I forgot all about the song, and I have come back to ask you to sing it for me again.”
“Very well,” said the Locust. “Be more careful this time.” So he sang the song over.
“Good! Surely I’ll not forget it this time,” cried the Coyote; so he whisked about, and away he sped toward his home beyond the headland of rocks. “Goodness!”

===Chinese===

“哈！”他唱完笑道，“我学会了，是不是？”
“嗯，是的，”蝉说，“学得还算可以。”
“那么，咱们一起唱一遍吧。”
于是蝉尖声吹着，郊狼粗声唱着——不过比头一回好多了——把那支歌唱了下来。
“瞧见没！”他一甩尾巴喊，“我没说错吧？”不等再说什么，他一溜烟朝石岬那头的家奔去。他在平原上一路跑，一路把那支歌念了又念，生怕忘了；两眼望天——人要想记住或念叨什么特别妙的东西时都是这个样子——以致没留神前方道上有只老囊鼠正瞅着他；老囊鼠就在洞里给他设下了陷阱。
郊狼小跑着过来，嘴里唱着“Shohkoya, shohkoya”，突然一头栽进囊鼠的洞里。他打了个喷嚏，咳嗽起来，揉眼中的沙子；随后跳出来，把囊鼠着实咒骂了一顿，想找他的歌，却发觉忘了个精光，吓的。
“这厚腮帮子的老囊鼠！但愿这些祸害都归了恶魔国去！”他喊。“他们挖他们的洞，害得谁也没法安生走路。这下我可把歌忘了。唔，我跑回去叫老蝉再唱一遍。他能坐在那儿自唱自的，为什么不能唱给我？不用问，他八成还在那根矮松枝上唱得欢呢。”说着，他撒腿飞奔回去。到了矮松树前，果然不错，老蝉还坐在那里唱。
“喏，多巧，我的朋友！”郊狼还没到跟前就喊。“那厚腮帮子、肥身子的老囊鼠在我道上挖了个洞；我一路唱着你那支妙歌，唱得太专心，一头栽进了他给我设的陷阱，吓得好半天，说实在的，把歌忘了个精光，我回来求你给我再唱一遍。”
“很好，”蝉说，“这回留点神。”于是他把那支歌又唱了一遍。
“好！这回我可忘不了啦，”郊狼喊；说罢他一转身，朝石岬那头的家飞奔而去。“天哪！”

===Original===

said he to himself, as he went along; “what a fine thing this will be for my children! How they will be quieted by it when I dance them as I sing it! Let’s see how it runs. Oh, yes!

> “Tchumali, tchumali, shohkoya,

Tchumali, tchumali, shohko﻿—”

===Chinese===

他一路自首自语，“这对我的孩子们是多好的事啊！我边唱边舞他们的时候，他们会多安静啊！让我想想是怎么唱的。哦，是了！

> “Tchumali, tchumali, shohkoya,

Tchumali, tchumali, shohko—”

===Original===

*Thli‑i‑i‑i‑i‑p, piu‑piu, piu‑piu!* fluttered a flock of Pigeons out of the bushes at his very feet, with such a whizzing and whistling that the Coyote nearly tumbled over with fright, and, recovering himself, cursed the Doves heartily, calling them “gray-backed, useless sage-vermin”; and, between his fright and his anger, was so much shaken up that he again forgot his song.
Now, the Locust wisely concluded that this would be the case, and as he did not like the Coyote very well, having been told that sometimes members of his tribe were by no means friendly to Locusts and other insects, he concluded to play him a trick and teach him a lesson in the minding of his own affairs. So, catching tight hold of the bark, he swelled himself up and strained until his back split open; then he skinned himself out of his old skin, and, crawling down the tree, found a suitable quartz stone, which, being light-colored and clear, would not make his skin look unlike himself. He took the stone up the tree and carefully placed it in the empty skin. Then he cemented the back together with a little pitch and left his exact counterfeit sticking to the bark, after which he flew away to a neighboring tree.
No sooner had the Coyote recovered his equanimity to some extent than, discovering the loss of his song and again exclaiming “No doubt he is still there piping away; I’ll go and get him to sing it over,”﻿—he ran back as fast as he could.
“Ah wha!” he exclaimed, as he neared the tree. “I am quite fatigued with all this extra running about. But, no matter; I see you are still there, my friend. A lot of miserable, gray-backed Ground-pigeons flew up right from under me as I was going along singing my song, and they startled me so that I forgot it; but I tell you, I cursed them heartily! Now, my friend, will you not be good enough to sing once more for me?”
He paused for a reply. None came.
“Why, what’s the matter? Don’t you hear me?” yelled the Coyote, running nearer, looking closely, and scrutinizing the Locust. “I say, I have lost my song, and want you to sing for me again. Will you, or will you not?” Then he paused.
“Look here, are you going to sing for me or not?” continued the Coyote, getting angry.
No reply.
The Coyote stretched out his nose, wrinkled up his lips, and snarled: “Look here, do you see my teeth?

===Chinese===

Thli-i-i-i-i-p, piu-piu, piu-piu!——一群鸽子从他脚边的灌木里呼啦啦飞起，呼啸哨响，吓得郊狼差点栽倒；他缓过神来，把那些鸽子着实咒骂了一顿，骂它们“灰脊背、没用的鼠尾草害货”；又惊又气，被摇撼得把歌又忘了。
且说蝉早已料到会这样；他也不大喜欢郊狼——听说这个族类有时对蝉和其他虫子绝不客气——便决意捉弄他一回，教他一教管好自家事的道理。于是他紧紧抓住树皮，鼓起身子，使劲一绷，直到背壳绽开；然后从旧壳里蜕出自己，爬下树，找了一块合适的石英石——颜色浅、又透亮，放在皮里不会不像他自己——把石头带上树，小心地安进空壳里。再用一点树脂把背壳粘拢，把自己一模一样的替身粘在树皮上，然后飞到旁边一棵树上去了。
郊狼刚刚定下神来，发觉歌又丢了，又嗔起“不用问，他还在那儿吹得欢呢；我去叫他再唱一遍”——他撒腿飞奔回去。
“啊哈！”他临近那棵树喊。“这一路额外折腾，我可累坏了。不过不要紧；我看你还在呢，我的朋友。我正唱着歌赶路，一群灰脊背的可怜地鸽子就从我脚底下飞起来，吓了我一跳，把歌忘了；可我告诉你，我把他们着实骂了一顿！那么，我的朋友，劳驾再给我唱一回，好不好？”
他停下来等回答。没有回答。
“咦，怎么回事？没听见我说话吗？”郊狼喊着，跑近了，细细打量那只蝉。“我说，我把歌丢了，要你再给我唱一遍。你唱，还是不唱？”他又停住。
“喂，你到底唱不唱？”郊狼接着说，火了。
还是没有回答。
郊狼伸出鼻子，皱起嘴唇，吃哮道：“喂，瞧见我的牙设？”

===Original===

Well, I’ll ask you just four times more to sing for me, and if you don’t sing then, I’ll snap you up in a hurry, I tell you. Will﻿—you﻿—sing﻿—for me? Once. Will you sing﻿—for me? Twice. Two more times! Look out! Will you sing for me? Are you a fool? Do you see my teeth? Only once more! Will﻿—you﻿—sing﻿—for me?”
No reply.
“Well, you are a fool!” yelled the Coyote, unable to restrain himself longer, and making a quick jump, he snapped the Locust skin off of the bough, and bit it so hard that it crushed and broke the teeth in the middle of his jaw, driving some of them so far down in his gums that you could hardly see them, and crowding the others out so that they were regular tusks. The Coyote dropped the stone, rolled in the sand, and howled and snarled and wriggled with pain. Then he got up and shook his head, and ran away with his tail between his legs. So excessive was his pain that at the first brook he came to he stooped down to lap up water in order to alleviate it, and he there beheld what you and I see in the mouths of every Coyote we ever catch﻿—that the teeth back of the canines are all driven down, so that you can see only the points of them, and look very much broken up.
In the days of the ancients the Coyote minded not his own business and restrained not his anger. So he bit a Locust that was only the skin of one with a stone inside. And all his descendants have inherited his broken teeth. And so also to this day, when Locusts venture out on a sunny morning to sing a song, it is not infrequently their custom to protect themselves from the consequences of attracting too much attention by skinning themselves and leaving their counterparts on the trees.
Thus shortens my story.

===Chinese===

“好，我只再问你四回；再不唱，我告诉你，我就一口吞了你。你——唱——不——唱？这是一回。你唱不——唱？两回。只剩两回了！当心！你唱不唱？你是傻子吗？瞧见我的牙没？就剩最后一回！你——唱——不——唱——给我？”
没有回答。
“好，你是个傻子！”郊狼喊，再也忍不住了；他猛地一跃，一口咬住枝上的蝉壳，咬得那么狠，咯的一声，把颛中间的牙咬碎了，有的深深陷进牙床里几乎看不见，另一些被挤出嘴外，成了獠牙。郊狼吐出石头，在沙地里打滚，疼得又嚎又吃哮又扭动。然后他爬起来摇摇头，夹着尾巴跑了。他疼得厉害，遇到头一条小溪便低头饮水想解解痛；他在水里照见了你我逮住的每一只郊狼嘴里都能看见的那副样子——犬牙后头的牙全陷了下去，只露出牙尖，七零八碎，像断过的一样。
上古之时，郊狼不管自家的事，又不制怒。于是他咬了一只蝉，那却只是一张空壳，里头塞着石头。他的子孙后代就继承了他那口碎牙。也正因如此，直到今天，蝉们赶个大晴天的早晨出去唱歌时，常常会蜕下自己，把替身留在树上，免得招来太多注意而遭殃。
我的故事就此收短。

## The Coyote and the Ravens Who Raced Their Eyes / 郊狼和赛眼睛的渡鸦
===Original===

Long, long ago, in the days of the ancients, there lived in Hómaiakwin, or the Canyon of the Cedars, a Coyote﻿—doubtless the same one I have told you of as having made friends with the Woodpounder bird. As you know, this canyon in which he lived is below the high eastern cliff of Face Mountain.
This Coyote was out walking one day. On leaving his house he had said that he was going hunting; but﻿—miserable fellow!﻿—who ever knew a Coyote to catch anything, unless it were a prairie-dog or a wood-rat or a locust or something of the kind? So you may depend upon it he was out walking; that is, wandering around to see what he could see.
He crossed over the valley northward, with his tail dragging along in an indifferent sort of a way, until he came to the place on Thunder Mountain called Shoton-pia (“Where the Shell Breastplate Hangs”). He climbed up the foothills, and along the terraces at the base of the cliff, and thus happened to get toward the southeastern corner of the mountain. There is a little column of rock with a round top to it standing there, as you know, to this day.
Now, on the top of this standing rock sat two old Ravens, racing their eyes. One of them would settle himself down on the rock and point with his beak straight off across the valley to some pinnacle in the cliffs of the opposite mesa. Then he would say to his companion, without turning his head at all: “You see that rock yonder? Well, ahem! Standing rock yonder, round you, go ye my eyes and come back.” Then he would lower his head, stiffen his neck, squeeze his eyelids, and “*Pop*!” he would say as his eyes flew out of their sockets, and sailed away toward the rock like two streaks of lightning, reaching which they would go round it, and come back toward the Raven; and as they were coming back, he would swell up his throat and say “*Whu‑u‑u‑u‑u‑u‑u*,”﻿—whereupon his eyes would slide with a *kʻothlo!* into their sockets again. Then he would turn toward his companion, and swelling up his throat still more, and ducking his head just as if he were trying to vomit his own neck, he would laugh inordinately; and the other would laugh with him, bristling up all the feathers on his body.
Then the other one would settle himself, and say: “Ah, I’ll better you! You see that rock away yonder?” Then he would begin to squeeze his eyelids, and *thlut!

===Chinese===

很久很久以前，先民时代，霍迈亚克温（Hómaiakwin），也就是雪松峡谷里，住着一只郊狼（Coyote）——多半就是我给你们讲过的、跟啄木鸟（Woodpounder）交上朋友的那一只。你们知道，他住的这道峡谷，在脸山（Face Mountain）东侧高崖的下方。
这只郊狼一天出门遛跶。离家时他说要去打猎；可是——可怜的家伙！——谁见过郊狼打着过什么，除了草原犬鼠、林鼠、蚱蜢之类？所以你只管信：他不过是出门遛弯儿，也就是四处转转，看看能看见点什么。
他拖着尾巴，一副满不在乎的样子，往北穿过谷地，来到雷霆山上一个叫肖通-皮亚（Shoton-pia，「挂贝壳护胸处」）的地方。他爬上山麓丘陵，沿着崖脚的台地走，就这么转到了山的东南角。那里立着一根圆顶的小石柱，你们知道，直到今天还在。
那立岩顶上，坐着两只老渡鸦（Ravens），正在赛眼睛。一只在岩石上蹲稳，用喙直直地指向谷地对岸台地崖壁上的某座石峰，头也不回地对同伴说：“看见那边那块岩石了吗？好，咳！那边那块立岩，转着，我的眼睛，去去回回。”然后他低下头，绷直脖颈，挤紧眼皮，“啵！”他喊一声，眼睛便飞出眼窝，像两道闪电直奔那岩石而去；到了跟前绕它一圈，又朝渡鸦飞回来；眼看着回来，他便鼓起喉咙说“呼——乌——乌——乌——”，眼睛呴咚一声滑回眼窝。随后他转向同伴，喉咙鼓得更圆，脑袋一探一探，活像要把自己的脖子吐出来，放声大笑；另一只也跟着笑，浑身羽毛都乍了起来。
接着另一只蹲稳身子说：“啊，我要赢过你！看见远远那块岩石没有？”然后他开始挤眼皮，噗——

===Original===

* his eyes would fly out of their sockets and away across the mesa and round the rock he had named; and as they flew back, he would lower himself, and say “*Whu‑u‑u‑u‑u‑u‑u*,” when *kʻothlo!* the eyes would slide into their sockets again. Then, as much amused as ever, the Ravens would laugh at one another again.
Now, the Coyote heard the Ravens humming their eyes back into their sockets; and the sound they made, as well as the way they laughed so heartily, exceedingly pleased him, so that he stuck his tail up very straight and laughed merely from seeing them laugh. Presently he could contain himself no longer. “Friends,” he cried, in a shrieky little voice, “I say, friends, how do you do, and what are you doing?”
The Ravens looked down, and when they saw the Coyote they laughed and punched one another with their wings and cried out to him: “Bless you! Glad to see you come!”
“What is it you are doing?” asked he. “By the daylight of the gods, it is funny, whatever it is!” And he whisked his tail and laughed, as he said this, drawing nearer to the Ravens.
“Why, we are racing our eyes,” said the older of the two Ravens. “Didn’t you ever see anyone race his eyes before?”
“Good demons, no!” exclaimed the Coyote. “Race your eyes! How in the world do you race your eyes?”
“Why, this way,” said one of the Ravens. And he settled himself down. “Do you see that tall rock yonder? Ahem! Well, tall rock, yonder﻿—ye my eyes go round it and return to me!” *Kʻothlo! kʻothlo!* the eyes slipped out of their sockets, and the Raven, holding his head perfectly still, waited, with his upper lids hanging wrinkled on his lower, for the return of the eyes; and as they neared him, he crouched down, swelled up his neck, and exclaimed “*Whu‑u‑u‑u‑u‑u‑u*.” *Tsoko!* the eyes flew into their sockets again. Then the Raven turned around and showed his two black bright eyes as good as ever. “There, now! what did I tell you?”
“By the moon!” squeaked the Coyote, and came up nearer still. “How in the world do you do that? It is one of the most wonderful and funny things I ever saw!”
“Well, here, come up close to me,” said the Raven, “and I will show you how it is done.” Then the other Raven settled himself down; and *pop!* went his eyes out of their sockets, round a rock still farther away. And as they returned, he exclaimed “*Whu‑u‑u‑u‑u‑u‑u*,” when *tsoko!* in again they came.

===Chinese===

噜！”他的眼睛便飞出眼窝，飞过台地，绕着他点名的那块岩石转；往回飞时，他伏低身子，说一声“呼——乌——乌——乌——”，呴咚！眼睛又滑回眼窝。渡鸦们兴致不减，又彼此大笑起来。
郊狼听见渡鸦们往眼窝里嗡眼睛的声音；那声音，加上他们那样开怀大笑的样子，让他喜欢得不得了，尾巴翘得笔直，光是看他们笑，自己也跟着笑。不多时他再也忍不住了。“朋友们，”他用尖细的嗓子喊，“我说，朋友们，你们好啊，你们在干什么呀？”
渡鸦们往下一看，见是郊狼，笑了，用翅膀你捅捅我、我捅捅你，朝他喊：“老天保佑！见到你来真高兴！”
“你们在干什么呀？”他问。“凭神明的白昼，不管这是什么，太好玩了！”他一边说，一边甩着尾巴笑，朝渡鸦们凑近。
“唔，我们在赛眼睛呢，”两只渡鸦里年长的说。“你没见过人家赛眼睛？”
“好魔鬼，没见过！”郊狼喊。“赛眼睛！你们到底怎么个赛法？”
“唔，这样，”一只渡鸦说。他蹲稳身子。“看见那边那根高岩石了吗？咳！好，高岩石，那边——我的眼睛绕你一圈，回到我这儿来！”呴咚！呴咚！眼睛滑出眼窝；渡鸦把头端得纹丝不动，上眼皮皱搭在下眼皮上，等着眼睛回来；眼睛临近，他伏低身子，鼓起脖子喊“呼——乌——乌——乌——”。嘰哆！眼睛飞回眼窝。渡鸦转过身，亮出那双黑亮黑亮、完好如初的眼睛。“瞧见没有！我说什么来着？”
“凭月亮！”郊狼尖声说，又凑近了些。“这到底是怎么弄的？这是我这辈子见过最神最逗的事了！”
“来，凑我跟前来，”渡鸦说，“我做给你看。”另一只渡鸦随即蹲稳；啵！他的眼睛飞出眼窝，绕更远处一块岩石转了一圈；飞回来时他喊一声“呼——乌——乌——乌——”，嘰哆！眼睛又回来了。

===Original===

And he turned around laughing at the Coyote. “There, now!” said he, “didn’t I tell you?”
“By the daylight of the gods! I wish I could do that,” said the Coyote. “Suppose I try my eyes?”
“Why, yes, if you like, to be sure!” said the Ravens. “Well, now, do you want to try?”
“Humph! I should say I did,” replied the Coyote.
“Well, then, settle down right here on this rock,” said the Ravens, making way for him, “and hold your head out toward that rock and say: ‘Yonder rock, these my eyes go round it and return to me.’ ”
“I know! I know! I know!” yelled the Coyote. And he settled himself down, and squeezed and groaned to force his eyes out of his sockets, but they would not go. “Goodness!” said the Coyote, “how can I get my eyes to go out of their sockets?”
“Why, don’t you know how?” said the Ravens. “Well, just keep still, and we’ll help you; we’ll take them out for you.”
“All right! all right!” cried the Coyote, unable to repress his impatience. “Quick! quick! here I am, all ready!” And crouching down, he laid his tail straight out, swelled up his neck, and strained with every muscle to force his eyes out of his head. The Ravens picked them out with a dexterous twist of their beaks in no time, and sent them flying off over the valley. The Coyote yelped a little when they came out, but stood his ground manfully, and cringed down his neck and waited for his eyes to come back.
“Let the fool of a beast go without his eyes,” said the Ravens. “He was so very anxious to get rid of them, and do something he had no business with; let him go without them!” Whereupon they flew off across the valley, and caught up his eyes and ate them, and flew on, laughing at the predicament in which they had left the Coyote.
Now, thus the Coyote sat there the proper length of time; then he opened his mouth, and said “*Whu‑u‑u‑u‑u‑u‑u!*” But he waited in vain for his eyes to come back. And “*Whu‑u‑u‑u‑u‑u‑u‑u‑u!*” he said again. No use. “Mercy!” exclaimed he, “what can have become of my eyes? Why don’t they come back?” After he had waited and “*whu‑u‑u‑u‑u‑d*” until he was tired, he concluded that his eyes had got lost, and laid his head on his breast, woefully thinking of his misfortune. “How in the world shall I hunt up my eyes?” he groaned, as he lifted himself cautiously (for it must be remembered that he stood on a narrow rock), and tried to look all around; but he couldn’t see.

===Chinese===

他转身朝着郊狼笑。“瞧见没有！”他说，“我说什么来着？”
“凭神明的白昼！我真想也会这一手，”郊狼说。“要不我把我的眼睛也试试？”
“唔，想试就试呗，当然！”渡鸦们说。“怎么，现在就想试？”
“哼！那还用说，”郊狼答。
“那好，就在这块岩石上蹲下，”渡鸦们说着给他腾地方，“把头朝那块岩石伸直了，念：‘那边那岩石，我的眼睛绕你一圈，回到我这儿来。’”
“懂了！懂了！懂了！”郊狼嚎。他蹲下身子，又挤又哼地想把眼睛挤出眼窝，可就是挤不出。“老天！”郊狼说，“我怎么才能让眼睛出眼窝呢？”
“唔，你不会？”渡鸦们说。“那好，别动，我们帮你；我们替你把它们取出来。”
“好嘞！好嘞！”郊狼喊，按捺不住。“快！快！我在这儿，都预备好了！”他伏下身，尾巴放平拺直，鼓起脖子，浑身肌肉绷紧，拼命把眼睛往外挤。渡鸦们用喙灵巧地一拧，眨眼间就把它们啄了出来，撒向谷地上空。眼睛出来时郊狼嘷了一声，可他硬是英雄似的站住没动，缩着脖子，等眼睛回来。
“让这蠢畜生瞎着眼走吧，”渡鸦们说。“他那么急着甩掉眼睛，去干跟他毫不相干的事；就让他瞎着走！”说罢他们飞过谷地，追上他的眼睛，吃了，然后接着飞，一路笑话着他们留给郊狼的窘境。
郊狼就在那里规规矩矩蹲了该蹲的工夫；然后张开嘴，说“呼——乌——乌——乌——！”可他白等，眼睛没回来。“呼——乌——乌——乌——乌——！”他又说了一遍。没用。“天哪！”他喊，“我的眼睛出什么事了？怎么还不回来？”他又等又“呼——乌——乌——”地念，念累了，认定眼睛丢了，把头垂到胸前，凄凄惶惶想自己的倒霉事。“我可怎么找回我的眼睛啊？”他呻吟着，小心翼翼地撑起身子（别忘了，他可是站在窄窄的岩石上），想四下张望；可什么也看不见。

===Original===

Then he began to feel with his paws, one after another, to find the way down; and he slipped and fell, so that nearly all the breath was knocked out of his body. When he had recovered, he picked himself up, and felt and felt along, slowly descending, until he got into the valley.
Now, it happened as he felt his way along with his toes that he came to a wet place in the valley, not far below where the spring of Shuntakaiya flows out from the cliffs above. In feeling his way, his foot happened to strike a yellow cranberry, ripe and soft, but very cold, of course. “Ha!” said he, “lucky fellow, I! Here is one of my eyes.” So he picked it up and clapped it into one of his empty sockets; then he peered up to the sky, and the light struck through it. “Didn’t I tell you so, old fellow? It is one of your eyes, by the souls of your ancestors!” Then he felt around until he found another cranberry. “Ha!” said he, “and this proves it! Here is the other!” And he clapped that into the other empty socket. He didn’t seem to see quite as well as he had seen before, but still the cranberries answered the purpose of eyes exceedingly well, and the poor wretch of a Coyote never knew the difference; only it was observed when he returned to his companions in the Canyon of the Cedars that he had yellow eyes instead of black ones, which everybody knows Coyotes and all other creatures had at first.
---
Thus it was in the days of the ancients, and hence to this day coyotes have yellow eyes, and are not always quick to see things.
Thus shortens my story.

===Chinese===

于是他开始用爪子一只接一只地摸索下山的路；一滑脚摔了下去，摔得几乎断了气。缓过来后，他爬起身，摸啊摸，慢慢往下挪，一直挪到谷地里。
且说他用脚趾探着路走，走到谷中一块湿地上，就在舒恩塔凯亚（Shuntakaiya）泉从上方崖壁流出、往下不远的地方。探路时，他的脚正碰上一颗黄色的小酸果，熟透了，软软的，当然也冰凉。“哈！”他说，“我真走运！这不是我的一只眼睛嘛。”于是他拾起来，扣进一只空眼窝；然后往天上瞅，光透得进来。“我不是说中了嘛，老伙计？凭你祖先的魂灵，这就是你的一只眼睛！”接着他四下摸，又摸到一颗酸果。“哈！”他说，“这就对证了！这是另一只！”他把那颗扣进另一只空眼窝。他看得似乎不如从前清楚，可这两颗酸果当眼睛实在够用，这可怜的傻郊狼愣是没觉出分别；只是他回到雪松峡谷的伙伴们那儿时，大家发现他的眼睛是黄的，不是黑的——谁都知道，郊狼和一切生灵起初长的都是黑眼睛。
---
先民时代事情就是这样；所以直到今天，郊狼的眼睛是黄的，看东西也不总是那么灵。
我的故事，就此收梢。

## The Foster-Child of the Deer / 鹿的养子
===Original===

Once, long, long ago, at Háwikuh, there lived a maiden most beautiful. In her earlier years her father, who was a great priest, had devoted her to sacred things, and therefore he kept her always in the house secure from the gaze of all men, and thus she grew.
She was so beautiful that when the Sun looked down along one of the straight beams of his own light, if one of those beams chanced to pass through a chink in the roof, the sky-hole, or the windows of the upper part of the maiden’s room, he beheld her and wondered at her rare beauty, unable to compare it with anything he saw in his great journeys round about the worlds. Thus, as the maiden grew apace and became a young woman, the Sun loved her exceedingly, and as time went on he became so enamored of her that he descended to earth and entered on one of his own beams of light into her apartment, so that suddenly, while she was sitting one noonday weaving pretty baskets, there stood before her a glorious youth, gloriously dressed. It was the Sun-father. He looked upon her gently and lovingly; she looked upon him not fearfully: and so it came about that she loved him and he loved her, and he won her to be his wife. And many were the days in which he visited her and dwelt with her for a space at noon-time; but as she was alone mostly, or as she kept sitting weaving her trays when anyone of the family entered her apartment, no one suspected this.
Now, as she knew that she had been devoted to sacred things, and that if she explained how it was that she was a mother she would not be believed, she was greatly exercised in mind and heart. She therefore decided that when her child was born she would put it away from her.
When the time came, the child one night was born. She carefully wrapped the little baby boy in some soft cotton-wool, and in the middle of the night stole out softly over the rooftops, and, silently descending, laid the child on the sheltered side of a heap of refuse near the little stream that flows by Háwikuh, in the valley below. Then, mourning as a mother will mourn for her offspring, she returned to her room and lay herself down, poor thing, to rest.

===Chinese===

很久很久以前，哈维库（Háwikuh）住着一位极其美丽的少女。她年幼时，她那身为大祭司的父亲便把她奉献给了神圣之务，因此总把她关在家中，避开一切男人的目光；她就这样长大。
她美到这般地步：太阳顺着自己一道笔直的光柱往下望时，那光柱若恰巧从屋顶的缝隙、天窗或少女闺楼上部的窗子穿进去，他便望见了她，惊叹于她罕见的美，无法拿他环游诸大世界所见的一切来相比。就这样，少女渐渐长成，出落成一个年轻女子，太阳深深地爱上了她；日子越久，他越发着迷，竟降到地上，乘着自己的一道光柱进入她的闺房。于是有一天正午，她正坐着编好看的篮子，面前忽然立着一位辉煌的青年，衣饰辉煌夺目。那是太阳之父。他温柔而爱怜地望着她；她望着他，并不害怕：就这样，她爱了他，他爱了她，他赢得了她做妻子。此后许多日子里，他都来探她，正午时分陪她小住；可她多半独处，家人进屋时她照旧坐着编盘，所以谁也没起疑。
她知道自己已被献给神圣之务，也知道自己纵然解释怎样做了母亲，也没人肯信，因此心里翻腾得厉害。于是她打定主意：孩子一生下来，就把他送离自己身边。
时候到了，一天夜里，孩子降生了。她细心地用软棉花裹好这个小男婴，半夜里蹑手蹑脚翻过屋顶，悄悄下到地上，把孩子放在哈维库下方谷中、小溪近旁一堆垃圾的避风处。随后，她像所有母亲为自己的骨肉哀恸那样哀恸着回到房中，这可怜人，躺下歇息了。

===Original===

As daylight was breaking in the east, and the hills and the valleys were coming forth one after another from the shadows of night, a Deer with her two little brightly-speckled fawns descended from the hills to the south across the valley, with ears and eyes alert, and stopped at the stream to drink. While drinking they were startled by an infant’s cry, and, looking up, they saw dust and cotton-wool and other things flying about in the air, almost as if a little whirlwind were blowing on the site of the refuse-heap where the child had been laid. It was the child, who, waking and finding itself alone, hungry, and cold, was crying and throwing its little hands about.
“Bless my delight!” cried the Deer to her fawns. “I have this day found a waif, a child, and though it be human it shall be mine; for, see, my children, I love you so much that surely I could love another.”
Thereupon she approached the little infant, and breathed her warm breath upon it and caressed it until it became quiet, and then after wrapping about it the cotton-wool, she gently lifted it on her broad horns, and, turning, carried it steadily away toward the south, followed on either side by her children, who kept crying out “*Neh! neh!*” in their delight.
The home of this old Deer and her little ones, where all her children had been born for years, was south of Háwikuh, in the valley that turns off among the ledges of rocks near the little spring called Póshaan. There, in the shelter of a clump of piñon and cedar trees, was a soft and warm retreat, winter and summer, and this was the lair of the Deer and her young.
The Deer was no less delighted than surprised next morning to find that the infant had grown apace, for she had suckled it with her own milk, and that before the declining of the sun it was already creeping about. And greater was her surprise and delight, as day succeeded day, to find that the child grew even more swiftly than grow the children of the Deer. Behold! on the evening of the fourth day it was running about and playing with its foster brother and sister. Nor was it slow of foot, even as compared with those little Deer. Behold!

===Chinese===

东方破晓，山峦和谷地一一从夜影中显现出来时，一只母鹿（Deer）带着两只亮斑点点的小鹿从南面的山上下来，穿过谷地，耳目警觉，到溪边饮水。正饮着，一声婴儿的啼哭吓了它们一跳；抬头一看，只见尘土、棉絮和别的东西在空中乱飞，几乎像垃圾堆上——孩子就放在那里——刮起了一小股旋风。原来是孩子醒了，发觉自己孤零零的，又饿又冷，啼哭着，小手乱舞。
“我的欢喜呀！”母鹿对小鹿们喊。“我今天捾着一个弃儿，一个孩子！虽说是人，他归我了；瞧啊，孩子们，我这么疼你们，一定也疼得了另一个。”
于是她走近小婴儿，朝他呵出温热的气，抚爱他，直到他安静下来；然后用棉絮把他裹好，轻轻托上自己宽大的角，转过身，稳稳地朝南走去，两只小鹿一左一右跟着，欢喜得直叫“呢！呢！”
这只老母鹿和她孩子们的家——她历年生孩子的地方——在哈维库以南，谷地在一个叫波尚（Póshaan）的小泉附近的岩层间拐进去的地方。那里，一丛矮松和雪松的掩蔽下，有一处又软又暖的巢，冬夏皆宜，这便是母鹿和她孩子们的窝。
第二天早晨，母鹿又惊又喜地发现婴儿长得飞快——她用自个儿的奶喂过他——太阳还没偏西，他已经在四处爬了。一天天过去，她越发又惊又喜：这孩子长得比鹿的孩子还快。看哪！第四天傍晚，他已经在跟着养弟养妹跑着玩了。比起那些小鹿，他的脚一点儿也不慢。看哪！

===Original===

yet greater cause for wonder, on the eighth day it was a youth fair to look upon﻿—looking upon itself and seeing that it had no clothing, and wondering why it was not clothed, like its brother and sister, in soft warm hair with pretty spots upon it.
As time went on, this little foster-child of the Deer (it must always be remembered that it was the offspring of the Sun-father himself), in playing with his brother and sister, and in his runnings about, grew wondrously strong, and even swifter of foot than the Deer themselves, and learned the language of the Deer and all their ways.
When he had become perfected in all that a Deer should know, the Deer-mother led him forth into the wilds and made him acquainted with the great herd to which she belonged. They were exceedingly happy with this addition to their number; much they loved him, and so sagacious was the youth that he soon became the leader of the Deer of the Háwikuh country.
When these Deer and the Antelopes were out on the mesas ranging to and fro, there at their head ran the swift youth. The soles of his feet became as hard as the hoofs of the Deer, the skin of his person strong and dark, the hair of his head long and waving and as soft as the hair on the sides of the Deer themselves.
It chanced one morning, late that summer, that the uncle of the maiden who had cast away her child went out hunting, and he took his way southward past Póshaan, the lair of the Deer-mother and her foster-child. As he traversed the borders of the great mesas that lie beyond, he saw a vast herd of Deer gathered, as people gather in council. They were quiet and seemed to be listening intently to someone in their midst. The hunter stole along carefully on hands and knees, twisting himself among the bushes until he came nearer; and what was his wonder when he beheld, in the midst of the Deer, a splendid youth, broad of shoulder, tall and strong of limb, sitting nude and graceful on the ground, and the old Deer and the young seemed to be paying attention to what he was saying. The hunter rubbed his eyes and looked again; and again he looked, shading his eyes with his hands. Then he elevated himself to peer yet more closely, and the sharp eyes of the youth discovered him.

===Chinese===

更奇的是，第八天，他已是个眉清目秀的少年——他打量自己，见身上没有衣裳，纳闷自己怎么不像弟妹那样，裹着一身带漂亮斑点的软和暖毛。
日子久了，鹿的这个小小养子（千万记住，他是太阳之父自己的骨血），在同弟妹玩耍、四处奔跑中，长得奇壮无比，脚程甚至比鹿还快，还学会了鹿的语言和鹿的一切习性。
他把一只鹿该会的都学到家后，鹿母带他到野外，把他引见给自家的大鹿群。群里添了这一口，个个欢喜异常；他们非常爱他；这少年又那么有见识，不久就成了哈维库一带鹿群的头领。
这些鹿和羚羊们在台地上来回游荡时，跑在最前头的就是这飞毛腿少年。他的脚掌硬得同鹿蹄一样，周身皮肤又黑又结实，一头长发波浪似的披着，软得同鹿身两侧的毛一样。
那年夏末的一天早晨，弃婴少女的舅舅出门打猎，取道往南，经过波尚——鹿母和养子的窝。他穿过再往外的大台地边缘时，望见一大群鹿聚在一起，像人们聚议事。它们安安静静，仿佛在凝神听群中谁说话。猎人手脚并用，在灌木间蹑行扭挪，挨近了些；他看见的是何等景象啊——鹿群当中坐着一位堂堂少年，肩宽背阔，四肢高壮，赤裸着身子，端坐地上，姿态潇洒；老鹿小鹿仿佛都在听他讲话。猎人揉揉眼睛再看；又看，用手搭起凉棚。随后他直起身子想瞧得更真切，少年锐利的眼睛发现了他。

===Original===

With a shout he lifted himself to his feet and sped away like the wind, followed by the whole herd, their hoofs thundering, and soon they were all out of sight.
The hunter dropped his bow and stood there musing; then picking it up, he turned himself about and ran toward Háwikuh as fast as he could. When he arrived he related to the father of the girl what he had seen. The old priest summoned his hunters and warriors and bade the uncle repeat the story. Many there were who said: “You have seen an apparition, and of evil omen to your family, alas! alas!”
“No,” said he, “I looked, and again I looked, and yet again, and again, and I avow to you that what I saw was as plain and as mortal as the Deer themselves.”
Convinced at last, the council decided to form a grand hunt, and word was given from the housetops that on the fourth day from that day a hunt should be undertaken﻿—that the southern mesa should be surrounded, and that the people should gather in from all sides and encompass the herd there, in order that this wonderful youth should not escape being seen, or possibly captured.
Now, when the Deer had gone to a safe distance they slackened their pace and called to their leader not to fear. And the old foster-mother of the youth for the first time related to him, as she had related to them long ago, that he was the child of mortals, telling how she had found him.
The youth sat with his head bowed, thinking of these things. Then he raised his head proudly, and said: “What though I be the child of mortals, they have not loved me: they have cast me from their midst, therefore will I be faithful to thee alone.”
But the old Deer-mother said to him: “Hush, my child! Thou art but a mortal, and though thou might’st live on the roots of the trees and the bushes and plants that mature in autumn, yet surely in the winter time thou could’st not live, for my supply of milk will be withholden, and the fruits and the nuts will all be gone.”
And the older members of that large herd gathered round and repeated what she had been saying. And they said: “We are aware that we shall be hunted now, as is the invariable custom when our herd has been discovered, on the fourth day from the day on which we were first seen. Amongst the people who come there will be, no doubt, those who will seek you; and you must not endeavor to escape.

===Chinese===

他喊了一声，一跃而起，风一样飞驰而去，整群鹿跟着他，蹄声如雷，转眼都没了影。
猎人丢下弓，站在那里发怔；随后拾起弓，转身朝哈维库拼命跑去。到了家，他把所见讲给少女的父亲。老祭司召集猎人和武士，让舅舅复述了一遍。许多人说：“你见了鬼影，是你家的凶兆，唉！唉！”
“不，”他说，“我看了又看，再看，又看，我向你们起誓，我看见的跟鹿群一样真切、一样是血肉之躯。”
众人终于信了，议事会决定举行一次大围猎，屋顶上传下话来：自那日起第四天出猎——围住南面的台地，人们从四面聚拢，把鹿群围死在那里，好教这奇少年跑不掉，定要见着他，兴许还能捉住他。
且说鹿群跑到安全地带，放慢了脚步，唤他们的头领别怕。少年的老养母头一回对他讲起她多年前对他们讲过的事：他原是凡人的孩子，又讲了怎样捾到他的经过。
少年垂头坐着，想着这些事。随后他昂起头，骄傲地说：“就算我是凡人的孩子，可他们不曾爱我：他们把我丢弃了；所以我只忠于你。”
老鹿母却对他说：“喳，我的孩子！你到底是个凡人；你虽能靠树根、灌木和秋天成熟的草实活着，可一到冬天你准活不成——我的奶到时候要断了，果子和坚果也都没了。”
大鹿群里年长的围拢来，把她的话重说了一遍。他们说：“我们知道，如今要被围猎了——我们的鹿群一被发现，照例第四天就来围。来的人里头，无疑会有找你的；你可千万不能想法子逃。

===Original===

Even we ourselves are accustomed to give up our lives to the brave hunters among this people, for many of them are sacred of thought, sacred of heart, and make due sacrifices unto us, that our lives in other form may be spared unceasingly.”
A splendid Deer rose from the midst of the herd, and, coming forward, laid his cheek on the cheek of the boy, and said: “Yet we love you, but we must now part from you. And, in order that you may be like unto other mortals, only exceeding them, accompany me to the Land of the Souls of Men, where sit in council the Gods of the Sacred Dance and Drama, the Gods of the Spirit World.”
To all this the youth, being convinced, agreed. And on that same day the Deer who had spoken set forward, the swift youth running by his side, toward the Lake of the Dead. On and on they sped, and as night was falling they came to the borders of that lake, and the lights were shining over its middle and the Gardens of the Sacred Dance. And the old Drama-woman and the old Drama-man were walking on its shores, back and forth, calling across to each other.
As the Deer neared the shore of the lake, he turned and said to his companion: “Step in boldly with me. Ladders of rushes will rise to receive you, and down underneath the waters into the great Halls of the Dead and of the Sacred Dance we will be borne gently and swiftly.”
Then they stepped into the lake. Brighter and lighter it grew. Great ladders of rushes and flags lifted themselves from the water, and upon them the Deer and his companion were borne downward into halls of splendor, lighted by many lights and fires. And in the largest chamber the gods were sitting in council silently. Páutiwa, the Sun-priest of the Sacred Drama (*Kâkâ*), Shúlawitsi (the God of Fire), with his torch of ever-living flame, and many others were there; and when the strangers arrived they greeted and were greeted, and were given a place in the light of the central fire. And in through the doors of the west and the north and the east and the south filed long rows of sacred dancers, those who had passed through the Lake of the Dead, clad in cotton mantles, white as the daylight, finely embroidered, decked with many a treasure shell and turquoise stone. These performed their sacred rites, to the delight of the gods and the wonder of the Deer and his foster-brother.

===Chinese===

连我们自己，都惯于把性命交给这个族群里勇敢的猎人——他们中许多人思念神圣、心地神圣，向我们奉献应尽的祭，好叫我们的性命在别的形体里绵绵不绝。”
一头漂亮的鹿从鹿群中站起，走上前，把自己的脸颊贴上少年的脸颊，说：“可我们爱你，如今却不得不与你分别了。为了使你像别的凡人、又胜过他们，随我到人类魂灵之国去吧；圣舞圣戏的诸神、灵界诸神，正在那里议事。”
少年信服了，一一应允。就在当天，那只鹿动身了，飞毛腿少年在他身侧奔跑，直奔死者之湖（Lake of the Dead）。他们一程接一程飞奔，夜幕降临时，来到湖边；湖心之上灯火通明，那是圣舞的花园。圣戏老婆和圣戏老翁正在岸边踱来踱去，隔着水面彼此呼唤。
鹿摌近湖岸时，转身对同伴说：“跟我大胆踏进去。灯心草扎的梯子会升起来接你；我们将在水底下，被轻轻巧巧、飞快地送进死者与圣舞的大殿。”
于是他们踏进湖里。越走越亮。巨大的灯心草和菖蒲梯从水中升起，鹿和他的同伴立在梯上，被送下灯光火光交映的辉煌大殿。最大的一间殿里，诸神正默然议事。圣戏（Kâkâ）的太阳祭司帕乌蒂瓦（Páutiwa）、举着永生火焰之炬的火神舒拉维齐（Shúlawitsi），还有许多别的神都在；两位生客一到，便互致问候，被安置在中央火堆的光里。西门、北门、东门、南门，鱼贯而入一列列圣舞者——都是渡过死者之湖的人——身披白如天光的棉披风，绣工精美，缀满珍贝和绿松石。他们行着神圣的祭仪，诸神欢喜，鹿和他的养弟惊叹不已。

===Original===

And when the dancers had retired, Páutiwa, the Sun-priest of the Sacred Dance, arose, and said: “What would’st thou?”﻿—though he knew full well beforehand. “What would’st thou, oh, Deer of the forest mesas, with thy companion, thy foster-brother; for not thinking of nothing would one visit the home of the *Kâkâ*.”
Then the Deer lifted his head and told his story.
“It is well,” said the gods.
“Appear, my faithful one,” said Páutiwa to Shúlawitsi. And Shúlawitsi appeared and waved his flame around the youth, so that he became convinced of his mortal origin and of his dependence upon food prepared by fire. Then the gods who speak the speech of men gathered around and breathed upon the youth, and touched to his lips moisture from their own mouths, and touched the portals of his ears with oil from their own ears, and thus was the youth made acquainted with both the speech and the understanding of the speech of mortal man. Then the gods called out, and there were brought before them fine garments of white cotton embroidered in many colors, rare necklaces of sacred shell with many turquoises and coral-like stones and shells strung in their midst, and all that the most beautifully clad of our ancients could have glorified their appearance with. Such things they brought forth, and, making them into a bundle, laid them at the feet of the youth. Then they said: “Oh, youth, oh, brother and father, since thou art the child of the Sun, who is the father of us all, go forth with thy foster-brother to thy last meeting-place with him and with his people; and when on the day after the morrow hunters shall gather from around thy country, some of ye, oh, Deer,” said he, turning to the Deer, “yield thyselves up that ye may die as must thy kind ever continue to die, for the sake of this thy brother.”
“I will lead them,” simply replied the Deer. “Thanks.”
And Páutiwa continued: “Here full soon wilt thou be gathered in our midst, or with the winds and the mists of the air at nighttime wilt sport, ever-living. Go ye forth, then, carrying this bundle, and, as ye best know how, prepare this our father and child for his reception among men. And, O son and father,” continued the priest-god, turning to the youth, “Fear not! Happy wilt thou be in the days to come, and treasured among men. Hence thy birth. Return with the Deer and do as thou art told to do.

===Chinese===

舞者退下后，圣戏的太阳祭司帕乌蒂瓦起身说：“你想要什么？”——其实他早已了然于心。“林中台地之鹿啊，你带着同伴、你的养弟，想要什么？无事谁也不会来卡卡之家。”
鹿抬起头，讲了他的来意。
“好。”诸神说。
“显身吧，我忠实的仆人，”帕乌蒂瓦对舒拉维齐说。舒拉维齐显身，把火焰在少年周身挥了一圈，使少年确信自己凡人的出身，确信自己离不开火熟的食物。随后，会说人话的诸神围拢来，朝少年呵气，把他们口中的津润点到他唇上，把他们耳中的油膏涂到他耳门上；少年就这样通晓了凡人的言语，也听懂了凡人的话。诸神又一声传唤，人捧来五彩绣花的白棉华服、缀满绿松石和珊瑚般彩石珍贝的神贝项链，还有我们先民中最盛装的人所能用来妆点仪表的一切。他们把这些打成一个包袱，放在少年脚前，说：“少年啊，兄弟和父亲啊，你既是太阳——我们众人之父——的孩子，就随你的养兄到他和他族人最后相会的地方去吧；后天，猎人将从四面八方聚到你家乡来。你们中间，哦，鹿啊，”他转向鹿说，“要有一些献出身来，照你们一族永续不断的死法死去——为了你这位兄弟。”
“我领他们去，”鹿只答了一句。“谢谢。”
帕乌蒂瓦接着说：“用不了多久，你就会聚到我们中间，或化作夜里的风和雾，嬉游长空，长生不死。去吧，带上这个包袱，用你们最擅长的法子，把我们这位父亲和孩子装扮起来，好让他去人间入席。还有，儿啊，父啊，”祭司之神转向少年，接着说，“别怕！来日你会快活的，会被人间珍视。你的降生正为此。随鹿回去，他们叫你做什么，你就做什么。

===Original===

Thy uncle, leading his priest-youths, will be foremost in the hunt. He will pursue thee and thy foster-mother. Lead him far away; and when thou hast so led him, cease running and turn and wait, and peacefully go home whither he guides thee.”
The sounds of the Sacred Dance came in from the outer apartments, and the youth and the Deer, taking their bundle, departed. More quickly than they had come they sped away; and on the morning when the hunters of Háwikuh were setting forth, the Deer gathered themselves in a vast herd on the southern mesa, and they circled about the youth and instructed him how to unloose the bundle he had brought. Then closer and closer came the Deer to the youth and bade him stand in his nakedness, and they ran swiftly about him, breathing fierce, moist breaths until hot steam enveloped him and bathed him from head to foot, so that he was purified, and his skin was softened, and his hair hung down in a smooth yet waving mass at the back of his head. Then the youth put on the costume, one article after another, he having seen them worn by the Gods of the Sacred Dance, and by the dancers; and into his hair at the back, under the band which he placed round his temples, he thrust the glowing feathers of the macaw which had been given him. Then, seeing that there was still one article left﻿—a little string of conical shells﻿—he asked what that was for; and the Deer told him to tie it about his knee.
The Deer gathered around him once more, and the old chief said: “Who among ye are willing to die?” And, as if it were a festive occasion to which they were going, many a fine Deer bounded forth, striving for the place of those who were to die, until a large number were gathered, fearless and ready. Then the Deer began to move.
Soon there was an alarm. In the north and the west and the south and the east there was cause for alarm. And the Deer began to scatter, and then to assemble and scatter again. At last the hunters with drawn bows came running in, and soon their arrows were flying in the midst of those who were devoted, and Deer after Deer fell, pierced to the heart or other vital part.
At last but few were left﻿—amongst them the kind old Deer-mother and her two children; and, taking the lead, the glorious youth, although encumbered by his new dress, sped forth with them.

===Chinese===

你舅舅将带着他的祭司青年们冲在围猎最前头。他会追你、追你的养母。把他远远引开；引够了，就停下奔跑，转身等他，平平静静随他回家。”
圣舞的乐声从外面的殿室传来，少年和鹿带上包袱，告辞而去。他们比来时更快地飞奔而去；哈维库的猎人们出发的那个早晨，鹿群在南面台地上聚成浩浩荡荡一大群，围着少年转，教他怎样解开带来的包袱。随后鹿群朝少年越围越拢，叫他赤身立定；它们绕着他飞跑，呼出灼热湿润的气息，直到腾腾热气裹住他，把他从头到脚蒸洗一遍，于是他得了净化，皮肤变软了，头发在脑后披成一头平滑的波浪。随后少年一件一件穿起那身装束——他见过圣舞诸神和舞者们穿戴的样子；又在脑后发间、束额带下，插上赠他的金刚鹦鹉的火亮羽毛。这时见还剩下一件——一小串锥形贝壳——他问那是做什么的；鹿叫他把系在膝上。
鹿群再把他围起来，老首领说：“你们中间，谁愿意死？”仿佛去赴一场喜庆，一头头漂亮的鹿争相跃出，抢那赴死的名额，直到聚起一大群，无畏无惧，准备停当。鹿群开始行动。
不久警讯四起。北、西、南、东，处处告警。鹿群开始散开，又聚拢，又散开。末了，猎人们挽弓奔来，箭雨很快飞进那些献身者的队伍，一头又一头鹿倒下，箭箭穿心，或中别的要害。
最后剩下没几只了——其中有那善良的老鹿母和她的两个孩子；那位英气的少年虽然累赘着一身新衣，仍旧领着头，同她们一道飞奔出去。

===Original===

They ran and ran, the fleetest of the tribe of Háwikuh pursuing them; but all save the uncle and his brave sons were soon left far behind. The youth’s foster-brother was soon slain, and the youth, growing angry, turned about; then bethinking himself of the words of the gods, he sped away again. So his foster-sister, too, was killed; but he kept on, his old mother alone running behind him. At last the uncle and his sons overtook the old mother, and they merely caught her and turned her away, saying: “Faithful to the last she has been to this youth.” Then they renewed the chase for the youth; and he at last, pretending weariness, faced about and stood like a stag at bay. As soon as they approached, he dropped his arms and lowered his head. Then he said: “Oh, my uncle” (for the gods had told who would find him)﻿—“Oh, my uncle, what wouldst thou? Thou hast killed my brothers and sisters; what wouldst thou with me?”
The old man stopped and gazed at the youth in wonder and admiration of his fine appearance and beautiful apparel. Then he said: “Why dost thou call me uncle?”
“Because, verily,” replied the youth, “thou art my uncle, and thy niece, my maiden-mother, gave birth to me and cast me away upon a dust-heap; and then my noble Deer found me and nourished me and cherished me.”
The uncle and his sons gazed still with wonder. Then they thought they saw in the youth’s clear eyes and his soft, oval face a likeness to the mother, and they said: “Verily, this which he says is true.” Then they turned about and took him by the hands gently and led him toward Háwikuh, while one of them sped forward to test the truth of his utterances.
When the messenger arrived at Háwikuh he took his way straight to the house of the priest, and told him what he had heard. The priest in anger summoned the maiden.
“Oh, my child,” said he, “hast thou done this thing which we are told thou hast done?” And he related what he had been told.
“Nay, no such thing have I done,” said she.
“Yea, but thou hast, oh, unnatural mother! And who was the father?” demanded the old priest with great severity.
Then the maiden, thinking of her Sun-lover, bowed her head in her lap and rocked herself to and fro, and cried sorely. And then she said: “Yea, it is true; so true that I feared thy wrath, oh, my father! I feared thy shame, oh, my mother! and what could I do?”

===Chinese===

他们跑啊跑，哈维库族里脚程最快的人紧追不舍；可除了舅舅和他的几个勇儿，旁人很快被远远甩在后面。少年的养弟先被杀了；少年怒起，回身要斗；随即想起神明的话，又飞奔而去。于是养妹也被杀了；他还在跑，只有老母亲独自跟在他身后。末了，舅舅和他的儿子们追上老母；他们只是捉住她，把她放走，说：“她待这少年，忠心到了最后。”随后他们重新追那少年；少年最后装出力竭的样子，回身站定，像一头被困的牡鹿。他们一摌近，他便垂下双臂，低下头，说：“哎，舅舅”（神明早说过谁会找到他）——“哎，舅舅，你要怎样？你杀了我的弟弟妹妹；你要我怎样？”
老人站住，又惊又叹地打量这少年俊美的仪表和漂亮的装束，说：“你为什么叫我舅舅？”
“因为，千真万确，”少年答，“你就是我的舅舅；你的外甥女，我那少女母亲，生下我，把我丢在了一堆垃圾上；后来我高贵的鹿找到了我，哺养我，疼惜我。”
舅舅和他的儿子们仍旧惊奇地望着。随后他们仿佛从少年清亮的眼睛和柔和的鹅蛋脸上看出了他母亲的影子，便说：“当真，他说的是实话。”于是他们转身，轻轻牵起他的手，带他往哈维库去，另有一人飞奔先行，去验证他的话。
信使到了哈维库，径直进了祭司的家，把所闻告诉了他。祭司怒冲冲地把少女召来。
“我的孩子，”他说，“人家说的那事，你做了吗？”他把听来的话讲了一遍。
“不，我没做过这样的事，”她说。
“做了，你就是做了，哦，你这狠心的母亲！孩子的父亲是谁？”老祭司厉声逼问。
少女想起她的太阳情郎，把头埋进膝间，前后摇晃，哭得伤心。随后她说：“是，是真的；真得我怕你的怒火，我的父亲！真得我怕你蒙羞，我的母亲！我又能怎么办呢？”

===Original===

Then she told of her lover, the Sun﻿—with tears she told it, and she cried out: “Bring back my child that I may nurse him and love but him alone, and see him the father of children!”
By this time the hunters arrived, some bringing game, but others bringing in their midst this wondrous youth, on whom each man and maiden in Háwikuh gazed with delight and admiration.
They took him to the home of his priest-grandfather; and as though he knew the way he entered the apartment of his mother, and she, rising and opening wide her arms, threw herself on his breast and cried and cried. And he laid his hand on her head, and said: “Oh, mother, weep not, for I have come to thee, and I will cherish thee.”
So was the foster-child of the Deer restored to his mother and his people.
Wondrously wise in the ways of the Deer and their language was he﻿—so much so that, seeing them, he understood them. This youth made little ado of hunting, for he knew that he could pay those rites and attentions to the Deer that were most acceptable, and made them glad of death at the hand of the hunter. And ere long, so great was his knowledge and success, and his preciousness in the eyes of the Master of Life, that by his will and his arm alone the tribe of Háwikuh was fed and was clad in buckskins.
A rare and beautiful maiden he married, and most happy was he with her.
It was his custom to go forth early in the morning, when the Deer came down to drink or stretch themselves and walk abroad and crop the grass; and, taking his bow and quiver of arrows, he would go to a distant mesa, and, calling the Deer around him, and following them as swiftly as they ran, he would strike them down in great numbers, and, returning, say to his people: “Go and bring in my game, giving me only parts of what I have slain and taking the rest yourselves.”
So you can readily see how he and his people became the greatest people of Háwikuh. Nor is it marvellous that the sorcerers of that tribe should have grown envious of his prosperity, and sought to diminish it in many ways, wherein they failed.
At last one night the Master of Sorcerers in secret places raised his voice and cried: “*Weh‑h‑h‑h! Weh‑h‑h‑h‑h‑h!

===Chinese===

随后她讲起她的情郎太阳——她是流着泪讲的——哭喊道：“把我的孩子带回来吧，我要亲自哺养他，只爱他一个，看着他做孩子们的父亲！”
这时猎人们到了，有的带来猎物，另一些人当中簇拥着这位奇少年，哈维库的男女老少个个欢喜赞叹地望着他。
他们把他带到祭司外祖父的家中；他仿佛认得路似的径直进了母亲的闺房。母亲起身张开双臂，扑在他怀里，哭了又哭。他把手放在她头上，说：“哦，母亲，别哭了；我这不是回到你身边了么，我会疼你的。”
鹿的养子就这样回到了母亲和族人身边。
他深谙鹿的习性和鹿的语言——深到一见它们就懂它们。这少年打猎从不费什么劲，因为他知道该向鹿行什么礼、献什么敬，能叫它们乐意死在猎人手里。不久，凭他的知识和成就，凭他在生命之主眼里的珍贵，单凭他的意志和臂膀，哈维库一族便吃得饱、穿上了鹿皮。
他娶了一位罕见的美貌少女，同她过得非常幸福。
他的习惯是一清早出门——那是鹿群下来饮水、伸腰、散步、啃草的时辰——带上弓和箭囊，走到一处僻远的台地，把鹿唤到身边，跟着它们飞跑，成片地把它们放倒，回来说：“去把我打的猎物运回来吧；分我一点儿就行，其余的你们拿去。”
所以你很容易明白，他和他的族人怎么成了哈维库最强盛的人。那一族的巫师们眼红他的兴旺，想方设法要折损它，也不奇怪；只是都没得手。
终于，一天夜里，巫师之主在秘处扬声喊：“韦——赫——赫——赫！韦——赫——赫——赫——赫——赫！

===Original===

*” And round about him presently gathered all the sorcerers of the place, and they entered into a deep cavern, large and lighted by green, glowing fires, and there, staring at each other, they devised means to destroy this splendid youth, the child of the Sun.
One of their number stood forth and said: “I will destroy him in his own vocation. He is a hunter, and the Coyote loves well to follow the hunter.” His words were received with acclamation, and the youth who had offered himself sped forth in the night to prepare, by incantation and with his infernal appliances, a disguise for himself.
On the next morning, when the youth went forth to hunt, an old Coyote sneaked behind him after he reached the mesas, and, following stealthily, waited his throwing down of the Deer; and when the youth had called and killed a number of Deer and sat down to rest on a fallen tree, the Coyote sneaked into sight. The youth, looking at him, merely thought: “He seeks the blood of my slain Deer,” and he went on with his prayers and sacrifices to the dead of the Deer. But soon, stiffening his limbs, the Coyote swiftly scudded across the open, and, with a puff from his mouth and nostrils like a sneeze toward the youth, threw himself against him and arose a man﻿—the same man who had offered his services in the council of the wizards﻿—while the poor youth, falling over, ran away, a human being still in heart and mind, but in form a coyote.
Off to the southward he wandered, his tail dragging in the dust; and growing hungry he had naught to eat; and cold on the sides of the mesas he passed the night, and on the following morning wandered still, until at last, very hungry, he was fain even to nip the blades of grass and eat the berries of the juniper. Thus he became ill and worn; and one night as he was seeking a warm place to lay him down and die, he saw a little red light glowing from the top of a hillock. Toward this light he took his way, and when he came near he saw that it was shining up through the sky-hole of someone’s house. He peered over the edge and saw an old Badger with his grizzly wife, sitting before a fire, not in the form of a badger but in the form of a little man, his badger-skin hanging beside him.
Then the youth said to himself: “I will cast myself down into their house, thus showing them my miserable condition.”

===Chinese===

”不多时，当地的巫师全都聚到他身边；他们钻进一个深洞——洞很宽大，燃着绿幽幽的火——在那里面面相觑，定计要害死这堂堂少年、太阳之子。
他们中有一个站出来说：“我要在他的本行里毁掉他。他是猎人，而郊狼最爱跟猎人。”他的话博得一片喝彩；自告奋勇的那个当夜就出动，念着咒，用他那些邪门家伙，给自己变了一副伪装。
第二天早晨，少年出门打猎，上了台地以后，一只老郊狼偷偷跟在他身后，蹑着脚，专等他放倒鹿；少年唤来鹿群、杀了几只、坐到一棵倒树上歇息时，郊狼溜进了他的眼角。少年看它一眼，只当：“它是来捾我猎物的血的。”便接着向死鹿行他的祷告和祭祀。可不多时，郊狼绷直四腿，倏地窜过开阔地，朝少年口鼻一喷，像打了个喷嚏，随即往他身上一撞，站起来变成一个男人——正是在巫师议事上自荐的那个人——可怜的少年被撞翻在地，爬起来跑了：心还是人心，脑筋还是人脑，身子却成了一只郊狼。
他一路漂泊到南边，尾巴在尘土里拖着；饿了没得吃；夜里在台地坡上挨冻；第二天接着流浪，到后来饿极了，只得啃草叶、吃刺柏的浆果。就这样他病了、垮了；一天夜里，他正找一块暖和地方躺下等死，望见一个小丘顶上透出一点红光。他朝那光走去，走近了才看清，那光是从谁家的天窗里照上来的。他扒着天窗沿往里一瞧：一只老獾和他灰毛的婆娘坐在火前——不是獾形，是个小个子人的模样，獾皮挂在旁边。
